[ALL MITTS RESERVED.] THE MAN FROM NEW YORK.

BY G. B.

BURGIN,

Author of "The Shutters of Silence," "A Creature of Impulse," "The Turk's Treachery," "'Broken Oa," "The Pink Dress," &e."

The fat pony, crawling leisurely alon., towards Hadley Common, paid not the slightest attention to its driver, a bright-eyed old lady of seventy, who, half-asleep in the sweet summer sunshine, suddenly discovered that the animal had come to a standstill and was eagerly cropping the grass by the roadside. "Get alcAig with you! "said old Lady Polperrow, with indignant emphasis, as she realised the situation. "Ah !if I were only a man I could do justice to the occasion. ' But I daren't hit you because of Jenkins; besides, he never allows me to have a whip. I wonder why all my servants bully me so? They make it so dis- agreeable for people coining to the house tha't poor Edith has a very dull time of it. She's a good girl, and I'd like to see her happily married. Go on, pony. Please! In the old days lady -could swear without losing caste. Modern manners are not without their drawbacks."

The fat pony, findinc, the grass by the roadside an agreeable change finding his daily food, and knowing that there was no whip in the little open carriage, went on feeding. When old Lady Polperrow somewhat feebly repeated her.

request he turned his head .for a m.oment as much as to say, "Aly dear good woman, don't you see that I am master of the situation ? " "If I were only a man I'd astonish you!" The old lady tugged at the reins; but the pon:. behaved with philosophic contempt, well knowinE that her wish was without the range of practical politics. "Was there ever such luck! My chance haF come at last after a fortnight's waiting," softly murmured a young man, who, knapsack beside him, reclined on the bank almost over Lady Pelperrow's head and watched the struggle between pony and mistress with ill-concealed amusement. "Stay a•moment, madam. I'll out you a switch." He cut the switch and presented it to the old lady with a graceful bow. The pony eyed it somewhat apprehensively and seemed annoyed. "Sir," said the old lady, with a twinkle in her eye as she took the switch, "are you aware that we have not been introduced? -I am surprised at your readiness to waive the customary formalities even in such a crisis as the present." "Oh, that's all right, madam. There's no time for conventionalities when you have to deal with this pampered boast." The young man jerked up the pony's head with fingers of iron. "Besides, you can cut me after I have put you in the broad way that leadeth to-" "Destruction ? " she asked, with an amused glance from her bright old eyes. "No, madam. To Hadley Common." He drew the unwilling pony into the road, straight- ened out the reins,and looked at her quizzically. "Here are your reins." "Well, sir," queried the old lady, "what can I do with them? The situation seems to mo to remain unaltered."

"I fear, madam," said the young man, "that you can't manage this bloated beast. In my country I'd not Make any bones about the matter but get you home somehow, even though I had to call up a patrol waggon." The old lady took.in the stranger's handsome, lean, saturnine face. He was equipped in the regulation tourist style, but with an unobtrusive neatness of fit which, tOgethcbr with his gallant bearing, excited her admiration. "If lam not very much mistaken you are a scion of the American _eagle ? "_

"From Nov York, madam. Howard P. Martin, by name. Just a brief fly round to study your country and your institutions." "Of course you approve of them ? " "Of the country—yes; of the institutions—no." The Old lady's eyes flashed. "Do you dare to—"

"Contradict old age? Never, madam. Have I your permission to drive you home. You see, this stuffed door-mat won't move." The old lady smiled. "In this country, it ill mot considered polite to allude to age." The young man bowed. "In my country, madam, we reverence old age, partly because so few of us live to grow old; it is a compliment to allude to it. Guess I'd better drive you home. I've a feeling that I can astonish this pony even though I should have to light a fire under him to Let him started." "Very well," said the old lady, Fuddenly. "If you can astonish so lethargic an animal it is more than I can do. I dare not use this switch, although it seems to be a gcod one. Consider that I leave myself in your hands." The young man took the switch, deposited his knapsack under the carriage seat, stepped in, and thirty seconds later the fat pony was trotting desperately; for the Man from New York made a peculiar noise which excited him to madness as the switch tickled his fat sides.

The pony's lost ambitions revived, and he actually broke into a gallop. "How do you account for the change in him ? _ _ . _ _

asked the old lady, watching the pony's efforts to establish a record. "It's the first house on the left behind the poplars." "Contact with a new country," said the young man, vigorously applying the switch; and the Folly, disliking the contact, bolted. But the iron grip of the stranger soon reduced him to order. Ten minutes later it was a very astonished pony which dashed up the gravel drive, and, with trembling legs and heavinq flanks, came to a standstill before Lady's Pot- perrow's door. As he did so, a fair-haired, handsome girl rushed out. "Auntie' auntie! What's the matter?" She stopped on seeing the stranger, sac), switch in hand, raised his hat. "At last!" he murmured. "At last—at last ! I've met her at last." "Nothing, my dear. This gentleman was kind enough to apply American methods of persuasion to the pony to make him hurry home ; that's all. The pony has had a much-needed lesson." The Man from New York assisted old Lady Polperrow out of the carriage with a kindly deference. She liked his skill and gentleness, but cast a dismayed look at the sweating pony. "Jenkins will never forgive me," !dm said. "Ah ! here he comes." "Jenkins! Who is Jenkins, madam ?" "My groom." The old lady trembled. "Can't you—can't you say the peny ran away? Any excuse is better than none." "Excuse!" The Man from New York's eyes flashed as a servant came running to the pony's head. "Who's been a ill-using one of my 'osses ?" the groom growled. "Your ladyship, I--" "Stop!" thundered the Man from New York. "Don't you dare to speak to your mistress like that. I don't like your style. Guess we'll go round to the stables and talk it over." He seized the astonished groom's collar, gave him a shake which. turned him apoplectically purple, and led both groom and pony round the corner. From the stal2le-yard there was the sound of a brief scuffle, then a suggestion of someone being forcibly held beneath a pump, and the swishing fall of a miniature Niagara.

Presently- the young Iman sauntered back, adjusting his cuffs. The knuckles of his left band were slightly swollen. The beautiful young girl came up to him trembling with excitement. "Thank you. Thank you so much! I've often wanted to do it myself, but auntie said it wasn't ladylike. I—l ought to have pumped for you! Where is.he now ? " "Looking round for a bit of raw beefsteak to put on his eye," said the Man from New York: "Is there any other little matter I can regu-- .L:xcuse me." He broke off for a moment. "Here's zny, card—Howard P. Martin."

-"I am more pleased to meet you than the pony was," said the old lady. "I am Lady Polpercow, and this is my niece, Edith Gra(m

"Charmed tow knoW you, MiiS draeme. Quite sure you haven't any mer 3 fat servants who'd be the better for a little exercise ?"

The girl laughed merrily, then glanced at her aunt. "After Mr. Martin's exertions, the least we can do is to give him some lunch, auntie. seem to have seen Mr. Martin haunting the neighbourhood for the last few days." "Do you think it's safe to give him lunch ?" asked the old lady, with mock earnestness. "He might eat us ; he has such a masterful way with him. How do you know we shouldn't be excnanging one torm ot svrvitude for another The young man's oyes turned from the old lady tu the ,votaiz cne,and lingt:red thwe as he dronned

CM one knle to kiss Lady Polperrow's hand. "If you would have the goodness to give me some lunch," he pleaded, "I'll thrash every servant in .he place for you. I've walked twenty miles since breakfast and could eat a wolf. People can do what they like with me when I'm not hungry." "Surely you must have some English blood in you ? " said the old lady. "Heaps ! " admitted Mr. Martin. "That's why I'm here. There's a little church behind the woods, and in that church reposes one of in ancestors. I've come all this way to take his picture, and wouldn't dare to go back without it." As the luncheon-hell rang, he offered Lady Polperrow his arm. "That settles it," said she. "Edith, Mr. Martin will be our guest." "That's very nice of you, auntie." The young girl walked on the other side of the old lady, from time to time casting amused yet approving glances at the young fellow whom she had seen several times during the last few days. More than once, she had asked what he was doing in the neighbourhood. Now, with some subtle feminine instinct, she tried to hide from herself her pleasure at meeting him. The old lady scented a romance anTwa.s secretly amused. "You think we ought to take him in ? " she asked, casually. "Of course, auntie. We could never face old Sir Howard do Martyne's, effigy again, if we allowed a descendant or his to depart hungry. Besides you wouldn't have had any lunch at all if dr. • Martin hadn't brought you home." ."That's so," said-the unabashed young man from New York. "This is the first adventure, I've had in this country, and l'd like it to turn out well. I—l'm eternally obliged to that pony. If I give that fellow five dollars to help mend his eye, we can call it square." The girl met his glance. He had eyes blacker than sloes, and his well-shaped, clean-shaven mouth, in spite of its determination, drooped humorously at the corners. She decided that she liked him. He was so very much alive. A stout, somewhat frightened-looking butler came forward to meet them. "Gilchrist," said the old lady, with a firmness which surprised herself, accustomed as she was to being tyrannised over by her pampered servants—" Gil- christ, take this gentleman's knapsack and chew him to his room.

Gilchrist hesitated, for he had just come from applying raw beefsteak to tha groom's eye. There was something in the expression of Mr. Martin's eye, however, which helpfd• him to decide quickly. "Guess you'd be tter carry my grip," said the Man from New York; and Gilchrist's actions betokened that he guessed correctly. When Gilchrist had put the "grip" down, the Man from New York gave him a couple of sovereigns. "There's one for you and one for the child of adversity with the raw beefsteak in his eye," he said, as the looked round the old- fashioned room. "If you behave yourself, double it before .I go." . _ _

"Would you like—some hot water, air?" asked the astonished old butler.

"No; cold's good enough for me." The young man hastily threw off his coat. "Apologise for me to the ladies, and say I'll be with them in fire minutes."

After Gilchrist had gone, the young man removed the signs of travel from his clothes. "She's tho beauty of the wcrld," he said, sauntering downstairs. "The beauty of the world! Lays over anything I ever met—anywhere. I had thought of Sadie Hawkins, but I'll not go on thinking. Edith,' the old lady called her. Wish I had the privileges of old age."

It was a protracted lunch. Usually, old Lady Polperrow had no appetite; but on this occasion the Man from New York seasoned the repast with anecdote, and she ate heartily. He assured Edith that she was mistaken when she imagined that the American girl usually opened the conversation with an epigram. The only fault to be found with .the American girl was her readiness to become an English duchess at a moment's notice. This put a strain upon her, prevented her from being natural; for all the time she was trying to live up to a station which might one day be hers, and it told upon her nerves.

"That's all very well," said Lady Polperrow; "bet why doesn't the American girl stay at home and marry the American man ? Why does she annex English dukes when there aren't enough for home consumption?" "I reckon it's this way," returned Mr. Howard P. Martin reflectively. "The American girl is full of the missionary spirit, it's her mission to go forth and marry mankind."

"But whi does she always -begin at the top ?" asked Edith.

"It's easier to spread downwards," returned the unabashed Mr. Martin. "There's always room at the top." "And you? Without seeming inquisitive, may I ask what you are doing over here?" inquired Lady Polperrow. "Just travelling," said Mr. Howard P. Martin, slowly. "Just travelling. It was Pop's idea." "'Pop's ' 1 What is a 'Pop '?" _ _ _

. "Oh, -it's a man with a family," explained Mr. Martin. "Popes one of those ft !lows who has made money. His great idea is to have his family tree put right, just to let those Knickerbockers in New York see how modern they are." Lady Polperrow was confused. "Knicker- bockers! What have knickerbockers to do with it ? "

Edith came to the rescue. "They're not things to wear, but old American families," she said, hastily.

"I aid not know that they had any old—really aid—families in America," rejoined Lady Pol- permw..

"Pop's idea," explained Mr. Howard Martin, "is to get the family pedigree straightened out, shew his descent in the direct line, and then have it painted on the front door. I've tried to stop him, but he's obstinate. When Pop's obstinate, why he—he stays firm; and that's all about it." "Painted on the front door ? " laughed Edith. "Yes, for a month—just to lower the pride of the Knickerbockers. Then, having asserted himself as the lineal descendant of an English nobleman, he's going to paint his American arms on the door and wipe out the pedigree." "His American arms ? What are they- ? " asked the astonished Edith.

"Same as his trade mark—a pig rampant on a green field. Pop's in pork. It isn't romantic, but it's money. "And—and what are you going to be in-4n pork?" queried the old lady. "No such luck," declared young Mr. Martin. "Pop's done all that kind of thing for me. I'd like to make money for myself, but Pop won't hear of it; and when I go down into the pit—" "Into the pit ?" "Yes, the wheat pit, or pork, or anything, Pop's EO friendly with all the fellows that they want to give me pointers." "Pointers!" echoed the old lady. "Can't you buy your own dogs?" Edith came to the rescue again. "Informa- tion—tips, auntie," she said, her eyes dancing with delight. "Mr. Martin means that he gets an unfair advantage over other people, and that he doesn't think it sportsmanlike." "That's it," heartily agreed Mr. Howard Martin. "How quickly you grasp the point."

"Come and sit- under - the chestnut--tree and nave your coffee there," said the old lady. "I want to hear some more about America. Edith, you" d better go down to the almshouse. Come, Hr. Aladin."

"It's a large subject, Lady Polperrow." But se got up and gave her his arm. All the vivacity which had hitherto marked f cung Mr. Howard Martin's manner quickly ranished. He asked permission to write a .tablegram, and sent it down to High Barnet for immediate transmission to America. Then he oegan to talk to Lady Polperrow about America, out in such level, monotonous tones, that, after fainly struggling to keep her eyes open, she suddenly went to sleep, and left the young man Jontinuing to drone. Directly Lady Polperrow was fast asleep, Mr. Howard Martin softly got up and made his way o the house as Edith came out, .a basket of roses in her hand. She was becomingly dressed in white, with deft suggestions of pink here and there. Mr. Martin seemed to find the cornflower- blue of her eyes very attractive. "I don't know the etiquette in this old country," lie said, irresolutely for him, "but Lady Polperrow's tsleep, and I don't like to call upon my ancestor in the church without someone to introduce me. seems too solemn a thing to face him without someone to .speak for me."

"Oh, I will introduce you to Sir Howard with pleasure." Edith came briskly down the path. "Auntie always sleeps for a couple of hours after lunch."

"There are many great institutions in England; and that's one of them," somewhat irrelevantly remarked Mr. Howard Martin, sauntering along at her side. "I suppose this old Sir Howard do Martxne was a bit abrupt in his da;; -

"Abrupt ? " "Yes—abrupt. Crusader, wasn't he ? " "Yes, but I don't follow you." "You will presently," remarked the Man from New York. "When he wanted a thing he generally took it, didn't he ? " "I—l suppose so."

"There l;-ere great institutions in those days also," still more irrelevantly remarked young Mr. Martin. For so resolute a man he was becoming almost dreamy. Edith led the way into the old church. There was a stately simplicity about it which went straight to the young man's heart. Hitherto he had never. concealed his pride at being an American. But, after all, what was an American? Someone whose ancestors came out from England, cast off the hidebound traditions of the past, ~and blossomed out into that perfect product. ,Of a somewhat hasty civilisation, the American citizen. "Now I know why," he said, hat in hand, as they came to the effigy of old Sir Howard—s" now I know why you English are bound to lick the world." "I'm afraid I don't understand you." . . . . . _ _

"Look at him!" said the Man from New York, pointing with reverent admiration to old Sir Howard. "It's his power of staying put`'.i.hat I like."

"Staying put ? " "Yes—staying put. Look at him! He's,been there eight or nine centuries, and hasn't moved. No one dare move him. Eight or nine years is our limit anywhere in America. There's a stability about him—a lack of restlessness. We're too restless. That's what's the matter 'with us. Now you always know where to find an English- man. A trifle of a hundred years or so doesn't make any difference with him; but you can never find an American. He doesn't stay put!" Edith gave the Man from New York a handful of glowing red roses from the little wicker basket which she carried. "Perhaps their fragrance will reach him even now," she suggested.. The young man reverently strewed the fragrant flowers over Sir Howard's stony heart. "Guess I'm going to modify myself a bit," he remarked, stepping backward to study the effect, as .the light came through an open window and fell across the feet of the old Crusador.

"Modify yourself? How?" "Come into the sunlight, Miss Graenie, and I'll tell you." They returned to the churchyard and stood beneath a spreading old hawthorn. The fragrance of newly-cut grass came to them from the neigh- bouring fields. Away to the left waved the stately oaks of Hadley. To the right were quaint little almshouses, each doorway filled with some poor old waif, peacefully loitering down- ward to the grave. The youvg man stood beside Edith. "It's all wrong," he said, gazing into his compel:lien's face, with bright, restless eyes. "What is all wrong?" "This system of American girls marrying Englishmen." "Why ? " "Seems so onesided. I'm not denying it's a good thing in a way; but it's selfiz;h. "What is selfish ?"

"Our girls coming over here and marrying dukqs. Why can't England reciprocate ? " "For the all-sufficient reason that you haven't any dukes in America." "Not your kind; but, Miss Graeme--" "Well, Mr. Martin ?"

"Don't you think that when two young ipeople recognise a wrong, it's their duty to help to set it straight ? " "Certainly. "

"Well, then" he said, with an air of 'relief, "that's what I've been trying to get at fo:zr the last few minutes. We haven't any duhes in America, but we have men."

" Ye—es ? So I have been given to understand." "The going down into the pit may have robbed us of what little polish we originally had; but we've the sense to see that there is something in this old civilisation dl yours which ought to be grafted on to American manhood." "Well ? "

"Well, our American girls are grafting' the Almighty Dollar on to English manhood. Don't you think it's the—the reciprocal duty of English girls to graft their own sweetness and beauty on to—' He hesitated. "To ? " "The American manhood ? " "How can I possibly answer so abstract a question ? " "Oh, it isn't really abstract." He got up. "I've cabled to Pop." _ _

"You have cabled —to about ? "

Pop ' ? What

He stood looking ':squarely down upon her. "You."

"Really, Mr. Martin, this assurance-" "Don't get mad," he bescught her, softly. "In America we do things in a hurry. We're born in a hurry, die in a hurry, make love in a hurry." • His audacity left her breathless. making love to me ? " "No, I'm just telling you the plain, square truth. Whether you take .me or not, I'll love you all my life. Pop will be here inside eight days."

"Aro you

What for?" "To vouch for me." "But what has this to do with me ? "

"Now you're honest. With you for my wife, I'd stand a mighty good chance of being President one day." "Oh, if ambition prompts you to make this outraeous pro--" "It ° does. Then I'd lay my power at your pretty feet, and give it all up if you said tho word."

"W—why ?" "Just to prove my love. I've been hanging round here for a fortnight to get introduced to you. 1 saw you in tile church the first day I came here, and I—l couldn't go away." "Come!" she said, abruptly. He followed her into the church, as she walked swiftly towards Sir Howard's tomb. The young man instinctively caught her mean- ing. When they reached the flower-strewn effigy, he placed one hand on the old knight's breast. "Sir Howard," he implored, "can't you wake up a moment? Just make sonic sign to tell her how much 1 love her„"

A soft gust of wind blew in through the open window and carried a vivid crimson rose from the old Crusader's heart to Edith Graeme's feet. "He—he's answered us," stammered the Man from New York, for once losing his self- possession. "Won't you—won't you pick it up?" She stooped slowly, picked up the rose, and went away. The young man knelt by the side of the cross- legged effigy. "You dear old chap," he said, brokenly—"you dear old chap! If you could only blow Pop over a little quicker, I'd be still more grateful to you."

SECONDARY SCHOOL

ANNUAL PRIZE DISTRIBUTION.

Does England Lag?

In the presence of a large and representative gathering the annual ptesentation of prizes to the pupils of the Warrington Municipal Secondary School took place at the Borough Gymnasium on Wednesday afternoon. The school has had a most successful year and the results obtained reflect the greatest credit upon the Principal and staff. The Mayor (Alderman W. J. Forshaw) pre- sented the prizes end the gathering was presided over by the Rev. Canon Alorley Stevenson. Amongst those present were :—The Maycress, Councillor Fulton Smith (chairman of , the Educaticn Committee). Councillor H. Roberts (vice-chairrnen). and Mrs. Roberts, Aldermen Smethurst end Benuctt, Rev. Canon Willis, Councillor Birtles, Mr. and Mrs. Robert Pierpoint, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, E. Plinston, E. S. A. Robson (prin- cipal), and 3Ir.J. Moore 111urray (director of education). Expressions of regret for inability to attend were received from Vice-Chancellor Dale, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P. Mr. W. P.Rylar,ds, Rev. Cancn Penrhyn,' Rev. B. Percival, Mr. J.Charlton Parr, Rev. N. Wilson Liebtenant Colonel Buckton, Mr. Holman Kingdon,Mr T. P. Bradshaw, Mr. Vera B. Davies, and Rev. G. C. Briggs,

The -CHAIRMAN referred to the great imirovement which had Leen effected by the railing in of the play- ground which had also been Put in good condition. During the year a room had Leen provided for MANUAL INSTRUCTION

_ end that would be found of considerable service. It was a great gain to a boy to learn to do something with his haw's. "I speak feelingly" continued the Canon "because when I was a boy these things were rot taught r.nd I wish from the bottom of my heart that I was more handy with my hands than I am. I cannot do anything with my hands; and if I had not got a little bit of a head I should have been in the Workhouse long ago." (Laughter.) Canon Stevenson commented upon the advantage which the school now enjoyed of using the Gymnasium within certain jicurs for physical instruc- tion, end also as a place of assembly for the whole school. During the year a Government regulation had come into force by which the size of the classes was reduced, and that would he a beneficial change as teachers were able to give more individual attention to smaller classes. The examinaticn results obtained pointed not to exceptional case bestowed upon a few of the most gifted scholars,but to the all-round care which had been bestowed by the Principal and his staff upon every scholar That was a much truer educational ideal than the mere singling out a few of the most fortunate and con- centrat;ng attention upon them so that brilliant results might be produced. Ihe school had, during the year, progressed quietly, efficiently and thoroughly. Canon Stevenson concluded by referring to the loss which the school had sustained by tLe resignation of Miss Frost who had rendered yeoman service, especially to the girls, in the excellent teaching she had given and in the way she had watched over their health, their conduct and their games. 'I hey wished her every happiness in her married life. (Applause). PRINCIPAL'S REPORT.

The PRISCIPAL. in submitting his report, said the numbers on .the roll at present were as follows : Secondary School pupils, boys 97, girls 110, total 207 : Teachers, boys 13, girls 12, total 25 ; mak:ng a total of 232 pupils altogether. There were 163 pupils under 14 3-, ars of age end 129 above that age showing an increase of older pupils compared with former years. He bad to report two changes in the teaching staff. Miss Mitchell form and needlework mie-ess, resigned owing to marrisge at the end of the first term and she was succeeded by Miss E. J. Forsyth B.A. (Liverpool). Last Midsummer the senior mistress Miss Frost, who bad completed six years of valuable service in the sOiool, also resigned on her marriage. He could not let this cccasion pass without referring to the energy and enthusiasm displayed by Miss Frost in her work, and although she had left Warrington he was sure the effect of her influence and character would still be felt by the pupils who had been under her care. The Committee had appointed as her successor Miss F. E. Clark B.A. (Aberystwyth) who had had a wide experience in Secondary School work and one who would gain the respect of parents and pupils alike. The teaching staff now numbered 16 and includes specialists in languages, mathematics, physics, chemistry, cookery, manuel training, drawing and gym- nastics. Not only had the Education Committee provided a staff capable of giving the highest type of instruction in the school, but ths facilities offered to parents for the education of their children bad been made on a generous sada. At the present time, including the pupil teachers, there were over 100 pupils in the school who wee provided with free tuition and judging by the splendid progress displayed by the pupils the induce- ments thus offered were appreciated by the parents. The examination results for the past year were satisfactory from the point of view of numbers. In the Preliminary Certificate Examination Part 11. 20 pupil teachers entered, all of whom were successful, and the following were awarded distinctions :—Dorothy Johnson, English, history, geography, mathematics, French and science (only four girls out of 10,400 candi- dates secured this number of distinctions); Annie Upson, English, French ; Elsie Swahae, English, geography; oomas Stirrup,geography ; Wilfred Barton, geography. Three pupils were entered for the Matriculation Examination, and all of them passed :—Ethel Cooper, Ralph Clatworthy, Northern Conjoint; Robert R. Bunting, London. For the Senior Oxford Local Exam'eations 24 entered and 22 passed. Four pupils. Ruby Witts, Jessie Upson William H. Rigby and George Whitfield secured bonours 3rd division, while Jessie Upsen, Rigby and Whitfield were granted exemp- tion from the University Matriculation Examination. Kenneth Witts passed the examination for artificers in the Royal Navy, and Nellie Shufflebotham received an appointment in the Civil Service Postal Department. —lt was gratifying ta learn that old pupils were showing the mettle of their pasture" in the Universi- ties and Training Colleges, and amongst other successes he noticed that four pupils who entered on their University course last session bad been successful in their Intermediate Degree Examination. These were: —Fred Jolley, Inter B.Sc., Manchester University; Edith Isaac, Inter 8.A., Manchester University ; Marion Jolley, Inter, B.Sc., Liverpool ; Percy Board- man, Inter B.Sc., Liverpool.—At the present time their nupils were scattered over England in the various train- ing colleges, and at the annual Christmas re-union they welcomed them back from Birmington, London, Chester, ,Edinburgh and Einndee, not forgetting a la!ge number from the splendid local training institute. (Applause). The reports of the Certificate Examination for teachers in training colleges also showed that the old pupils were taking a high position in the lists. The altera- tions in the school buildings were now nearing comple- tion, and they would shortly have the use of the Gymnasium for assembly purposes, the woodwork- shop for manual training, and the extensive boys' and girls' playground. Turning to the social side of the school lite it was to be regretted that in a day Secondary School of this type the corporate life was not so vigoious as in a residential school, but the pupils managed to get along satisfactorily with the hockey, football, cricket and tennis clubs and school choir. In conclusion Mr. Dobson expressed his sense of obliga- tion to the loyal and willing staff of teachers who had enabled him to present such a gratifying report, to the Committee and Director of Education for their generous provision of apparatus and for their readiness in adopting suggestions for the better wOrking of the school, and lastly he thought that the success of a Secondary School of that character was so dependent on the previous training of the pupils that ho freely admitted the claims of the head teachers of the elementary schoojs to a share in its reputation. (Applause.)

The MAYOR addiess:ng the pupils, said they must not forget that the true object of education was the formation and development of character, and that real education began when they had left school. They got a good foundation upon which to build up and improve their education and character at that school. True education, like our leicg. was of a three fold nature, physical, intellectual and spiritual. It was intended to fit them not only to take their part in the business life of the world but to have a high ccnception of their duties and responsibilities as cithecs. During the six years the Secondary school bad been opened 400 pupils

He conidered a good educa- tion was far better than to have a fortune left to them because by educition they could raise themselves, with perseverance and industry, to the highest possible rositions. Whatever they did when they weut out into the world to fight life's battle, he would urge tbara not to make any false step, which • would be fatal to their character, but to be industr3us and endeavc ur to become a credit to their teachers, to their school and to th,3ir town. (Applause). TRAFALGAR ESSAY COMPETITION.

Alderman Bennett then presented book prizes gained in the patriotic essay competition instituted by the alderman in commemoration of Trafaltar Day. The subject of the essays WA S "My duty to England" and the winners were Ruby Witts and John Sephton who were also successful last sear. Two additional prizes given by the Principal were awarded to Eleanor Shaw and Allan Evan. In the course of an address, Alderman BENNETI" urged the pupits to fit themselves to do their duty to their town and country. He said England had won a great reputation as a nation of shop-keepers and we wanted to keep it by the best and 'healthiest means. He believed, however, that we ot the greatest curses of the country was that we worshipped gold and com- mercial success more than cne ought to do. Still, business success and capacity was exceptionally important and if we did not bestir ourselves other nations who gave more thorough attention to these matters and educated their people more efficiently, would eventually pass us in commercial prosperity. He had been attending the , FLYING MEETING AT -BLACKPOOL

and the question which impressed itself upon him was: Who excelled in this new art which would probably revolutionise modern conditions? He would like to have felt that England led the way but England was hopelessly behind and be watched with mingled pain and amusement an English biplane which vainly tried all afternoon to mount up in the air, while a machine which was the product of French ingenuity soared up like a bird and and was able to sail all r3und the course with great success. The fact that an Englishman had not discovered the North Pole was also a great grief to him and the only consolation be could get was that America was really part of that expanded England of which he was always thinking. He hoped an Englishmen would succeed in discovering the South Pole. Referring to the importance of local government., Aldermtn B nnett said many towns in England were an absolute disgrace to civilization, a blot and strain on the fame of the country. Who was to blame for that? Every man who had a vote was more or less to name for that state of things. It was a scandal in a town like 'Warrington that there should he so much difficulty in getting capable and pudic-spirited men to COM forward and d their duty to the town. They gave miserable and centsanptible excuses such as "I world not be seen with such a lot." (Laughter.) They had no ladids in the Council yet but he hoped they soon would have, and it was more than likely that it would not-end there. (Laughter.) Service of the State . was a noble ambition and he had a feeling that no man was really worthy of being a citizen of a free State unlesci, when occasion required, he was prepared and willing to defend that State. if necessary, at the sacrifice of his life. Eng7and could do rrore for civilization, progress, humanity and Christianity than any other ration in the world if England were true to its responsibilities and if every English boy and girl tried to do his or her duty. (Applause). Councillor FULTON-SMITH proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayor. Councillor 11. ROBERTS, in seconding, said be did not believe England was decadent or that the young people were decadent either. The vote was heartily accorded and a similar com- pliment was passed to the Chairman for presiding. During the afternoon the school choir gave the choruses" Whispering wind" and "After rain" with taste and expression and a number of girls sang "The House that Hook built" in charming stsle. There was also a clever display of Swedish drill and dumb- hell exercises by pupil teachers and the proceedings closed with the school song" Forty years en."

The Bedroom Window.—Our ancestors thought with the Psalmist, that it was well to beware of the pesti- lenee that walketh in darkness. Hence they shut their windows at nightfall, and they were wise says a writer in the Press, who is a little angry with the "fresh Air faidists." Not for nothing, he says, do the dogs, cats' and birds cover their mouths when they go to sleep at night, and, in his opinion it is most unwise to sleee with an open bedroom window, except in the height cq summer. That is not a theory that will find general acceptance in these days of light, fair, but of course circumstances alter cases. There is no great need for the open window in a large airey bedroom, with ono or two do3rs which it may be possible to leave open, bat in a small shut-up room, the open window is desire, even though the air has an autumn dampness aboet 1,, to which our friend the "ante fresh air faddist" ev much objects. WOMEN'S CHAT.

Princess Mary of Wales.—Princess Mary, who iq now in her thirteenth year, is already in possession of her own suite of apartments at Marlborough House, in charge of her governesses. The young Princess can scgrcely be termed a student, but her Royal Highness is as much an open air aspirant as the Princess Patricia of Connaught. And in her love of romp and mischief, Princess Mary may be said to greatly excel, as those in charge of her younger brothers—whom she loves to lead into all kinds of tnforbidden joys—known full well.

The Lyttonsf—The Dowager Lady Lytton and the Dowager Lady Lech, are twin eisters, daughters of the Hon. Ernest Villiers, and both the widows of "dis- tinguished men. Lady Constance Lytton, who has twice been in and out of gaol as a vehement member of the militant suffiagists, is the Dowagers second daughter. The Lyttons are descended from Sir Robert de Lytton, who was controller of the Household of Henry IV., and the late Earl was the son of Baron Lytton the novelist—Lady Constance Lytton's younger brother—the Hon. Neville Stephon Lytton—is married to a great granddaughter of Lord Byron, and his children are therefore descended on both sides fi our the bearers of two great names in literature—Lord Byron the poet and Edwarti•Bulwer Lytton the novelist. -Mrs. Penkhurst in America.—Mrs. Pankhurst, the suffragist, appears to have had rather a mixed recep- tion in America. The ladies of the Four Hundred, ccnstituting the fashionable society of New York, decided to boycott her meetings as a protest against "militant methods." Then, whilst there are plenty of Wornen's Suffragist Societies in America, their leaders appear to be jealous of any interference from outside advocates of the cause. They have not given Mrs. Pankhurst a very cordial welcome, probably because whilst she can command as much as £6O for a lecture, no Americhn suffragist can get more than £2O. Tbat is humiliating to those imbued with ideas of America's greatness, and the suffragist societies have taken to abusing one another, and will evidently welcome the departure of Mrs. Paakhurst from their midst. One Child One Cot.—Several of the great Midland towns have been leading the way in efforts to improve the conditions of infant life amongst the poor, and this movement has led to a curious competition at Hudders- field. One of the first necessities for the healthy up-bringing of infants is a separate cot for each child, but as the very poor cannot afford to go to the furnish- ing ironmonger, it was suggested that very good cots could be improvised out of an old clothes basket, or a twopenny banana crate. A competition to produce the test cots out of such inexpensive materials was there- upon started by the Womens Total Abstinence Union, and resulted in some twenty or more co's being exhibiteo; for the best of which prizes were presented by Alderman Broadbent. It was shown that good enough cots could be produced for a shilling, a price which should bring them within reach of the very poor, and according to health scientists one child one cot, is a more important matter for the well-heing of the reel than is one man—or one woman—one vote. Gambling and the Church.—The question that tuts again been raised, and somewhat energetically, con- cerning the suitability or unsuitability of raising money for the Church by means of Whist Drives and Bridge Touinaments bas been looked at from msny points of view. One cannot however, get away from the fact that all sucla methods savor of the idea of giving reluct3ntly and with a fixed idea of gaining some little pleasure or satisfaction from the transaction. And one carnot quite forget that all games of chance, and bear- ing a suspicion of gambling, are best kept quite apart and separate from religion. Undoubtedly therefore Whist Drives and Bridge Tournrnents—quite harmless in themselves—should not be connected in any way,

Wakefield Trinity v. Hunslet. Keighley v. Treherbert. Led s v. Ebbw Vale. Rohlile hornets v. Merthyr. Runcorn v. %Vigil'. SAlford v. Widn es.

Warrington will return to League football to-day with zest. They are third in the League table and by the end of the month should be at the top. To-day's opposition will be furnished by Broughton Rangers, who have done very little this season to maintain their reputation. Warrington will not have to treat them lightly, however, but go all out for a sub- stantial win. Last season the margin was 10-0 in Warrington's favour.

There is only one alteration in the Warrington thirteen as compared with last week's team. Frawley takes the place of Bradshaw and will, I hope, soon regain the form which distinguished him when repre- senting the Kangaroos. Be played clever football for the " A" team last Saturday, but did not exert himself greatly.

The teams against Broughton Rangers will be : Tilley, full back; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor and Frawley, three-quarters; Brooks and Jenkins, half backs; Boardman, Thomas, O'Neill, O'Malley, Shugars and Belton, forwards.

The" A" team should maintain its unbeaten record at Broughton with the following strong team : Mclntyre, full back ; Bradshaw, Jordan, Cartwright and Smith, threequarters ; Lloyd and Daintith, half backs; Bradshaw, Gibbs, Harrison, Harmer, Naylor and Cheaters, forwards.

NOTES BY " OVALIST."

Raving said" good-bye" to the Lancashire Cup, the Warrington men can now devote themselves to more Igerious matters—such as the League' Championship! There did not seem to be any very keen disappoint- among Warrington's supporters when the team. 19(4 at Wigan. The result was according to proba- adities, and beyond a natural desire to see the LWirepullers triumph, the prevailing feeling seemed to "e that Warrington were well out of a competition Which throw a lot of extra work on the men for the chance of only a moderate amount of glory. -

„Wigan won by 15 points to 8 points, and they were smarter team on the day's play. But after, 4droltting so much it is only bare justice to Warring- -4741 to add that Wigan were lucky in many respects, lltl Warrington were very unfortunate not to have at least drawn the game. The course of the play was tather curious. During the first half Warrington !tere hardly in the picture. Wigan played superior kotball and everything came off. Five minutes from the start Warrington were penalised in front of the tcosts for off-side, and the goal was a gift to Leytham. ive minutes later Warrington were again ?sufferers la a similar way, though the decision was not so elearly just. There was no question about Leytham's g°4l, however. Then followed a spell of desperate attack on the Wigan line, in which Warrington ‘tatild have scored but for some faulty handling. While Btill on the attack Jenkins miskicked across the field and put Leytham in possession. The Wigan skipper .asily slipped Bradshaw—too easily in fact—and after ra really beautifully run he beat Tilley and scored a try. Thus at half-time, Warrington had a margin °' seven points against them.

It was the general opinion that they were A beaten de, but the more optimistic of their supporters got t 11 justification later on for the faith that was in tirta. The tide did not turn all at once though ! hno ! The second half had not been long in pro- Pess before Leythtun got another try. He had stepped 111 touch and received a forward pass, but the howls Of the injured Warringtonians had no effect on the !core. It was a faulty point, but it counted. Even "Ytham seemed somewhat ashamed of the try, and I ?,tia told he admitted after the' match that it was a 10ng 'tin." Warrington's cup of sorrow was more 'mu full at this point.

~..,.Perhaps Wigan slackened. Anyhow, Warrington lied in great style and after 'a hot attack in the quarter Fish opened Warrington's score with LI'Y at the corner, just after missing a similar ,„IPortunity. Thomas kicked a fine goal and wiped half the arrears. Excitement bubbled up again, aIA the Wiganers raced back to the other end, Price Ittaing a beautiful opening with the WarFington pefence completely beaten. One of Wigan's forwards, arr, was sent off for foul play. Then we had !"Other example of Warrington's dogged cup-tie !ootball. They drove Wigan back by superior power 11.1 the scrummage and Fish took play right to the ;s1!le with a great individual effort. It was thought he scored, but the Refpree ruled him held outside. ere was a scrummage on the line, and the desperate f_wirepullers literally drove Wigan across the rut and L'llehed down near the posts. After the roar there ne dead silence. Warrington had only to place the over the bar and the match was drawn. Thomas 71_0k the kick with great care and missed! Then there ,74!3 another roar, but it was from the throats of felieved Wiganers, while Warrington supporters '?.ere asking savagely why Fish had not taken the O• K. If he had, there might have been 'a different ;Wt. Perhaps not. I remember Fish having just :Ich an opportunity at Old!lam with the scores I,laetly the same and failing equally badly. The ▪ ates decide these things, and not tht players!

%The match would certainly have been drawn if .6 goal had been kicked. There was only five ll'Autes left for play, and Warrington seemed 'spirited, while Wigan came up again smiling. They tot another five points through as faulty a decision as lave seen this season. Miller was tackled near the 17arrington line and brought to the ground. While en Ts back he threw the ball out and J. Thomas got a 11 without opposition, Leytham adding his third goal, 'llter being tackled Miller should have played the .214111, according to rule. Instead he passed, and the tlecision should have been a penalty kick for Warring- tpl instead of a try to Wigan. Every dog has his "67. Warrington's must be coming!

t. I thought Wigan were superior in their combina- On and speedier in their running. Warrington's galldling was never great, and neither halves nor ten.tres were good enough.' They failed to make the ,Pilway that the Wigan men did when in possession the ball or more opportunities would have matured. .firadshaw again proved unequal to the duel with Ythani, who will have a remarkable record against jarrington if he goes on. It amuses me to hear some e_ .our experts say that Leytham is no good. RUGBY FOOTBALL -

THE NORTHERN UNION.

Results of Saturday's Matches.

County Cup Competitions.

YORKSHIRE SENIOR CUP.-FIRST ROUND.

G. T. P. G. T. P. Hunslet 3 4 18 Bradford N'th'rn 0 0 0 Wakefield Trinity 0 1 3 Leeds.. 1 0 9 Bramley York 2 1 7 Hull - 0 2 6 Huddersfield LANCASHIRE SENIOR CUP.—FIRST ROUND.

Wigan Leigh 3 3 15 Barrow 0 1 3 Oldham 7 8 38 Rochdale Wants 0 2 6 St. Helens Warrington's Great Fight at Wigan.

The most attractive tie in the first round of the Lancashire Cup-ties was undoubtedly the one between Wigan and Warrington at Central Pdrk, Wigan. Both sides were fully represented, and 20,000 spectators flocked to the ground, more than 2,000 of whom came from Warrington. The odds were slightly in favour of the borne side, and as events turned out they gained the victory, though Warrington had the drawing of the match in their hands ne&r the close of the game. Thomas missed a simple goal kick, and Wigan after- wards scored again, though the point was Opealed against. It was a keen, exciting game and contested— with the exception of one incident—in very good spirit. The teams and officials were:

Wigan —Sbarrock, back; Leytham, Jenkins, Price, and Miller. three-quarter backs; Jones and Thomas, half backs ; Ramsdale, Silcock, Whittaker, Francis, Parr and Cheetham forwards. Warrington—Tilley, back; Fish, Diokenson, Taylor and Bradshaw, three-quarter backs ; Jenkins and Brooks, half backs; Boardman, Belton, Shugars,Thomas, O'Neill and O'Malley, forwards. Referee, Mr. G. F. Dickinson. Halifax. Touch judges, Messrs. E. Tongs, Swinton, and R. Jones. Widnes. Warrington lost the toss and Thomas set the ball in motion. Some lofty kicking by Jenkins and Leytham looked ominous, but Dickenson and Taylor were safe in their catching and play was warmly contested at centre field. Eventually Warrington forced matters, and Jenkins dribbling through sent the ball over the line, but Sharmck saved cleverly. Following the drop-out the Wiganers reached the Warrington 25 flag as the result of a misunderstanding between Bradshaw and Tilley, and Jones opening out the game, Thomas, Jenkins and Miller in'turn handled the sphere, but the wing man was speedily bustled into touch by Taylor. The Warrington forwards, in wheeling a pack, got offside, and the referee awarded a penalty against Shugars. Leytham took the shot at goal and easily sent the ball over. Warrington went direct to the Wigan quarters from halt-way and dashes by Jenkins, Brooks and Taylor caused the locals trouble. Passing was tried and the oval was speedily sent across to Fish, who, in essaying to defeat Miller, was tackled and Wigan escaped. The forwards threw plenty of energy into their work, end a brilliant effort by Boardman was only checked inches inside. J. Thomas ably utiliied the advantage of touch und follow- ing a judicious kick the ball was worked in close proximity the Warrington quarter line, where Leytham bad a fine chance, only to slip when apposition bad been cleared. By easy stages Warrington got the ball away to hilf-way, where Fish and Dickenson combined well, and it was left to Sbarrock to clear of the invasion. Wigan again attacked, Warrington again being penalized in a good position. Leytham p'aced his side further ahead. Warrington did not relax their energies in the least, and after Taylor and Bradshaw had done good work on the right Sharrock was lucky in holding up the young three-quarter at the corner. The home forwards, led by Siloock, forced the game to neutral quarters. lie-e Jenkins kicked across the field from the Warring- ton pack. Leytham dodged between Taylor and Brad- shaw and raced away to the open. Here Tilley's speed

was deficient, for the Wigan skipper continued his career, and though Fish took up the chase the advan- tage gained was too great, and a hurricane of cheers was a fitting reward to what was a brilliant effort. Sharrock missed the goal. Warrington next became pro ninent in some smart work in mid-field, and Dicken- boll made a commendable effort to get through, knock- ing down the referee in tho course of the rush. A long shot at goal by Leytbam hid a futile ending and at half-time the visitors were in a minority by two goals and one try (seven points) to nil. On resuming, the game contir ued to be strenuously fought and the pace did not slacken in the least. Toe Prat piece of pl .y which met with appreciation was a smart movement of the Warrington backs, and Fish and Dickenson raced along Ouch, passing and re-passing in fine style. Thomas next broke away and handing on to Jenkins that player made an opening for Leytham, who though he put his foot in touch succeeded in grounding at the corner. Warrington appealed against the point, but in vain. The visitors were not daunted by this reversa, but threw more energy into their work, and a fast forward rush, led by Thomas and O'Malley, was well saved by Sharrock. Warrington continued on the aggressive, and after Fish had failed to take advantage of a somewhat easy effort to cross, Leytham r.nd Jenkins forced matters to half-way line. War- rington again excelled in loose forward work, and Sh .rrcck and Price failing to stop the rush. Boardman fielded opposite the posts and throwing wide to Fish, that player scored. Thomas took the kick and landed a magnificent goal. Wigan immediately visited the Warrington quarters on resuming. PI ice tried a drop at goal, the ball striking the goal-post and rebounding into the field of play. Next came a lofty kick by Dickenson, and Sharrock failing to field, Fish raced to the line, being grounded inches inside. There was a strong appeal for the try, but a scrimmage was ordered, and no sooner had this been formed than the Warrington sextette rushed the opposition over and Thomas was awarded a try. The position was a good one and excitement was intense as Thomas took the equalising kick. The ball was carefully placed and the kick taken, but the shot was a failure, and the signalling of the same was hailed with cheers by Wigan and groans of disappointment by the Warrington section. Only a few minutes were now left for play. but during that time the visitors forced matters and Taylor and Fish were again to the fore with good work. Just in time Miller ran to the corner and was tackled. While on the ground he threw to J, Thomas, and the break of the rules was overlooked. Thomas got an easy try, and Leytham kicked another fine goal, Final: Wigan,3 goals 3 tries, (15 points) ; Warrington 1 goal, 2 tries (8 points.)

The Examiner, Saturday, October 23, 1909

Northern Rugby League.

G. T. P. G. T. P. Broughton R. 3 5 21 Swinton Keighley bbbw Vale 0 2 6 Batley Runcorn The game between Treherbert and Salford was post- poned owing to the ground being flooded.

RUNCORN GAIN POINTS IN WALES. Runcorn played a League game on Saturday at Merthyr Tydvil before 3,000 spectators. Several. of the visiting team having lately received injuries, there were alterations In their combination. The visitor's lighter pack were fast and gained advantage by their speed, and they showed superiority behind the scrum- mage. They soon got the better of matters, and from a fine run down the field Padbury scored nicely behind the post, Moran goal ing. Good passing by the visitors followed, in which they promoted a scientific attack, but the movement failed. Subsequently they again got away and near the goal White passed from the scrum- mage, Flannagan rnshing forward to receive, and adding to the score. Moran again safely goaled. Unsworth with a dash, nearly got through, and some further movements of the visitors proved ineffectual. Padbury opened out well and walked in, but the try was not improved. At half-time the visitors led by 13 points to nil.

_ After the interval Merthyr pressed vigorously, and the forward did good work, especially Lewis. From a penalty Paddison scored a goal and from a rush Bevan got a try, which was unconverted. Result—Runcorn, 2 goals 3 tries (13 points), Merthyr 1 goal 1 try (5 points).

HELENS MAINTAIN THEIR GOOD FORM. Widnes and St. Helens met at Widnes, and there were about 8,000 spectators present. At the outset St. Helens attacked, and Cteevey placed a goal from a mark. After this the home forwards got possession almost every time, and the backs showed good com- bination, but it was not effective. Towards the interval Widnes kept up a hot attack, and from a scrummage close in Lally dropped a goal. This was followed soon afterwards by another dropped goal from Noon. Half • time—Widnes 2 goals (4 points), St. Helens 1 goal (2 points). In the second half St. Helens showed improve 4 form, and Trenwith picked up from a scrummage dose to the line and scored. Creevey improved. The cheers had hardly died away when Trenwith dashed away, and gave to J. Greenwood, who scored a second try. Towards tbe finish Widnes iittscked, but failed to score Result :—St. Helens 2 goals 2 tries (10 points), Widnes 2 goals (4 points).

LANCASHIRE SENIOR CUP.

A meeting of the Lancashire County Union Com- mittee was held at Manchester on Monday evening, Mr. Robert Collinge (Rochdale Hornets) occupying the chair. WARRINGTON "A " V. INcE.—These teams tried conclusions at Wilderspool before a large crowd, the home aide including Frawley, the Australian, who, how- ever, did not appear to have thoroughly recovered from his injury. YLANDS RECS. v. WIDNES WHITE STAR.

Last Saturday, Rylands' Recs. played Widnes White Star on the ground of the former. The game resulted in a pointless draw. The home team were handicapped by having three men off, including Darbyshire, who was injured in the match with P. and K. but was not taken to the infirmary as stated last week. The teams were fairly equal and each side kept pressing, and it was only the smart tackling that prevented any side scoring. Furness for Rylands had hard lines in not scoring from a cross kick by Horracks. Fillingbam, also a Rylands' man, just missed, when attending a drop goal. Noon for the visitors was particularly unfortunate in not registering a try, for although he had defeated Rylands' backs he was pulled up by McGowan when only five yards off the line. During the second half a Widnes representative had a good try for a goal which just grazed the upright. Today (Saturday) Rylands' Recs. will play St. Helens White Rose away. STOCKTON HEATH v. LIVERPOOL AND DISTRICT LEAGUE.

WARRINGTON ALBION'S V. HELSBY.—Warrington Albions entertained He!shy on Saturday afternoon at Liverpool-road. There was a good attendance. Dilworth won the toss for the Albions and Griffiths started. Doyle made little progress for Dilworth kicked down the field. The ball was returned, end Forster got hold.cif the ball and defeated Wyatt with a ground shot. Thomas and Hunter combived well and the former unlucky in not opening the Albions account. Hughes had an open goal presented him, but his shot went wide. Helsby still pressed and Wyatt saved well from 'Locket, Ritchie and Hunter raced down on the Albions right, but L. Fletcher ran back and cleared, The home forwards troubled the Helsby backs severely, but were unable•to place the ball within the visiting team's net. For a few minutes the backs on both sides had the ball to themselres, long kicks being prominent. Ultimately Pendlebury got the better of Hughes and was making for goal when Fletcher ran across and upset him. Wyatt was lucky in saving a minute later. Play centred round the visitors goal, but the Albions were unable to score up to half-time. Interval : Helsby, ode goal ; Albion, nil. Play was keen in the second half. Thomas broke away and beat the backs, hue Jones saved well. He saved high up later a header from Hunter. Forster broke away and had only Wyatt to beat when close in, but Short intercepted and his shot went wide. The ball was rushed from end to end frequently, and the opposing backs were kept busy. Ellworth worked the ball into the Helsby quarters, and beat Jones with a high dropping shot. Result: Albion, 1; Helsby, 1.

FOOTBALL GOSSIP.

BY B. BENNISON.

ILLUSTRATED BY JACK DODWORTII.

In other days—the good old days, I like to call them—there were no two full-backs I liked to see snore than Tom Brandon and Johnny Forbes. of Blackburn Rovers. Nicholas J. Ross appealed to the imagination; there was some- thing • charmingly distinctive about him and Bob' Holmes and Howorth, who were at one time or other his partners. Donald Gow was wonderful, but Brandon and Forbes, totally dissimilar, were my ideal pair. I preferred them even to the brothers Walters. Now at Black- burn as the successors of Brandon and Forbes we have Bob Crompton and Cowell, two worthy sons of Lancashire. Crompton, of course, even when in his teens, was a remarkable player; to- day he stands alone; there is no right-back like him; big and strong, the picture of health, Crompton is Jan Ridd in real life. His bigness is exaggerated as he is ranged alongside Cowell—a wee fellow indeed. You have physi- cal extremes with a vengeance. Cowell is a smaller man than Herbert Burgess, who, by the way, seems to have passed out of big football, though I understand that he is still a member of the Manchester United Club; he is every bit as good as the old International was, which is saying much, for really when Burgess was at the height of his power there was not a

PI.KI AM* --t-s.rt TIN

able internationalists.

cleverer footballer in his position in the coun- try. One writes of Cowell because he is playing an absolutely great game. He seems stronger physically than last season, and one can scarcely imagine a greater trio of defenders than Ash- croft, Crompton, and Cowell, who will most likely be asked to play with his fellow - back in the Internationals now that Jesse Pennington may possibly be affected by his most unfortu- nate injury. The Blackburn defence so far has been the foundation of the Rovers' team; and so long as the present form is maintained•the days at Ewood Park will be very prosperous ones. Sheffield United's Success. It would appear that there will be a re- ilniffling of honlurs this season; and if there is the game will be all the better. Perhaps the most astounding featuni of League life is the dontinued suocess of Sheffield United. No one could have poesibly dreamt that they would go into the second month of the season without having suffered a single reverse. People at Bramall-lane, almost from the birth of the United, have been particularly happy in deve- loping talent. Sheffield United have done as much, as most folk 41 Inc ire :Artiaimkpef

acqutinig lui,Lu•ctimr3 wile nave maw tneir repu- tation elsewhere. Look over the list of young men now with the club, and you will not discover that in any individual sense they have contributed to the game's his- tory. In saying this, I refer to the younger school at Bramall- lane—to men like Kitchen and Harding°, for instance. - It may be that Sheffield United will finish the League cham- pions; certainly no team are playing better at present, but they can scarcely hope to improve upon their performance against Evcrton at Goodison Park. Their victory proves, if it proves anything at all, that Sheffield United arc a most excellent side. Ordered Off!

Everybody will be very, sorry to hear' that James Turnbull, the Manchester United centre- forward, and Hunter of Aston Villa, were sent off the field at Clayton for an unwarrantable loss -of temper. It is, I know, most difficult to curb one's feelings, at all times; but one looks to men

like Turnbiill and Hunter to be beyoind such an affair as that in which they appear to have en- gaged et Clayton. • I expect they are both very sorry that they forgot themselves; they need make their apologies to the Football Associa- tion•, they must be prepared to pay the penalty of their indiscretions.

It will appear very strange to many people that at a time when Woolwich Arsenal can do little right—they aro passing through a most un- happy experience—Bury should come along and bid for and take off Lee, one of the Arsenal for- wards. I should have thought that Woolwich had absolutely. no players to spare. Leo at times is a very capable footballer; he promised to do a great deal better than he has done at Plum- itead. Still. I think Bury have done well to take him to Gigg-lane, though I suspect that, like a good many more clubs, they would have 'pre- ferred the young Scotsman, Greenaway, who is a bright and accomplished forward. Steve Bloomer as Centre. I do not suppose that Middlesbrough will finish the season amotig the high-placed clubs in the First Djvision; but I am almost sure that, on _their day, the forwards, as a combination, will bear comparison with any five in the country. Really, however, do those responsible for the selection of the team seriously expect us to ac- cept Groves, whom we have always thought to be a back, as a right-wing forward? It was diffi- cult to refrain from smiling when one saw Stephen Bloomer in the centre and Groves and Common on the right- wing. It was a queerly- constructed Middlesbrough team one saw at Tot- tenham; but it was too much for the "Spurs," at any rate.

London Clubs Doing Badly. These are poor days for the three London First League clubs; but in the Second Division Fulham, without doing anything very startling, are playing steadily and well. This they need

f-jo RUS

Irday.

continue to do. It becomes increasingly difficult every year for a club to march from the Second to the First Division. There is not a pin tc choose between any of the first half-dozen, though at the moment Glossop clearly hold thE best record.. But what, after all, is the spice ol Second League football is that the apparently strongest club can easily come a cropper when they engage in a match they seem to have in keeping. Take the recent experience of West Bromwich Albion, who were well beaten b 3 Wolverhamnton Wanderers. There has been a loud cry for new men at Wolverhampton lately and results justified the demand for recruits but there cannot be very much wrong with the side when they go and beat West Bromwich by three goals to one.

WILD BRITISH CATTLE.—The wild cattle of Great Britain have become one of the curiosities of the bovine race in this country, says "Old Breeder," in "Farm and Home." lhere are only very few herds of them remaining, and most of these are diminishing from a very natural cause. Of course, they are con- fined in parks, and are jealously guarded from any admixture of alien blood. They are as wild as buffaloes, and are treated in the same way as deer. In colour they are white, with red ears, and historians assert that they had a large share in the evolution of the Shorthorn, as it is known to-day. It iacertain that the colour men- tioned crops up quite unexpectedly in our pedigree herds. They will probably become extinct in a feu years, owing to the extreme difficulty of procuring sires unrelated to the herds, and yet of the same-breed.

POISONED HANDS CURED.

" I have suffered four years with poisoned hands, caused by dye. I tried many physicians and hospitals and had my fingers lanced without any success. After all the cutting my hands got to be a mass of small holes and water came out of them. The itching was something terrible, especially at night, and I could not sleep. After using so many hospital remedies without avail I thought I should have to suffer for ever. Then seeing an advertisement of the Cuticura Remedies I thought I would try the Cuticura Soap, Cuticura Ointment and Cuticura Resolvent Pills. There is no more breaking out of water and I can sleep well, without itching. I only used two cakes of Cuticura Soap, one box of Ointment and two vials of Pills to effect a complete cure. R. Goodman, 88, Chamber St., Leman St., London, E., June 19, 1909."

===== 0002601_19091023_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM WARRINGTON TO WIEN AND BACK.

WITH SOME IMPRESSIONS BY THE WAY. (SPECIALLY WRITTEN FOR "THE EXAMINER" BY ALDERMAN BENNETT).

===== 0002601_19091023_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
When the Warrington Corporation were good enough to appoint me as their repre- sentative on the Second Continental Town Planning Tour, I promised the Editor of "The Examiner" that I would give him some impressions of the same. I have already reported to the Town Council in reference to the special objects of the Tour, but there were many incidents in my long journey of something like two thousand five hundred miles which it is cimpossible to include in a formal report, and at eyery stage there was some interesting fact to be noted, or some experience which it is a pleasure to recall.

I left Warrington by the eight o'clock morning express for Euston on Friday, the 10th September, and, after lunching in London, joined the rest of the party at Charing Cross Station at two o'clock. Organized by the National Housing Reform Council, and numbering thirty-four all told, it included the energetic Chairman of that influential body, Alderman William Thomp- soil,the Mayor of Richmond ; the Chairman of its Finance Committee, Councillor Harold Shawcross, of Rochdale; its courteous and untiring Secretary, Mr. Henry R. Aldridge, of Leicester; Mr. Stanley P. Adshead, the new Professor of Civic Design at the Liver- pool University; Mr. Albert E. Cave, the genial editor of the "Municipal Journal" ; Mr. Walter Peacock, Surveyor General to the Estates of the Duchy of Cornwall; Mr. W. McGregor Ross, who, as Director of

===== 0002601_19091023_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
unabated interest at the higher and the lower town, the first enclosed by massive walls and bastions; the domed cathedral; and the busy harbour from one part of which Napoleon had proposed to hurl his great flotilla on our English coasts; but I was not loth to leave them all behind and glide out quickly into the shadowy wastes of sea and sand which fringe the river Liane and the railway • till it gradually diverges through long, rows of unfamiliar trees into the pleasant plains of La Belle France. Almost as soon as we had started we sat down together to our first meal in a continental train. It was too dark to see anything of the country, but I remember that we stopped at Abbeville, with its girdle of ramparts (for close upon two hundred years an English city) ; and then at Amiens, the birth Place of Peter the Hermit, and though we could not see it, thought about its fine Cathedral, and recalled the famous Treaty once concluded there. ,In spite of its 91,000 inhabitants, the town looked singularly dark and silent. as we stepped out for a moment on to the low platform ; and the same remark applies to Loan, which is, however, a much smaller town, and stands upon a low"e' and isolated hill and also boasts a fine Cathedral. We expected to change here, but our carriage was detached instead, and it was not until we had nearly reached the end of our five hours journey that we had to leave the train at Betheny just outside Rheims, and, on a

===== 0002601_19091023_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
RHEI MS.

Public Works in British East Africa, was looking out for new ideas for that great new Colony; Mr. Councillor Peter Wright, of Newport, the champion twelve stone wrestler of the world; two clergymen (both Socialists); a Congregational Minister who was also a town councillor and never wore his hat ; and a miscellaneous company of surveyors, housing reformers and municipal aldermen councillors and officials too numerous to mention, but all exceedingly good fellows. I must not forget to add that we were honoured by the company of three ladies, including Miss Sybella Gurney, the gifted -and charm- ing hon. secretary of the Co-Partnership Tenant's Housing Council. A more interest- ing, public spirited and enlightened party it has not lately been my lot to meet. At twenty minutes past two,in two saloons, we all set out for Folkestone, and I did my best to divide my attention between the rapidly receding English landscape and an English gentleman who had made a fortune as a mining engineer in Australia, and was very full of Germany and all that he had seen there in the Spring. As we crossed the Thames, and saw the two great towers of Westminster, I recollected that the Town Planning Bill, which was the primary inspiration of our Tour, wa,s down for its Third Reading in the House of Commons that very afternoon. The conversation of my friend, however, was so engrossing that it left me little time for looking out of the window, though I did succeed in catching (once or twice) a flying glimpse of some of London's poor, freed for a little time from all the squalor of their everyday surroundings and happily engaged in picking hops beneath the soft blue Kentish sky. The last_ time I had visited the Continent was in 1905, when I attended the Autumnal Meeting of the Associated Chambers of Commerce at Liege; and on that occasion I had walked down to the steamer with Sir William Holland, who was then the Chairman of that important body. It was a curious coincidence that the first figure I now observed, on leaving the train, was the same Sir William, and that I walked down once again with him to the same steamer. He told me that he was relieving the tedium of his parliamentary duties by a week-end trip to Paris.

We lost the `.`, white walls" of our much- loved native isle almost as soon as we had left the land, for there was a soft gray mist upon the sea; and, almost ere I realized it, I was gazing once more at the harbour of Boulogne, the crowd of bathing tents, the big Casino and the line of white hotels which fringe the harbour. As the steamer slowed down quietly, I asked Sir William what he thought about the pros- pects of the Budget, and told him how diffi- cult it was for me, as a convinced Tariff Reformer, to oppose a measure with much of which, as a Social Reformer, I heartily agreed. He sympathized with my dilemma (though he is a staunch Free Trader), and, as the steamer neared her moorings and her decks became a chaos of promiscuous passengers and jumbled baggage, we parted with the mutual hope that our hereditary legislators will, after all, be wise enough to let the Budget pass, and so relieve one ardent Tariff Reformer, at any rate, from a very awkward dilemma.

...... The quays, the streets, the railway and the Station at Boulogne are so mixed up that it is difficult to tell where the one ends and the other begins, and the general impression left upon one's mind is that of mingled danger and confusion. The strange low platforms which are usual at these continental stations are not unlike the side-paths in our English streets. The trains are entered by two or three steps at the end of each carriage, and they have a knack :of starting off with- out the slightest fuss, and, if you are not careful, leaving you in the lurch. I only just contrived to scramble up to our reserved compartment in the nick of time, and in another moment we had left the bustle of the Station/and the busy quays behind, and were gliding swiftly on our way to Rheims. I had very pleasant recollections of a previous visit to Boulogne, when I was fortunate enough to spend a night or two at this extremely quaint French port and watering place; and I had gazed once more with

dark and silent country platform, wait until another train came up to take us to our destination. Rheims, by some curious perversity of language, is spelt " Reims " and pronounced "Rance," but we found a classic warrant for the natural pronuncia- tion in the legend of the famous Jackdaw, and everybody seemed to say that this sufficed. If, however, we specially wished to impress each other, and were disposed to give ourselves airs, we called it" Rance," with a sort of snuffle in the nose to add a little local colour. Rheims, too, looked strangely silent and deserted as, in a funereal sort of covered wagonette, we left the Station and rode through a broad leafy open space, and on along the stony streets to the Grand Hotel, which stands right in the shadow of the great Cathedral. A peep at its twin towers, which loomed directly over us, beneath the stars, some fifty yards away ; a quiet chat with Mr. Shawcross in a neighbouring café; and, at midnight, I was very glad to climb three flights of stairs and wrestle uhsue,cess- fully with the problem how to cover my poor weary limbs with a short eider down which reminded me more of a balloon than a bed quilt. The sun was shining brightly through the white Venetian shutters of my bedroom window when I woke the following morning. I flung them open and gazed out across the ancient city wistfully, but there was little time for reverie; and so I quickly dressed and joined my friends at their coffee, rolls and honey, which, with little variation, is the usual continental breakfast. It suited me extremely well, and wherever I went the coffee was—well coffee! And what can any one say more? As soon as we had finished, we found three large brakes, provided by the town awaiting us, and set out to inspect its varied glories. It would, of course, be quite impossible to do justice either to its history, its architecture or its general characteristics in the limits of this hasty article, and with its leading civic features I have dealt else- where; but I may say that it stands upon the right bank of the little river Vesle, in a plain which is bounded by vine-clad hills i that it was the capital of the Remi, and the Durocortorum of the Romans; that Christianity was preached here in the third century; and that, after the departure of the Vandals and the Huns, it became a religious centre of the first importance. Here Clovis was baptized and many of the kings of France were crowned. From its forgotten walls brave Joan of Arc drove out the English, and (such are Time's .revenges) English firms now mainly carry on its thriving trade in flannels, cashmeres and merinos.lt has to-day a population of 110,000, and who is there who has not heard how, just above its vine-clad plains, some two months ago, man first successfully disputed with the birds for the dominion of the air? We drove through the Place Royal and the long straight Rue du Fauborg Ceres, and visited some fine new streets but recently laid out, and I was glad to notice that, in several instances, when four streets intersected, Rheims, like Warrington, had planned a Circus. We visited a fine new Elementary School, and passed the Tramsheds,and the Cemetery, and a Military Hospital and a Creche, and rode by the establishments of many world-famed makers of champagne, and round a fine old. Roman arch (the Porte de Mars), most care- fully preserved, and a beautiful foun- tain, its broad stone basin poised upon the shoulders of a lovely group of emblematic female figures, and passed on into the chest- nut shaded Public Promenades,and sauntered slowly through the Parc de la Patte and the fine adjacent Horticultural Gardens, ' where fruits, and flowers, and vegetables of a hundred kinds grew temptingly; and then we crossed the big Canal; and, after glancing at the Abattoir and the public Wash-Houses along the river, rode back through the Place Royal, with its fine bronze statue af Louis the Fifteenth, erected in 1818, in place of one destroyed at the Revolution, and adorned with the original figures of Mild Government and Popular Happiness; and passed some quaint old ,
