The Examiner, Saturday, October 23, 1909.

medival houses, and a flower-filled market, and so through the spacious Place des Marches to the beautiful Hotel de Ville, where M. Langlois, the most English-look- ing Frenchman I have ever seen, received us cordially, amid the portraits of his prede- cessors, and drank to us and to the Entente in the only beverage which really appeared to suit the place and the occasion. Then we saw the city archives, and the fine museum, with its noble paintings and its faded tapestries, and the wonderful Roman mosaic work discovered at Rheims, 36 feet long by 26 feet broad, which finely represents the sports which once prevailed in the old amphitheatres Then, highly pleased with our first morning's work, we went back willingly to our Hotel for lunch.

In the afternoon we visited the School of Commerce and Industry and the handsome home for aged men and women, where the citizens of Rheims, on payment of a reason- able sum, can spend the sunset of theill lives in peace; and passed the big Municipal Hospital for Consumptives, and the ancient Church of St. Remi, which, though freely altered in modern times,"retains the outlines of a vast and noble basilica of the early part of the eleventh century,"and is more ancient, though, in my opinion, much less beautiful, than the Cathedral.

In the afternoon, we visited the School of Commerce and Industry, and the handsome home for aged men and women, where the citizens of Rheims, on payment of a moderate sum, may spend the sunset of their lives in peace; and saw the big municipal Hospital for Consumptives, and the ancient Church of St. Remi, " which, though freely altered in modern times, retains the outlines of a vast and noble basilica of the early part of the eleventh century," and is more ancient, though, in my opinion, much less beautiful than the Cathedral. Unluckily, we had not time to enter, for we were due to visit an entirely different, but not less interesting place, to wit, the famous " caves " of Messrs. Pommery and Greno, who are known all. over the world for their champagne. A Moorish-looking building and some curious air-shafts indicated their proximity, and, entering a large apartment which con- tained a barrel big enough to satisfy a race of Brobdingnagians, but beautifully carved and filled with the rich• product of the choicest vintages in France,we were received by a stout English gentleman who was, we understood,the manager for the firm,and who, assisted by two other English members of the staff, conducted us down a sloping path- way, to the huge chalk caverns, of a peculiar dryness and a singularly even temperature, in which the queen of wines,in endless armies of black bottles, was enduring the pro- bationary period of five years which is supposed to bring it to maturity. The " caves " are something like twelve miles in length, and cover eighty acres. In certain central dome-like places, emblematical designs of great artistic beauty have been carved out of the solid chalk, and,in the semi- darkness, look like marble. Each tunnel is appropriately distinguished by the name of some world-capital or famous statesman, and they formed a veritable maze bewilder- ing in their extent and their complexity. The vastness of the place astonished us, but we were even more surprized when we were quietly informed that twelve and a half million bottles of champagne are kept con- tinually in stock in these great cellars. The grapes are pressed at the vineyards and mingled here in -mighty barrels like the one which we had seen, the quality depending mainly on the vintage and the blend. The champagne is afterwards bottled, periodi- cally shaken, sometimes by hand and some- times by machinery, and stored at varying angles until, at last, each bottle is exactly upside down. The corks are then removed, with the sediment which has gradually been deposited thereon, a little of the champagne escaping at the same time. This loss is replaced by a mixture-of sugar, cognac and old wine, and the bottles are then corked once again, sent by an endless chain to upper rooms, wired, decked in the accustomed labels and packed in boxes and in baskets ready for the world-wide market, and one does not wonder that the price is high. . But I was surprized to discover that champagne is practically as dear in Rheims as in London. We witnessed nearly all the processes described above and then our courteous guides invited us to sample the result. The wine they gave us, with a delicate regard for what they called the English taste, was a" dry" wine, but it was the only " dry " champagne we tasted on our journey. The continental taste is all for the sweeter kinds, and I honestly confess that I like them much the best myself. Moreover, I have a shrewd suspicion that if they would only tell the truth, quite nine- teen out of every twenty Englishmen would say the same. But it sounds distinguished to say that you like a " dry " champagne, and an Englishman will suffer martyrdom in order to look big.

After this acceptable little interlude, our good friends went with us to the Parc Pommery, a fine new recreation ground of eighty acres—exactly the same area as the cellars—which the firm have lately opened .for the benefit of their employes; and, hear- ing here that the big dirigible, the "Colonel Renard," was at present housed in the huge wooden Eta rodome which stood in the broad valley just below, we all immediately expressed a wish to see it, and, thanks to the presence of the Mayor, who had been good enough to accompany us on our afternoon's epursion, we were allowed to pass the frog-like company of soldiers who were guarding it; to walk around the elongated bag which filled the mrodome ; to scrutinize the four small gas bags bulging from its farther end, to look at its commodious car, its powerful engines, its propeller and its steer- ing gear; and to vainly long to join in one of those wrial journeys between Paris, Rheims and Nancy which are seriously con- templated as part of a regular service. The monster had soared into the air, the month before, like some great whale amid a shoal of dolphins,and my good friend,Mr. J.A. Ransome, of Warrington, had seen them all together gliding peacefully above the sunny plains of 13etheny in Aviation Week; but there was not any chance of its emerging for our special benefit that day; our waggonette was waiting; this item, as our good friend Mr. Aldridge laughingly reminded us, was "an extra ;" and we had to get back to the town without delay. By passing that resolution they gave a certain amount of substantiality to the proposition and created an expectation of its fulfilment in years to come, when they knew their successors in office could do what they liked. That was not a businesslike or fair proposition. With regard to the alternative that the Education Authority should provide a higher or secondary school to which free access should bo given, the proposition required that the permanent staff should give the religious instruction. He was prepared to go a very long way to settle this frightful religious diffi- culty, but he was not prepared to go to the extent of requiring the servants of the committee, who should be appointed and paid for work purely in connection with secular instruction, to give denominational instruction of any kind vthatever. If denominational instruction was the highly important thing many people It-mostly believed it to be, it was not too much to expect them to provide the means for it to be given and not expect it to be given at the expense of the community. Canon STEVENSON: The denominations would pay for it.

Councillor ICOBEETS, continuing, said he objected to the giving of denominational instruction being made a condition of emp!oyment. lie did not, object to denominations who provided their own schools giving denominational teaching, although he knew that in that mitt or he would not early with him a very large section of that part of the community to which he hekonged. Personally he would give free access into all schools for giving religious instruction under certain proper conditions providing the instruction was given at th 4 expense of the denomi- nation and without intrfering with the staff. Councillor VERNON, as one of the managers of a denominationAl school, considered the proposition to allow freedom of access unworkable. It would lead to chaos.

The amendment was carried by a large mljority. MARRIED WOMEN TEACHERS. THE CASE OF MRS. CANN.

During he discussions on these resolutions s letter was read from Mrs. Langdon 1. Cann asking to be reinstated as superintendent of cookery instruction, but the committee could not see their way to accede to her request. The Rev. JAMES DAVIES moved an amendment thlt the minutes be not approved. He said that at the last meeting it was stated that a superintendent was not necessary, and that the staff was sufficient. Now it was proposed to appoint a superintendent. Some members might have voted at the lasteneeting against Mrs. Cann being reinstated on the ground that a superintendent was not wanted. There was no economy in discharging Mrs. Cann and appointing another superintendent. He thought an honourable understanding had been come to whereby married teachers in schools directly connected with the Education Authority should be treated exactly the same as teachers in non-provided schools, where marriage was not an educational disqualification. Let Mrs. Cann be re-instated and if it was decided later on that the married teachers bad to go then Mrs. Cann must go also. For the good name of the School Management Sub-committee Mrs. Cann should be re-instated. If she were dismissed the teachers would have no security of tenure of their positions and there was a danger that their hearts would not, be in their work. Mr. GREENING seconded.

The Rev. JOHN YONGE, who said he was obliged to leave the last meeting before Mrs. Cann's ease was voted upon, supported. He considered her dismissal had features of great hardship about it. ARCHITECTS AND THEIR FEES.

Mr. H. ROBERTS enquired for information respecting a communication which had been received from the Royal Institute of British Architects in reference to the conditions of the architectural competition for the Evelyn-street Council School. Alderman BOLTON said they advertised for plans and particulars, and they left it open to all architects to send in designs, the committee stipulating that the cost should be a certain sum • per head and that the plans should comply with certain conditions. Those con- ditions did not suit everyone. Seventy persons tendered on the committee's lines but 20 wanted conditions of their oWn, and the latter would t have left them in this condition that instead of getting a school at £lO per scholar it would be anything from that to £2O. (Laughter). The architects wanted unlimited scope and to waste as much money as possible. Councillor ROBERTS said it was in common circula- tion that the dispute was on the question of fees to be paid to the architects. Was the usual standard scale of fees supposed to be paid, or were the committee trying to get a cheap job and breaking down their own rule as to paying trade union prices? Alderman BOLTON: We leave the architects to settle their own fees.

THE PROPOSED NEW SCHOOL. GRACE'S POTTERY SITE APPROVED.

It having been reported to the Sites and Buildings Committee that the resolution passed by that com- mittee at their last meeting, with reference to the acquisition of the site in Winwick-road, had not been approved and adopted by the Education Committee, it was decided that ths resolution in question be re-affirmed. It was, therefore, resolved that subject to the Law Clerk being able to obtain sufficient adjoining land, the site in Winwick-road known as Grace's Pottery be recommended to the Education Committee as suit- able for a new school ; that the necessary notices be issued for the provision there of a school to accommo- date 1,250 children.

Alderman BOLTON, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said the committee were practically unanimous. The Corporation bought the land years ago for the purpose of building a school. The land was in the right place for the purpose, and the school would serve the needs of that end of the town. There were several schools in the district that were overcrowded, and many people could not afford to pay the school fees. The committee had been taunted with not having built schools fast enough, but directly they got a piece of land that was suitable it was said it was not the right spot. Councillor WILKINSON moved an amendment that the minute be,not approved, lie contended that it was the most unsuitable site they had in Warrington. The neighbourhood itself was objectionable as there were vArtous works in it throwing out all kinds of vapours which would not at all he conducive to the health of 1,200 children who would be educated in the school if it were erected. It was a district that would never develop even if they put up a ralace in it. Councillor LOCKER seconded. Mr. BRADSHAW pointed out that the Council approved a resolution in July that the site be set apart for a school.

Alderman BOLTON said 10 was not surprised at the remarks Councillor Wilkinson had made because he had advocated this particular site as suitable for a bowling green. If it was good enough for a bowling green he (Alderman Bolton), considered it.w4a4oo4enough for a school.

Councillor WILKINSON: A very. poor argument. Alderman BOLTON contended that it was. an admirable site, if they excluded sentiment fram the matter. The Beamont school was now full. '1 he Rev. J. The Council; of which Alderman Bolton was an admired ornament— (laughter)—ought to see that fresh air was admitted into the schools. In some of them theyjiad had te close the ventilators and all the windows lt ainst the prevailing winds because vast volumes of smoke evvePt in and the atmosphere became so foul. He contended that the present suggestion was one of the most remarkable which had ever been brought before the committee. The whole scheme of school building je Warrington was rotten and was not properly considered. The district, which was not growing, was not suitable for a-school. To commit themselves to such a. late expenditure for the largest school in Warrington which they could not fill was unwise. Councillor ROBERTS asked on what ground the last speaker made that remark. Mr. UPSON replied that accommodation was to be provided for 1.250 children. Councillor ROBERTS said he was surprised to hear that, lie was rather amazed to hear that there had been a diminution in the number of houses in the neighbourhood. That was a statement which ought to have evidence to prove it. Mr. tiPsoN : I referred to the houses which have been taken down. Councillor ROBERTS s- id there was no evidence of * diminution of population. On the contrary there bad been a considerable increase of population so far as Orford Ward was concerned at any rate. If the locality was such an awfol one as fain been described, what had the past and present munici- pal representatives of the district been doing not to do something for it? It was absolutely impracticable to carry out the suggestion that the schools should be built nearer to the centre of the town. , Mr. UNSWORTH supported the amendment and said that teere were nearly 2,000 places not filled up in the schools. Mr. UPsoN : Is it not a fact that this site has been viewed by an official from the Board of Education aaa condemned? WARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

RELIGIOUS TEACHING IN SECONDARY SCHOOLS.

Important Discussion.

There was a full agenda for the monthly meeting. of the Warrington Education Committee which was held at the Town Hall on Monday eveoing. Councillor J. Fulton Smith presided and there were also present : Mr. H. Roberts (deputy•chairman) ; Aldermen Bolton, James Evans, ForEhaw, Smethurst and Tinnion ; Coun- cillors Barton, Birtles, Dickenson, Locker, Lord, Sankey; Toole, G. N.Vernon and Wilkinson ; Revs. James Davies, andJ. Yonge ; Messrs. T. P. Bradshaw,R. T. Fairclough, L. Greening, A. J. Jolley, H. Kingdon, C. E. Parker E. Plinston, C. H. Uusworth, J. Upson, and G. Walker; Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes ; Mr. J. Moore Murray (director), and Mr. R. B. Roberts (asststant director).

===== 0002601_19091023_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SCHOOL OF ART.

CANON STEVENSON, in moving the adoption of the minutes of the School Management Committee, said a request was to be made to the Sites and Buildings Committee to provide extensions and additions to the School of Art. He thought the school was in every way juetifying the policy of the committee in extending and giving better accommodation. •(Hear, hear). The pupils were increasing in numbere, the work was appreciating and the results were improving. He earnestly hoped the sites and buildings sub-committee would see their way to carry out the extension which was very much needed. FAIRFIELD SCHOOL. THE RELIGIOUS QUESTION.

The School Management Sub-committee reported that they had had an interview with the managers of the Fairfield School with reference to the amalgama- tion of the two girls' departments. The maragers stated that they were willing to agree to the departments being amalgamated, provided the sub-committee would support them in certain proposals which they laid before the sub-committee. After considerable discus- sion, it was resolved that the managers be requested to submit in writing the exact nature of their proposals, so that the committee might fully consider the same. At a su'sequent meeting the following communica- tion was rend from the Managers: "The Managers of the - Fairfield school vill accept the amalg•tmation of their two Girls' Departments on certain terms. Their proposal is that the Local Education Authority shall, if mid when the needs of the district shall reqeire it, support an application to the Board of Education and grant all facilities for the building, by the Managers, of either a Secondary or a Higher Elementary Crouch of England school for girls, either at the Fairfield school or upon Churchproperty in Beresford-s} root, or in the alternative, that the Local Education Authority shall, in the same event, erect and open a Secondary or a Higher Elementary school for girls on other land near the Cemetery, and shall grant to all religious denominatiors it right of VCCOSP to the school to give and to superintend the giving by the permanent stair denominational religious instruction in school hours to those children whose parents require it." The Sub-committee resolved (a) That the proposals of the Managers be agreed to so far as it lies within the power of this committee to cl.> so; (b) that the two girls' departments of the Fairfield school be amalga- mated; aed (c) that the managers he requested to con- sider the necessary arrangements for carrying out the amalgamation, and to submit their suggestions to the committee as soon as possible."

Canon STEVENSON, in explaining the minute., said correspondence had been goin; on for some years over this matter. The managers were now prepared to accept the amalgamation of the two departments on conditions set forth in the carefully worded resolution. He supposed the managers felt thatin past years they had done their best to provide a higher education fc.r the children at that end of the town who required it. The two schools were to be amalgamated, and conse- quently, there would be simply one ordinary elementary school. The managers felt that if in the future there was a sufficient number of children in the neighbour- hood requiring a higher education, they would like to see it provided. That was a ery reasonable com- promise Ind the committee were not committed to any- thing because if there was a read for higher education at that end of the town it was the duty of the corn- pittee to supply it whether they liked it or not. Councillor BA TON seconded.

Mr. JOLLEY moved an amendment that the resolution be not approved, He said the managers of Fairfield School appeared to him to have shown that their previous attitude was not justified. The amalgama- tion had been delayed quite unnecessarily, and the public bad been repeatedly assured that the delay was on educational g•ounds. It was perfectly clear now that that was an unjustifiable contention. An attempt was being made to induce the Education Authority to enter into a bargain which ho ventured to say, they ought not to enter into. Canon Stevenson had properly pointed out that if a higher or secondary school were needed at that end of the town it would be the duty of the Education Authority to provide it. He (Mr. Jolley) suggested that the Education Authority had never shirked its responsibility in that direction, and if such accommodation were necessary, they would no doubt provide it. It ought not to be a matter of bargaining. He considered this an nnworthy bargain and one that could not ho carried out, and would bean undignified procedure and unworthy of an authority of that hind to enter into it. If there was to be a real bargain, let them know the conditions and not join in a policy which appeared like an attempt to throw dust into somebody's eyes. It looked as if the managers had been trying to throw dust into the eyes partly of that authority and partly of persons outside' and that the School Management Committee suggested in return for that compliment that they (the Committee) should now throw dust into the eyes of the managers. (Laughter.) Mr. PARKER seconded. He said he did not object strongly to the first part of the proposal of the managers. While he thought it would be much hotter if the whole of the education was directly under that authority, he respected the feelings and desires of those who wished to have a special school to give their denominational teaching, but if they were going to commit themselves to a proposal of that sort, it should be accompanied by the idea that there should be within reasonable distance another ' school to which those parents who did not desire special denominational teaching given could send their children. (Hear, hear). It would be far better, instead of the committee tying their hands as proposed, if the matter was left as it was by law, and those interested in denominational teach- ing made their arrangement with the central authority. He objected strongly to free access to the school, and if they agreed to this resolution approving the proposal be did not see how they could object to freedom of access to all schools. It would be better if the ques- tion of freedom of access were debated on a straight issue and not come in on a side issue. With regard to the last clause—the granting to all religious denomina- tions of right of access to the school to give and to superintend the giving by the permanent staff i denominational religious instruction n school hours to those children whose, parents required it—that went very much further than freadom of access. The com- mittee were asked to endorse a proposal that the per- manent staff should be placed under the authority of the denominationalists who would control the religious teaching. The committee had happily steered clear of religious difficulties because they had mot as citizens whether they were parsons or not. Supposing the Rev. James Davies, the Rev. John Yonge and the Rev. Father Wilson entered the school and had charge of the permanent staff. as to what denominational teaching they should give. The whole thing was ridicul- ous and would undoubtedly lead to confusion. He did not think that in a citizens' school it was well to allow the separation of the children into little flocks for religious teaching. There was also the further question of whether the committee had power to ask the teachers to give any religious instruction other than what was generally known as Cowper-Temple teaching.

Mr. BRADSHAW supported the amendment, because he did not think privileges should be extended to the managers of Penfield School which were denied to managers of other schools. If the school minagement committee had made out a case for amalgamation they should insist upon its being carried into effect.. The Rev. JOHN YONGE said he entirely agreed with Mr. Parker, and he trusted the committee would carry the amendment.

Alderman Bor.ToN said this resolution appeared to be committing the Corporation to an expenditure at some future time without the consent of the Council. lie thought they were going a bit too far. If a qew school was wanted at once let them build it ; if not, let the future look after itself.

'Canon STEVENSON said the recommendation com- mended itself almost if not quite unanimously to the members present at the last meeting of the Sghool Management Committee who represented various shades of opinion. They felt the controversy had been going on so long that it was not in the interests of Education that it should 1 e continued. It was a very difficult question, but it seemed to him at all events the second proposal of the managers was one there ought to he no difficulty in accepting. He gathered that the managers would be content if the committee accepted the proposal or the alternative. It was open to accept either. What was the a lternative? When there was a need for a higher or secondary school at that end of thetown ithe committee would be asked to build one near the Cemetery. There might he a religious diffi- culty. The proposition was that right of access should be given to various denominations to give religious teaching in the school. Then camo the sentence Mr. Parker referred to which he was bound to say, on second reading, was a little awkwardly worded. Ile did not think Mr. Parker's :nterpretation of it was the interpretation intended, and was not the interpretation in the minds of the School Management Sub-committee. They understood it meant that a person of a denomi- nation might go into the school either for giving religious instruction to the children of his own denouti-

Some Impressions by a Casual Visitor.

At present these are the difficulties which' have stayed the soaring lubitions of all but the Farmans,Paulhans,Lathams Bleriots of the world. 47 miles on Wednesday. In the distance on the left of the photograph are the hangars an - The figure leaning on a walking stick and wearing a black " bowler" is the

glance upward, and the white flag of a blameless flying day was discerned fluttering from the mast- head of the Tower in a breeze which spoke eloquently of possibilities. A crowded tramcar conveys us—at summer prices—to the South Shore, where a change of vehicles is necessitated to reach the Aviation Ground. The momentary pause gives time for another glance aloft and there amid general eplama- lions of delight is discerned the red flag denoting "flying begun." In another moment we have joined the dense stream of vehicles and foot-passengers who make black the long straight course of the road to Squire's G ate, now an historical spot. Soon we are in sight ' of the aerodrome, a vast enclosure surrounded by miles of canvas raised to a height of at least eight feet in order that "Peeping Tom" may not get a surreptitious view of I the flying. Of course, when the daring al iator 1 'sses the clouds he can be seen both inside and outside the enclosure, but on the present occasion no flying man was visible, crane our necks as eagerly as we may. Once within the gr3und however, and flying became a real understandable thing to all. There was the intrepid Farman circling the arena at a height of twenty feet and a steady speed of thirty miles an hour on one of those huge 'bi-planes which have been likened unflatteringly to a hen-cote in a storm. He comes sailing calmly through the clear air with an absence of fuss which strikes one as the most remark- able thing of all. There is the loud crackle of a motor revolving at immense speed, and at a distance reminding one unmistakably of the far-away threshing machine in the soft stillness of a summer's day. The aeroplane can be followed the eye for the whole of its almost three miles circular course, and as it swings past every 3 mins. 50 secs. we are guiltily conscious of the feeling that we are not as impressed as the circumstances demand. The crowd, drawn out thinly' along the rails of the huge ring.breaksdinto desultory cheering and handclapping, but there !V an absence of that excitement and enthusiasm that one would expect to follow on such a unique spectacle. How easily we get accustomed to the most remarkable things, and what an undemonstra- tive race we are! We have never seen a malt fly before, but we are not going to indulge in bysteria to convince the flying man that he is a genius of the first water. Oh no! Flying looks easy, and we shall doubtless achieve flight ourselves some day. Let us be decorous, dignified, and not too easily surrrised. So we arc decorous, with true British stolidity. Meanwhile Farman completes circuit after

circuit with almost too mu* ease. We should all like him better if he soared more after the style of. the birds whose realm he has invaded with so much success. The great fault of the biplane, from a spectacular point of view, is its inability to look graceful or perform feats which appeal to the imagination., Its motion fails entirely to suggest flight. The opposite is the case with the monoplanes of the Latham and Bleriot type, which are shaped like creatur.es of the air and perform evolutions which conform more with the popular notion of flying. But of these, more anon. Just when the crowd was beginning to think that Farman had worn a groove for himself and was going on all afternoon he suddenly ceased to fly. The machine glided gently to earth, the now visible and slowing screw propeller scattered a puddle in the grass and the record British flight was over. Farman had been in the air 1 hour, 32 mins., 1.6 4-5 secs., and had covered in official measurements, 47 miles, 1181, yards. Leaping stiffly to . the earth, the Frenchman was the recipient of a salvo of cheering which he well deserved. We learned later that the. cold wind, now freshening considerably, had cramped the muscles of the aviator, and he was obliged to descend or lose control of the machine. Hardly had Farman retired than Rougier was up. His bi-plane, of a very similar type to the other, rose beautifully from the ground after amu over the spongy turf of about 60 yards. There was a roar of applause and delight for Rougier has the reputation of being a high- flier. He at once rose majestically, and in a minute the October sun was glistening on- the white canvas of his planes a good hundred feet from the ground. Thrice Rougier went round the course in an attempt on the slowest circuit prize, which .is one not easily won. If a fairly rapid pace is not maintained the machine would come down, and the slowest Rougier was able to command worked out at 27 miles per hoar. He was successful in" putting up" the best figure for slow circuit, viz., 4mins. 22 2-ssecs., during the second of his three laps, and there was general regret when he gracefully glided to earth. All the time the wind was getting stronger, and when Paulhan essayed a flight he was sood compelled to return. This was alittle after 3 p.m. and.it really appeared as •if flying was over for the day. There had been keen speculation as to ss hether Latham would have another try. Though he has done nothing at Blackpool beyond charging a golf bunker, he seemed to be the man of the hour. People were agog to see the man who nearly"flew"the Channel,

===== 0002601_19091023_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
Manchester Guard;an. I on the .right are the stands, Blackpool Tower being just visible near the centre. Grand Duke Michael of Russia.

and twice " landed " in the waters. We took the opportunity of a lull in the proceedings to stroll towards the hangars—what a new vocabulary aviation is providing us with !—where the machines are housed. There were a dozen or more of these capacious sheds s tanning side Ly side, each with the aviator's name painted large over the lintel, and each with a huge sailcloth curtain for a door. Here one could study the different types of machines to advantage. There was Coun- cillor Parkinson's Bleriot, sulking like Achilles in his tent. Never yet has it left the ground, but the owner and his fellow-townsmen are hopeful, aye hopeful! Further on we found the indomitable Britisher, A. V. Roe, working desperately hard at his fragile-looking triplane. Each day he brings it out, "gets up steam," and scutters along the ground at a proniising pace. But ' ne'er a rise into the ethereal blue! There is another British-built machine sleep- ing 'neath the combined and fearsome name of Greese- Dederich. It, too, has yet to make its debut in the upper, air. There is Singer, too, of our own nationality, possessing the machine but not the flying capacity. Ttuly, we English are only infants in aviation, and have much to learn from the inventive, mercurial Frenchman. No doubt we shall assimilate all he can teach us, and then show him the way to build and sell flying-machines. We started last with motor- cars and are now finishing an easy first. A little further on and Latham was discovered superintending a cmall, voluble army of French mechanics who were putting the finishing touches to his damaged propeller blade. Latham is a slight, dark, young man with an air of modesty and nonchalance. He smokes eternal cigarettes and does not get excited 'about nothing. While we were in front of Latham's hangar rale of the stewards appeared as escort to a small party, the chief figurf s in which we immediately recognised as the Grand Duke Michael of Russia and his wife, Countess Torby. Latham was presented and conducted the distinguished guests round his famous Antoinette, a lively conversation in English proceeding the while. Nearly an hour elapsed in which there -,was no more flying. limy people went away, but the patient 'ones got due reward. Notwithstanding a breeze officially posted up as being 28 point something miles per hour, the crowd hoped aga inst hope, and there was a great cheer when Latham's machine was seen emerging from the hangar in tow of a lusty cart-horse. The humble "gee-gee" is not entirely superseded even yet! This one rose to the occasion, and with the air of a ecnqueror

ugged the quiegeent Antoinette the full length of the north side of the course._ Then the. machine was turned, head on to the wind. The engine started with a roar, the propeller blades flashed into and out of sight; Latham mounted, and after a run of only about 50 yards along the ground the machine soared gracefully into the air. and flew in the teeo-1 of the wind for half a mile, or more. It was only a qualifying flight to cross the line, but it sufficed to demonstrate the beauty and grace of the monoplane. More like a huge dragon fly than any- thibg else with wings, Latham's macbine appeared to establish a.kinship to the flying things of nature. I Soon it was back in the hangar, and the dismantling ' of the signal pole announced that the day's programme was concluded. Then followed a great exodus, and it was no easy thing to find a place on one of the innumerable vehicles, which crawled humbly—but safely—along a man-made - roal -to Blackpool, tea, and trains. As dusk devmded, the illuminations sprlng into. being, and - the town loolid its gayest. Talbot Squsre was a transformation scene in glowing electric bulbs, and anything finer than the sparkling front of the Town Hall we have never had the pleasure to see. Our day ended with a stliking demonstration of flying by the noted L. and N. W. The twenty miles betty( m Blackpool and Preston were actually covered in 105 minutes. Farman was not in it7—(the train).---Lucky Farman! . E.V.W.

More Revenue Wanted—Suggested Million Pennies Fund.

LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

ELECTORAL REFORM BILL. Tories Propose 'Election Day Closing

of Public Houses.

There was a good attendance at the weekly meeting of the Warrington Parliamentary Debating Society which was held in the Assembly Room at the Conserva- tive Club on Tuesday evening. The Attorney General (Mr. F. G. Taylor) introduced a Bill for the amendment of the law relating to the Parliamentary franchise. The extension of the franchise embodied in the Bill applies to every male adult who shall (a) Be liable in the event of war to be called on for active service with the Army, Navy, or Territorial Forces. (b) Be a graduate of any British University. (c) Be separately assessed for the payment of Income Tax. Other proposals include the closing of public-houses on election days, the reform of the House of Lords,and various provisions to compel electors to use their votes or be struck off the register. The ATTORNEY-GENERAL 10 the Course Of a lucid explanation, said there was no desire to reduce the qualifications for the vote, but there were, on the other -hand, a number of iodividuals who ought to be placed upon the voting lists of the realm. First,those who were liable to be called upon to the service of this country should have some voice in the control of ,the destinies of the country, for which they might be called upon to lay down their lives. Then there were a number of persons who, by their earnestness in acquiring know- ledge which might be beneficial to the country, should also be entitled to the franchise. Their very zealous- ness might at the present time prevent them from having an opportunity for qualifying for the exercise of the vote. There were also a number who had to pay taxes direct, but yet had no property qualification. They ought to have the vote. There was the subject of women's suffrage. This the Government proposed to leave to the House to decide without any control from the Front Bench and they would be prepared to consider an amendment on the subject. An amendment might also be made to remove the disqualification at present attaching to the man who removed from one constituency to another. With regard to the clause closing during the hours of poll all houses for the sale of intoxicating liquor, the Conservative party hai been taunted that they dare not bring in such a clause. The Conservatives had, however, nothing to fear, and judging from Os personal experience, the opposite side had better look to themselves. (Hear, hear.) While such a claws was necessar, in the case of a number of voters who were liable to temptation, he admitted that it offered a rather studied insult to a large proportion of the intelligent voters. Alluding to the clause forbidding the soliciting of votes after the issue of the writ and the provision of conveyances to the poll, the Attorney-General said there were some individuals who would vote for the candidate who took them to the poll in the most comfortable manner aud Who might have other inducements to offer. The Government, therefore, proposed a clause prohibiting any conve3ance to the poll except in case of physical incepacity. Canvassing had become an intolerable nuisanca and led to an immense amount of lying. As to the provision that every Parliamentary voter who failed to vote should be struck off the Parliamentary register; if a voter did not think it worth while to go to the poll at one election be should be penalized at the next election by not having the vote. As a general election had a disturbing influence upon the trade of the country, it was desirable that all the contests should be got through as quickly as possible. Therefore, the Bill proposed that all Borough eleetions should be held on one day and the County elections on another day. This would also do away with the influence which the first day's elections had upon the subsequent elections as was markedly shown in the last two general elections. cWith regard to the re-adjustment of Parliamentary representation, which had become a glaring matter for investigation, this would affect the country of Ireland considerably. It was estimated that on January Ist, 1908, the male population of England and Wales was 91 milliens, Scotland 11 millions, and Ireland rather under 1 f millions. This would reduce the representa- tion of Ireland in the House of Commons to somewhere

===== 0002601_19091023_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
mate power should dwell in the civil hands. They regarded expert opinion as a good servant but a bad master and the votes of the army and navy might have to decide the issue of peace and war at a great crisis. To take away a man's vote because he did not use it was entirely opposed to the spirit of our laws. They should educate the people so that they would be less indifferent, hut he did not agree ,with penalising them in the manner suggested. He considered the hereditary principle in the House of Lords was very objectionable; but the proposals of the Government were such that the Prime Minister for the time he:ng might " pack" the House of Lords, with the knowledge that in a certain number of years the evil would cure itself. Mr. H. E. HAWTHORNE (E. Belfast, C.) 'defended plural voting on the ground that property should carry a Noting qualifization. Proceeding to speak of the advance of Socialistic ideas, Mr. Hawthorne exclaimed: "We don't want the red rag." Mr. ENGLAND caused some anansemeat by interjecting "1 rise to a point of order. Tbe hon. member talks about not having the red rag, but he is wearing one in his coat." Mr. F. G. TAYLOR: I protest r.gtirst the pro- ceedings being msde into a farce. Mr. HAWTHORNE, continuing,pointed out that during the first fifty years after th 3 passing of the Act of Union the population of Ireland increased by two millions. The Act protected the Irish from themselves. Before it was adopted they were always engaging in tribal wars. Mr. Daltnvsitinr. (Davenport, L.) said he was dis- appointed that his party had seen fit to move the rejection of the Bill as a whole, because there were several features with which he agreed. It was a splendid thing that such a WU should emanate from the C inservative benches, and he claimed that the Liberal party had been the educators. He congratu- lated the Government on having so far ridded them- selves of the trammels and cobwebs of Conservatism as to introduce such a measure. The proposal to give the vote to the army and navy was fraught with great danger. The environment and discipline in the army did not tend to the free exercise of opinion on matters of moment in an election. The closing of public houses on election day would probably mean the death knell of Conservatism in .many districas. It would be the first piece of legislation to break the coalition between Conservatism and the "trade," but it would spell disaster to the Conservative party. Ha considered it was only just that Ireland should be represented on the same basis as the English constituencies. about 80. The matter undoubtedly concerned the Irish members and he would be interested in hearing that they had say upon the matter. He ventured to say that the decrease of the population was very largely due to the agitation which was csaried on by the seople who were sitting in the House of Commons at the present time, who were returned by the Irish Land League. TIRED, NERVOUS WOMEN have a Marvellous P rnedy in DR. CASSELL'S TABLETS.

COMPENSATION AWARD REDUCED.

Allegations Against Trades Union Society.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday before Deputy Judge Sellars an application was made by the Vulcan Foundry, Co. Ltd., for the diminution or termination of compensation paid to Edwin Mather, 92, Rydal-street, Wargrave, Earlestown, in respect of an accident to his eye in October 1907. Mr. Adshead Elliott (barrister) represented the applicants and Sir William Cobbett the respondent. It was stated by Mr. Elliott that the accident was caused by a chip flying into the respondents left eye which deprived him of his sight for some time. A sum of 19s. 3d. was paid weekly without any suggestion that the mm should make any effort to retura to his work. About ten months afterwards the eearetary of the company saw the respondent and another man, who, singular to say, had met with an exactly similar injury in December 1907. When asked whether it was not time they were looking for work they replied that there were others to please besides themselves which significant remark was taken to refer to the trades unions. Nothing was said furtber at the time but in May 1909 the secretary again saw both men when Mather asked if he could have his own job back. The secretary asked if be would be right to start the next day to which a reply was made that they wanted some time to make up their minds. The Vulcan Foundry did not want men of that sort in their employ. They said rightly or wrongly thit the men ha i bee n malingering for some time. They had not the slightest doubt the two men would have found some job if they had been allowed. There was a strong suspicion that they were not allowed. The Works Secretary, (Arthur Doggett) gave evidence respecting the interviews with the men. Mr. Cobbett contended that the respondent was not able to earn the wages as previous to the accident. He had offered to try his old work, but had been refused and was unable to fled employment elsewhere. Medical evidence was given by Dr. Emays ;tones, senior surgeon at the Royal Eye Hospital, Manchester, who st-ttei that on the 7th of that month he nude a careful examination of the man Mather. He found that the sight of the left eye was completely destroyed from what might have been the result of PII accident. The condition of the right eye was fair, but distinctly below normal. He was of the opinion that the defendant was incapable .of working at his occupation as a rivetter. _ _

_ The respondent, Edward Mather, in reply to Mr. Elliott, denied that he was acting under the instruc- tions of the Boilermakers Union in not asking for wotk for eighteen months. His Honour reduced the amount to 12s. weekly—ln the case of John Shams, 200, Wargrave-rcad, town, a similar award was made.

Sequel to Cycle Collision.

A claim for damages for injuries, sustained as a result of a cycle collision, was brought before Deputy Judge Sellars at the County Court on Thursday by James Firth, 26, Wilkinson-street, against .Joseph Wood, newspaper publisher, Warrington-road, Poulton. —Mr. Browne, who appeared for the plaintiff, stated that on July 15th the plaintiff was riding his bicycle along ..llarshhouse-lane and when near the railway bridge he was run into by the defendant Wood who approached on his .cycle from the opposite direction on the wrong side of the road. A collision occurred in which the plaintiff was knocked off his machine and rendered unconscious. He was taken into the 'house of a Mrs. Graham and afterwards con- veyed home. Defendant accompanied Mr. Firth to his home and obtained his address. In consequence of the accident plaintiff was away from work for three weeks and his wife also had to give up her work at the factory to attend to her husband.7—Evidence in support of the plaintiff's story was given by Mr. and Mrs. Graham, 76, Marshhouse-lane and the plaintiff's wife.—Mr. T. S. Steel, who appeared for the defendant, contended that the plaintiff WEIS at fault in being on the wrong side of the road. His Honour gave judg- ment for the defendant With,costs.

A BREAD TRANSACTION.

James Harding; baker and provision dealer, sued Mr. and Mrs. Wood, 1, Chester-road, at the Warrington County Court on Thursday for £26 I6s. 51.: for goods supplied. Mr. T. S. Steel who appeared for, plaintiff said the male defendant carried on -business in Warrington and had a running account frimi 1904. In November 1904 he was compelled to 'go away and the business was assigned. to his wife. The bread had been personally delivered by Mr. Harding. Payments had been made from time to time by Mrs. Wood who-asked for time as she would endeavour to pay the balance. The amount claimed was 26 16s. 5d., and. the defendant had paid into court £1 12s. 6'd. and Ss. costs saying that it satisfied the plaintiff's claim.—Mr. Browne, for ,the defendant, said his client paid cash for all the breai received from Mr. Harding. There was nothing owing until a little before Christmas when a boy came with the bread. Every item was then entered into a book. An account was rendered amounting to ;W. Mrs. Woods paid a cheque which left a balance of £1 12s. 6d.—His Honour held that the Plaintiff had made out his claim. It was monstrous to think that Mr. Harding should commit a deliberate fraud on the defendant. He therefore gave an award for the plaintiff for the amount claimed.

the DUKE of MONTROSE were able to show that this ought not to be d6ne, he would find his power of coarse invective an unnecessary qualificatip Tor the platform of a Conserva- tive Club.

Mr. R. Pierpoint MANNERS! probably prides himself on belonging t 9 the "gentlemanly party," and usually one of the last faults we should think of charging him with is that of discourtesy. But he over- stepped the, bounds of both decency and courtesy at one stride on Monday evening when he sneered at the Chancellor of the Exchequer as "a solicitor from an obscure town," and stated boldly that he was receiv- ing "at least £4,500 a year more than he was worthy of." Now these are the last taunts of the jaundiced unscrupulous politician and we should as soon have contemplated Mr. Pierpoint's return to the House of Commons as his use of so despicable a mode of party warfare. "Manners make the man," and whatever the consequences we should have thought Mr. Pierpoint would never have stooped so low as to need reminding of the fact.

This important ques- LD SCHOOL tion is at present FEES BE engaging the attention ABOLISHED,? of the Warrington Education Committee. On Monday evening Mr. Jolley introduced a resolution that no fees be charged in any of the elementary schools from January Ist next, but time did not allow of its discussion and it will form the subject of a special meeting of the authority on Monday even- ing next. Theoretically most people are in favour of the abolition of school fees and, as Mr. Jolley said, it does look anomalous that while the Council schools are entirely free, the non-provided schools should continue to charge fees. The question however is not so simple as it appears on the surface. We have it on good authority that the abolition of fees would mean the addition of a, twopenny rate and it would cer- tainly involve the replacement of some schools. Already the educational rate is equal to the rate of other large towns. If twopence is to be added for school fees, the white elephant on Grace's pottery to be paid for, and three or four additional schools to be built, we shall not envy the Chairman of the .Finance Committee his position. The solution of the whole difficulty is for the State to take over the entire cost of Educa- tion. There is now almost a consensus of opinion that Education is a national concern and as such we believe it should be paid for by the nation. It is said that no local control could be expected, if the State pro- vided all the funds. Yet we have an example in the town at the present moment of local control of State funds. The State finds the entire cost of Old Age Pensions but the other administration devolves upon the local committee who, to no small extent control the fund. ,

The Conservative party A FEEBLE PROTEST. in Warrington indulged in the luxury of a Budget protest meeting in Parr Hall on Monday evening. There was a large audience—carefully canvassed and admitted by ticket—and the Budget Was belaboured by the speakers in a whole-hearted fashion. That it suffered at all in the process is open to question. The Budget has roused all the worst passions of Toryism, of monopoly, and of vested interests. The very fact that in its main provisions it readjusts the burden of taxation so that wealth shall bear a share more in proportion with the load under which the workers have staggered for centuries is enough to condemn it utterly in the eyes of those who think imperially and pay parochially. Their dread bogey is Socialism, so the Budget is described as Socialistic. It seems to us that the Tories are injuring their own cause by such tactics. The people like the Budget, as the meetings on both sides all over the country have conclusively proved, and to hear it called Socialism merely lessens the dread of many cautious reformers in Socialism itself. "If this is Socialism" they think and say, " let us have more of it." - The " protest " at Warrington will not carry much weight. Though the Conserva- tive party had filled the hall with their friends they were afraid to put a resolution of protest to the meeting and very judiciously did without. Could anything be more conclusive that the Budget• has gained the day? Even the Budget Protest League has put up the shutters, and -written itself down a political failure. The speaker who mainly voiced the " protest " on Monday was the Right Hon. J. H. Campbell, K.C., M.P. (ex-Irish Attorney-General) about whom it is worth while noting that at Warrington' he sat under the presidency af a Brewer and deplored the "cruel injustice and vindictiveness of the licensing duties." At Islington on Wednesday he was beating the drum in similar style on behalf of the Allied Brewery Trades Association, so that he may be as legitimately dubbed "the Brewers' Advocate" as Mr. Ure is rightly known as "the Budget Advocate." Mr. Campbell was at his alliterative best in the congenial London company. He likened the Budget licensing duties to "the finance of the freebooter and the vindictiveness of the vendetta." The audience was mcrie reliable than the one at Warrington, and we learn that a resolution of protest was actually put and carried.

The Tariff Reformers A TARIFF REFORM have at last given up ' CLIMB DOWN. trying to prove that the effect of the French and German duties on wheat is not, to increase the price of bread. It has been a gallant struggle, which has never been more than a forlorn hope. The sponge is now thrown up in an artidle in the "Monthly Notes on Tariff Reform" published by the Tariff Reform League, which deals with French and German wheat prices. The &Aide admits (the italics are our own) the following significant conclusions: "That wheat and bread have not become dearer . but cheaper, although higher duties have been imposed, is true, but that the German and French duties have, on the whole, had the effect of increasing the price of wheat in those countries is also true. It would indeed be strange if the French and German duties had not had this effect, as they were deliberately imposed in order to produce it. The German and French wheat duties are not intended to produce revenue, nor are they intended to bepaid by the foreigner. They are not intended as ordinary tariff duties, but as practically prohibitive duties imposed for the purpose of making agriculture attractive and remunerative, not so much to enrich agriculturists but as being on the whole the most economical means of keeping in the country and on the land the requisite number of men required for military purposes. 7:lie high French and German ditties' do, there- fore, raise the price of \corn in these countries. They are intended to do so. But what, we put it to our "Free Traders," has the effect of these duties in France and Germany to do with Mr. Chamberlain's proposal to put a duty of 2s. per quarter on all foreign wheat imported in Great Britain? The only possible answer is, "Nothing at all ! " The :things are in entirely different

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. " The Prince and the Beggar Maid."

Dramas from the pen of Mr. Walter Howard hay° often been produced with great acceptance JJI Warrington and this week local playgoers have tile opportunity of seeing Mr. Howard himself in his most recent success " The Prince hod the. Beggar Maid: Mr. Howard is not only a gifted playwright but a powerful actor and his inclusion in the cast is suffi- cient to invest a dramatic enterprise with more thall usual interest. Con.equently large audiences havie been the rule at the • Royal Court Theatre anu enthusiasm has run high. "The Prince and the Beggar maid" was first produced at the Lyceuillci London, where it had a remarkable " sen off and the honotft of a visit from 11 Majesty the Queen. With an attractive mi"- tary setting and an absorbingly original plot, the, play is conceived in a delightful romantic vein awl there are exciting situations which hold the audience spell-bound. Mr. Howard impersonates the dashillg Prince Olaf with consummate skill, and whether.' the tender love passages with the Princess MOlliCa or in that superb dramatic Scene when he defieg his ambitious brother Hildred's soldiery, YI is equally clever and convincing. From t,ile earliest stages of the play he wins tILe favour of the audience and repeatedly comes in for rounds of cheering. Miss Stella Campbell is a will-- some Princess Monica, her portrayal hein,gi characterised by rare depth of feeling. A sPecia, word of praise is due to Mr. Lionel Gadsden for ID° remarkably realistic study of Michael the Hunchback-. Mr. Robert Noble is a capital Captain Hector; 31r; Mayor Cooke makes the most of the unenviable par' of Prince Hildred ; and Mr. Victor Rae displays F„°11- siderable talent as Michael's dumb servant. company is a capable one all round and the mounting is excellent. THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, OCTOBER 23RD, MO9.

It must be admitted SOME DUCAL that the Dukes have LANGUAGE. come to the front in a really surprising way. We hear very little about them in the House of Lords as a rule. The DUKE of NORTHUMBERLAND has a certain §et of narrow opinions which lead him to make frequent 'excursions into the debates, and the DUKE of BEDFORD speaks with ability O n army questions. But the Dukes in general either do not' attend the House or are silent when they take their Places. Now, suddenly, however, they have almost all of them become vocal,—not, indeed, in the House of Lords itself, but on Platforms in the country. The latest instance in point is the DUKE OF MONTROSE, who is entitled to sit among the Peers as EARL GRAHAM. We do not remember to have heard of him in politics up to the Present time. But the Budget has roused the fighting spirit in him, as it has roused it in the DUKE OF PORTLAND, and the DUKE OF DEVONSHIRE, and the DUKE OF NORFOLK, and so many others. He has, it appears, been hiding a fine talent for invective which as Mr. DISRAELI said, is a great 'ornament of debate. "But," the late Tory leader was careful to add, "invective is not argument." It is characteristic of the DUKE OF MONTROSE and the other Dukes, that they use invective as if they considered argument to be unnecessary. In this they do not differ from the most humble of their fellow-countrymen. "Don't argue with him; hit him ! " is an exclamation that may frequently be heard at country fairs when two men are obviously spoiling for a fight. This, it is evident, is _ the Ducal scheme of controversy. The DUKE OF MONTROSE remarked to a meeting at Glasgow that the Midget is being forced upon the country by a section of poli- tical hypocrites; 'and that Mr. LLOYD GEORGE Posed like a rampaging Sociklist lion at New- castle, but was like a sucking dove in the Rouse of Commons. Mr.WINSTON CHURCHILL Was given a worse character. To use the elegant Ducal language, The goes one better." One would imagine, said the DUKE OF MONT- ROSE, that he took his daily chop and glass • of ale in company with Mr.KIER-HARDIE and Nlr. VICTOR GRAYSON ; whereas he actually clines at the best houses, sometimes "squeez- ing himself even into the exclusive society of Lansdowne House.". And now we know what ducal manners are, and what is the style of language favoured by the highest class of the British aristocracy. It must be conceded that the DUKE of MONTROSE is one of our enlighteners, Anyone would know, from the manner of his speech, that he had been to a -public school. The historic reply to the man who said to another, "You're no gentleman," was You're no judge." We do not consider the DUKE of MONTROSE to be a judge of the 'behaviour of Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE in the Rouse of Commons ; but .we can say, with confidence, that the DUKE himself would not venture to employ in the House of Lords the .coarse sort of language and metaphor and insinuation that he considered good enough -for the Conservative Club at Glasgow. That sort of thing is more fitted for race- -courses than for assemblies such as either -the House of Commons or the House of Lords. There was nothing said by Mr. LLOYD- GEORGE at Newcastle that he might not .repeat anywhere. It is true, however, that, in the House of Commons, he has exhibited a -tact, a suavity, and a general habit of Con- ciliation which have been of the highest value in expediting business. Does the DUKE of MONTROSE impute these virtues to him as crimes.? His crime is, of dourse, that he framed a 'Budget; one object of which is tO place more fairly the burden of taxation -(1/1 the,backs that are most able to bear it. If

But the fact remains that Mr. Chamber- lain's 2s. per quarter on all foreign wheat imported into this country would lessen competition and most certainly result in increased prices at home. One would follow the other as surely as night follows the day.

Lti3TES AND NOTIONS.

"You can tax wealth or you can tax wages "—that is Mr. Churchill's concise summary of the Budget dilemma. The Chancellor has already decided,and it is easy to surmise which alternative the general public will adopt. * * *

The "man in the street," perhaps, needs no other answer to the contention that the Budget is attacking capital, than Mr. Winston Churchill's plain statement: was shown yesterday "—Mr. Churchill was speaking on Saturday, the 16th—" a list of thirty-eight leading industrial and com- mercial securities in which there has been a substantial ackance since the Budget was introduced. It is curious that amongst these securities which have advanced I notice the Alliance Assurance Company,chairman Lord Rothschild." * *

The Siamese are a simple but a most ingenious people. The manager of the Siam Electric Company has just issued a circular letter to the consumers of current, which reads:

Sir, for the case that your electric light should fail we beg to send you enclosed a postcard, which please send us at once when you find your light out. The company will then send you another postcard. To the more exacting Western mind the possibility of supplying the place of the light that fails with a match and "another post- card" would nsvei have occurred. ** * • _ _

The employment- figures for September show a gratifying improvement. It is not large, but its significance will be seen from the following comparison:

TRADE UNION UNEMPOYMENT PERCENTAGE. August. 'September. 1908 1909 -

All the principal iudustries show an improve- ment as compared with a year ago except coal-mining, in which the state of employ- ment is about the same. The figures, indeed, are one of the many indications which go to show that trade and industry are slowly, but surely, recovering.

*- w Mr. C. E. Parker ha*s intervened to good purpose in the Bermondsey election. The district is one of the centres of the leather trade, dnd the Tariff Reformers have been arguing that the importation of leather does unnecessery damage to Bermondsey, and that the trade could be revived by taxing the foreigner. The question appears to have aroused widespread interest,and on Monday, Mr. Hughes, the Liberal candidate, produced a letter he had received from Mr. Parker, who wrote that the land monopoly and the high price of land in London were largely responsible for the stagnant condition of the leather industry in Bermondsey, and enclosing particulars showing that cheaper land in his district had resulted in the development of the industry until now four yards mentioned put through 420,000 hides per annum, though the oldest began opera- tions only 25 years ago and the youngest twelve years ago.

Another sample of Mr. Lloyd George's "vitriolic vituperation"! Speaking at a Welsh dinner on Wednesday, when Mr. Balfour was the guest of the evening, the Chancellor of the Exchequer made the follow- ing uncalled-for remarks at the expense of the Tory leader : To-night we are assembled to pay honour to one of the most distinguished men of the day. (Cheers.) I will not speak of his interest in science and art, his ,literary interest; or his great literary attainments. I know him better as a statesman and as a member of the House of Commons. I say that the House of Commons has a special Pride in Mr. Balfour--;cheers),--a pride in his great gifts, a pride in his courage, a pride in his chivalrous bearing. (Hear, hear). There is no debate in the Hoise of Commops in which he takes part which he does not nrieli by his contribution. He is one of the greatest assets of the House. (Hear, hear.) Will the Tory papers be able to contain themselves after this last attack? * * *

The Mayor caused a sensation at the Swimming Gala last Friday night by a personal attack on Alderman Bolton, chair- man of the Baths Committee. The gist of his -remarks was that the .baths were a disgrace, and "as long as Alderman Bolton was chairman _ . . . . they would have'

nothing done for them." There was some truth in what His Worship said, but his remarks were ill-timed aud uttered in the wrong ,place. If he had said as much in open Council and in the presence of Alderman Bolton, no objection would have been raised. But the attack of last Friday evening was to say the least, injudicious. The Council will have before it on Tuesday evening„the usual vote of thanks to the retiring Mayor for his " courtesy and impartality," and it seems to be the general opinion that Alderman Forshaw has jeopardised the chances of that unanimous vote, without which the resolu- tion is valueless. * * *

The Government's splendid legislative record was eloquently reviewed by Mr. Winston Churchill in a speech at Dundee on Tuesday. Those who have been disposed to belittle the work of the past -few years -would db well to study Mr. Churchill's summary. It shows what a great deal has been done to improve the prospects of many humble sections of the community, and to protect the child life of the nation as well as to brighten the declining days of the aged. "No one, looking back," said Mr. Churchill, " over the last four years has any reason to feel ashamed of the record of his Majesty's Govern- ment. (Cheers) Although from causes beyond our control we have not been able satisfactorily to settle the problems of education and licensing, no lover of progress who surveys the Statute Book as it stands to-night, and contrasts that Statute Book with the condition in 1905, need feel that it has laboured in vain. All classes of our great community have benefited. The trades unionist, as he surveys his organisation freed from the injury done it by previous judicial decisions ; the miner, whose cage brings him to the surface of the ground after eight hours of labour (cheers)— ; the aged pensioner when he goes to the post office to draw his weekly pension —(cheers)—; the Irish Catholic, who sees the rdnges of a university career thrown open to his sons ; the child, wlio is protected in the street and in his home; the peasant, who finds it easier to acquire a small parcel of land which he needs for his individual exertions; the youthful offender in prison and saved from the prison—(cheers) ; the citizen, who takes his seat on the county bench, not debarred by 'a property qualification; the servant who is injured in domestic service— all these bear witness to the workings of an active and tireless humanitarian effort." (Cheers.)

Before a large and enthusiastic audience, the members of the operatic section of the Warrington Musical Society gave their concluding performance of "The Pirates of Penzance" at the Parr Hall on Saturday evening. The week's experience on the boards,had inspired all concerned with greater con- fidence and the final representation of the opera was highly finished and pleasing in every respect. Every- thing went with delightful crispness and animation and hearty encores were the order of the evening.. At the close the principals were called to the front 'and warmly cheered, and they were alsolhe recipients 'of Many handsome floral and otlicr presents. Mr. F. Fieldhouse, the Pirate King, was p'resented with an appropriate memento in the form of a two-masted toy ship, and much men iment was caused when a large pie, labelled" From the cook" was +handed to Mr. j. W. Bibby, the sergeant of police. ' Mr. F. H. Crossley, the conductor, and Mr. Foden Williams, the coach and stage manager, appeared on the stage and were accorded a great ovation. The opera company joined hands and sang" Auld Lang Syne " and the proceedings closed with the National .Anthem. From the musical and artistic points of view this year's opera enterprise is entitled to rank worthily with the six which have preceded it. The only cause for regret is , that%the multitude of counter-attractions caused a falling off in public patronage.

SEQUEL TO DOG BITES.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Deputy Judge Sellars, Ernest Smith, a postman, brought an action- against Joseph Smith, assistant-overseer, residing at Sankey, to recover damages for a dog bite. Mr. T. E. Knowles appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. A. Browne for the defendant. Mr. Knowles said that on the evening of May 27th, abont 8.40, the plaintiff was riding a postal carrier tricycle from Warrington to Penketh, and as he was riding past the defendant's house a do-,b ran out and bit him on the leg. Plaintiff did nothing to provoke the animal. His Honour : The point is whether the dog is a savage one. _- Plaintiff gave evidence in support of Mr. Knowles' statement, and said that he was off work for 19- days, and the shock of the bite had upset his nerves. At the present time be suffered inconvenience from the bite, and he had lost 6s. Ss. The doctor's bill was 7s. 6d. Mr. Browne • We paid that. In reply to ;fr. Browne, plaintiff said the wound was not a slight graze as the wound bled. Mr. Smith had been very kind to him. Thomas Kenyon Taker, another postman, spoke to the defendant's dog having flown at him. Samuel Atherton also said that the dog haol attacked him. and he had complained to the defendant's daughter Mr. Browne, for the defence, said the dog had never bitten anyone else. lany people called at the house daily to see Mr. Smith on business and there never bad been any complaint about the dog before. After hearing the evidence of the defendant his Honour gave judgment for the defendant with costs. Fifteen shillings was the award given to John Lowe, a groom residing in Kendrick-street, Warrington by Deputy-Judge Sellars at the Warrington County Court, on Thursday against William Haslam, farmer, Burton- wood as a result of a dog bite. Mr. E. A. White appeared for the plaintiff awl Mr. Browne for the defendant. Lowe bad eccasion to visit the farm of Mr. Haslarn when the latter's dog flew at him and bit him on the lea. He was medically attended at the Infirmary but was able to continue his work. The claim was for the damages to his clothing. •

DEATH OF MR. EDWARD BRAIDE.

The news has been received with deep regret in Warrington of the death of Mr. Edward Braide,late dis- trict goods manager of the London and North-Western Railway Company, who passed away at the residence of his son at Watford, (Hertford) on Tuesday morning. The deceased gentleman, who was , seventy-two years of age, was one of the most familiar figures in Warrington up and district to six months ago when, shortly after the death of his wife, he went to live with his son, Mr. J. E. Braide,at Watford. Despite his advanced years he was most active and enjoyed wonderfully good health. About a fortnight ago he was quite unexpectedly seized with a stroke and never recovered, death ensuing as stated abave. 11r.Braide, who was a native of Huddersfield, had spent almost the whole of his life in the service of the L. and N.W. Railway Company. His first important position was at Liverpool where he was engaged in the accountant's department,remaining there until he became chief. Next he was transferred to the office of the general manag.r at Euston to assist in some complica'ed work which had arisen, and after being there for thret or four years he came to Warring- ton as district goods manager ill 1874. He 'held that important post until 1903 when he was superannuated by the company. His many sterling qualities and not least his conscientious devotion to duty gained the confidence and esteem alike of the company and of the staff under his control, and on his retirement the high regard entertained for him by the whole of the dis- trict staff, found expression in the presentation of a handsome silver salver and tea and coffee service. Mr. Braide took no prominent part in public affairs. He was a devoted Churchman and for many years was a regular worshipper at St. Paul's Church. He had also acted as superintendent of the, Sunday School. In politics he was a Conservative. Of late years Mr. Braide had suffered several heavy bereavements, one son—Dr. G. Braide —passing away in 1908.

THE LATE MR. E. BRAIDE.

WORKINGMEN'S MISSION,BANK QUAY The League of Help.

The first annual meeting in connection with the League of Help associated with this Mission was held on Tuesday evening in the schoolroom. There was a good attendance of members and friends. , Mr. George Dale, President of the League, took the chair, being supported on the platform by Captain Crosfield, Rev. W. Bracecamp, Mr. Rowlands (the Secretary of the Warrington Citizens' League of Help) and Mr. Hough. After hymn and prayer, the Secretary, Mr. T. Edmunds, read the first annual report, which showed that a good work had been done: When they commenced to organize the League about 11 months ago it was to them quite a new undertaking, and they had to feel their way into it. A basis was agreed upon by which funds could be raised to carry on the League of Help work. This was done by means of monthly subscriptions from the six sections into which the League was divided. The six sections were as follows :—Men's Class, Women's Class, Young Men's Class, Young Women's Class, Society Class and the Sunday School Teachers. A secretary was appointed for each section to receive from any member the amount they decided to give, from as low as id. per month upwards, so as not to make it a burden upon anyone. Donations were also received from a few sympathizing friends who were not members of the League. The total income for the 10 months ending September had been £4l 12s. 4d,; total expenditure in actual -relief, £36 Bs. 21d. ; printing and stationery, us. 2d.; leaving a balance in hand of £4 12s. 111 d. Number of cases relieved in December, 15: January, 18; February, 21; March, 22; April, J 4; May, 6 • June, 7; July, 8; August, ; September, 8; October, 6. The funds subscribed by the six sections of the League of Help amounted to £32 is. 10d.- The remainder was received as follows :—Captain Crcsfield, ; Mrs. Crosfield, Colwyn Bay, £1 10s. ; Mr. P. Carter, Frodsham, £1 12s. 6d. ; Mr. Potts, Liverpool, 11. •' Mr. Medley, Ts.; and Mr. Caird, I,a. The funds had been dis- tributed either by a note for provisions or money. The amounts granted varied from is. 6d. to 55., and in one or two special cases 10s. Many cases had been assisted with food and clothing by individual members of the League,and certainly £l5 worth of pro- visions and clothing had been distributed in this way. The Leaue had worked in conjunction with,. the Labour Bureau during last winter, investigating cases. and giving information to the Bureau-. In smile cases employment had teen obtained for men otit of work. No question of denomination or creed had entered into the work of the League. No matter what the oreed, creed or no creed, they had worked to endeavour to sweeten the lives, to bring warmth and comfort to the homes of the people in the neighbourhood. They had been amply repaid by seeing the people, whom they had tided over the dark and difficult days, able to fight their way through their difficulties. The CHAIRMAN, in his remarks, stated that the mem- bers of the League had recognized the importance of not pauperizing the people whom they assisted. The Object had been rather to put those in. need in the way of helping themselves. But there were many cases where there had been such extreme poverty and hopelessness that it was found to be absolutely necessary to grant help in the way of food, money or clothing. It was a distressing fact that in Warrington, with its thousands of Christians, such a condition of things should be possible. Perhaps some might say that the municipal authorities ought to look after such cases, but whatever the responsibility of the municipal authorities might be, the Christian Churches could not possibly shirk the duty which was very plainly set before them. • The singing of hymns and prayers were of no avail unless supplemented with active efforts to help their fellow men. He was glad to extend a hearty welcome to Mr. Rom-buds, (Applawe.l Mr. ROWLANDS said he hoped that befor6 16ng tEI would "gee a network of similar organisations such as theirs right throughout tile town. He likened the League of Help workers to the pious bridge builders of olden times, who worked with a holy zeal in the building of their bridges to increase the means of communication and intercourse between man and man. So the League.of Help was the means of bridging the gulf between us and our fellow men. Mr. BRACECANIP congratulated the League on the example which it had set to the whole of Warrington. While others had, been talking they had got to work, and had already done a good deal of work. The movement in connection with the Warrington Citizens' League of Help had been well launched. All sorts of difficulties had cropped up which they had never anticlpated;4mt they were being ,gradually overcome, and very shortly he expected to see it in working order.

• Captain CROSFIELD exprosed his great pleasure at being present and listening to the excellent report from tne Secretary. lie was ,delighted to recognize the spirit that had prompted the workers of the Mission to undertake the League of Help, and he believed that the fruits of the work carried on by that League would be good. • A vote of thanks to the speakers, moved by Mr. T. DALE, and seconded by Mr. Homi, concluded the meeting, the Chairman being thanked for his services in the chair.
