ROLLER SKATING

Special Attractions at the Local Rinks.

-The seaside carnival held at the above .rink on Thursday evening was a pronounced success. The rink presented quite a gay festive appearance, being very prettily and tastefully decorated for the occasion with baskets of flowers, gaily coloured bunting, streamers and flags. The costumes of the ladies were very pretty and effective. One saw rolling round on the little wheels coons, pierrots, donkey drives, aviator, fortune tellers, midshipmen, boy scouts, seaside sultans, and "Kelly from the 1.0. M.." whose humorous attire and antics whilst rolling round preveds,great fun. The originality and smartest of the costumes made the task of the judges no light one, and finally the .awards were as follows :—Smartest costume : 1, Miss E. Owen; 2, Miss West; consolation prize, Miss A. Fairclough ; special prize, Miss Holbrook, fortune teller; original costume, gentlemen, Mr. Fearnley; two step competition, Master and Miss Hughes ; special prize two step, Miss Smith and Mr., Frodsham. The management of the Palatine Rink have made important reductions in prices. They have arranged a number of attractions in the near future, particulars of which will be found on our front page. Thursday was also a special night at the handsome new Enipire Rink, a series of interesting competitions being entered into with great zest. The management adopted the novel idea of allowing the public to judge by ballot and it proved completely successful. About 350 skaters pursued their Fascinating pastime and over fifty took part in the contests. The prizes were awarded to the following :—Lady and gent partners (graceful competition), 1, Miss Dolly Jones and Mr. Walton; 2, Miss Ormsby and Mr. W. H. Wadsworth; gentlemen's two step competition, 1, Mr. R. Atkinson; 2, Mr. W. H. Wadsworth; ladies' competition (graceful),-1, Miss Dolly Jones; 2,,Miss Ormsby. The competitions were open to residents within five miles of Warrington. Mr. Boothroyd, managing director, distributed the prizes which were supplied by Mr. J. Finlaysen. The recipients were warmly cheered. There is to be a carnival next Thursday, when vain- able prizes will be offered.

SIX MONTHS FOR SHOP BREAKING.

At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday Frank Tracey, living with his mother in Catherine-street, was charged with breaking and entering the lock up shop of William Shield, pawnbroker, of 139, Bewsey-road and stealing goods to the value of £8 17s. 6d.—Mr. T. S. Steel appeared for prisoner and pleaded guilty.—Tbe Chief Constable stated that at a quarter to three on Monday morning the 11th inst. Mr. Wright, butcher, residing next doot, heard a noise in Mr. Shield's shop. On looking through the shop window, he saw the prisoner crouching down. Mr. Shield was sent for and on unlocking the door the prisoner bolted.— Mr. Shield gave chase and with the assistance of the night watchman, succeeded in capturing him, and banded him over to P.C. Burrows. On the way to the Police Station, prisoner threw a skeleton key away which on tieing tried in a door at the rear of Mr. Shield's shop, opened it. On arriving at the Police Station be was searched and the property found in his possession. There was a further charge preferred against prisoner of breaking and entering the shop of William Tinton, next door to Mr. Shield's, but as there was not sufficient evidence, the charge would be with- drawn.—Mr. Steel said that prisoner was only 20 years of age and joined the army two years ago. Whilst in the army he was charged with being concerned in a robbery from the officer's mess, and he was given 12 months under the Borstal system. He had only been released a little over a month. He was discharged from the army with ignominy. Since he came out he had only been in casugl employment and had been drinking. Concluding he hoped the Bench would deal with prisoner leniently so that on being liberated he would be able to help to support his widowed mother.—A sentence of six months was passed.—Prisoner left the dock smiling.—Joseph Tracey (prisoner's brother), was also charged mith breaking into the same shop on the same date.—Mr. Steel, who appeared on his behalf, pleaded that he might be discharged, as be was the only support of his mother.—Tbe Chief Constable stated that as the evidence was rather scanty, the police had no objection to Mr. Steel's proposals.—Tracey was accordingly bound over until December 21st, to come up for judgment if called upon. _

===== 0002601_19091023_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
PALACE AND lIIPPODROME.

There was a very bright show this week at the Palace and Hippodrome, though there is natural dis- appointment at the absence of Winifred Ward, well known and billed as "the finmaccnlate boy." The Buster Browns are a superior .troupe of American vocalists and dancers, whose work is cordially appre- ciated. Their combined cinematograph dance at the end was very clever and effective. Hayes and Merritt are the worlds's champion bounding trampolinists— whatever that means. The turn is not at all mysterious however. Hayes and Merritt are a couple of servant acrobats with a bounding net beneath them, and by the aid of which they perform some remarkable feats of strength and dexterity. Charles North is an actor-vocalist of high merit, and his rendering of a couple of Kipling's Barrack Room Ballads was worthy of a more cultivatad andience. " Gunga Dheen " was especially fine and well merited the hearty applause which followed. Karr and Rooney appear as comedians in scarlet. They are one of the smartest pairs at cross-talking seen here for some time and keep the audience in a ripple of laughter. Harry Cotton a comedian of the eccentric type. makes a capital impression with his imitation of "One of the old Guard." Bessie Foy, vocalist and raconteur, has a good reception and Vent and Earl contrive to amuse with their musical comedy sketch. The Palace Pictures enchain the attention of the audience to the end.

The Examiner, Saturday, October 23, 1909

" Theie's Milly Wright," I suggested. " Yes, dear—frowsy, baby-faced Milly! But I do wish she wouldn't wear her hair short. It does make her look so awfully—but--er—didn't you and Milly used to be rather—well, rather nice _to each other?"

"Oh, bosh ! "

" No', it isn't bosh ! "

What's 'off? " "Your niceness with Milly."

Yes, and invited all the good-looking, _ _

" I would at least have shown my ap. preciation of the beautiful, which is perhaps more than you would do had you the selec,tion of the girls. I never knew a man like you, who sees attractions in girls whom nobody else would ever think of-"

I suppose that's the reason I took such a fancy to you—out of pity, you know, just be- cause nobody .else 7- .

"You stupid, give over. Your sarcasm is very poor _stuff."

I don't know how many times we quarrelled during those three hours Mary and I were mak- ing out the list. 1 believe I called her a con- ceited little creature, and I'm quite sure she called me an unfeeling brute. Twice the en- gagement was broken off for ever and for ever. and .we regretted we had met one another; and then Mary threw a cushion at me to &hew her spite, and then I 'kissed .her just to shew I was master; and then—well, we. did manage to get the list made out towards midnight. If I hadn't been engaged to Mary it's likely she would have treated me with respect. But simply because I had honoured her by .asking ber to change the name of Morrison to Franklin she thought she possessed the right of ordering me about like a footman, and bullying me and telling me my faults in the presence of other people. She was always polite to other men; that is why I think she must have been very much__in love with me.

"I'll want you to come. and help in the after- noon," she said, as the "At Home" hove in sight.

-"I won't be any good. I can't arrange cakes on plates, and if you want any scrubbing done I think the servants might once in a way-" 'Oh! but the servants can't more the piano, and it's no good getting in a man when you are about. Besides, Eve hired a lot of palms and plants, and I'm going to have them nicely ar- ranged in my particular I;ttle den, the one that gives you such a magnificent view of the Dis- trict Railway, and I'm going to have a num- ber of nice little fairy lamps-" "Oh, that will be distinctly original," I mut- tered.

"Will it, Mr. Quiz? Well, if it won't be original it'll be pretty." So I had to go along to the flat and work like a nigger. Of course, I was blamed as a cumber- some nuisance who was only in the way, and who would have conferred a distinct service by not helping at all. I moved the piano too near the 'wall; my arrangement of the plants was simply preposterous, for it could be seen with half an eye, said Mary, that the proper place for the settee was behind the greenery and not in front of it. Else what would be the good of an alcove at all if it didn't shut off the view? I did not know, and as I began to have a head- ache I did not inquire. I had to go to the greengrocer man and cart off some fruit, a thing which made me ashamed, for I knew every woman in the street was restraining a laugh till she got past me. ' With a yell Mary re.membered she had for- gotten to order any milk. Off I had to go to the dairy in the next street to tell them to be sure to send round an extra quart. I suggested to Mary that perhaps she would like me to bring it back with me in a can, and she -was heartless enough to reply that I mit,rht bring it in a paper bag if I liked. "And now you can run off home and drew, but be sure to be back early before the guests begin to arrive," she said..

" But aren't you going to give me a cup of tea after all this work?" I gasped. "No, I can't, for all the cups and saucers we have are laid out in the dining-room ready for to-night." "But I'm simply dying—" "Well, you can call at an A. B. C. place, and get alive again. Off you go!" i • I call that base ingratitude. A man would never put up. with it even from his wife. But when a man is only engaged, he inisees the kind, flattering attention of young ladies, who think he ought to be engaged, and he does not re- ceive the respect wkich, as a married man, he will be entitled to some day. Of course, the "At Home" was a failure. That, however, didn't prevent everybody, in- cluding myself, saying it was most enjoyable. Mary was radiant. I felt inclined to tell her she was charming, but I would only have been snubbed for my pains. The red gown—l think she said it was a sort of a neutral shade—suited her so well, and the poppy among her unruly black hair was delightful. I went early. As no guests had arrived, I wanted to take Mary in my arms and say—but, oh dear no! I was forbidden on pain of eternal penalties to oome within a yard of her. She hadn't put on her dress simply to be crushed! And then the big sleeves always betrayed one, didn't they? It looks so bad when you wander in from the conservatory and one of the sleeves of the lady's dress is pressed flat against the shoulder. It makes people laugh more than when you eater a ballroom with your trousers turned up. The Framptons were the first to arrive, the four of them, and that awful Tithe' amongst them, and their mother, a pompous lady who, after inspecting me through her glasses, said, in a most audible tone: "So that's him, is it? What a plain young man!" Mary couldn't openly resent the remark. She claimed a monopoly in advertising my defici- encies, and when anybody poached on her pre- serves in this direction she always became par- ticularly loving, and called me a stupid old dear whatever a stupid old dear may be. Mrs. Frampton kept most of her girls round her all the evening, for which I was thankful. The younger Miss Frampton was given to levity, and her mother always reprimanded, her by remarking: "My de-ar, do remember!" Then the younger Miss Frampton would smirk and say: "Oh, ma!" All the women came first., which was a bore. Some of the girls I knew; many of them I didn't. They all looked at me as a sort of wonderful animal, because I had the audacity of being en- gaged to Mary Morrison. I let Mary manage the introductions. "Torn, dear, let me introduce you to Miss Teddington—Miss Teddington, Mr. Tom Frank- lin! Now be agreeable, Tom"; and then she bustled off as there was congestion in the hall. I'm never, good at talking to ladies I've just met, yet I managed to say the usual things one says on such occasions—that the stairs were rather a big climb; that I hadn't been to tho theatre for some time lately; no, I didn't think the Academy was up to the average this year; oh! yes, I had heard it was an interesting book, but I hadn't had time to read it y'Ft; it was quite likely I would be going north in Septem- ber; oh, indeed, yes, the weather had been terrible; well, that depends on whether Surrey was up to its usual form, but I didn't know much about cricket; no, not till the late autumn; we hadn't decided where we would go yet, but I left it to Mary; it would be a very quiet affair—only half-a-dozen friends or so; well, of course, we w6uld both be charmed to see Miss Teddington; and so on. The drawing-room was already overflowing, but I was thankful when a few men arrived. I picked one for myself, and then introduced the others to some fair ones. "Oh, Tom dear, here's Major Flanigan whom we knew in Dublin," said Mary, all excitement, introducing me to a red-faced, white-haired, stiff-backed old fellow. "Delighted! delighted, I'm sure," he ex- claimed, nearly twisting my arm off at the elbow. "And so you are going to marry Mary." I do so dislike people calling ladies by their Christian names in the presence of other people. "I think you'll get on all right with the Major, Toni dear," said the little mistress of the household, as she passed me, leading long Sam Sadler to the two Misses Blake, who were thin and scraggy, and consequently wore low-necked dresses. "Oh, yea, Mary, thank yoq," I answered back. "Dear, dear! and I knew her father," said the Major. "You never knew her father?" "No, I never had that honour." "What a dev—what a beggar of a boy he was, to be sure! Ah! we did have some times in Cork, but that was long before you or Mary were thought of. Why, I remember the day she was born. What a soaking we did have! Ha, Ha! And so you're going to marry her, you young dev—dog, you!" WHEN MARY WAS "AT HOME."

BY JOIN FOSTER FRASER, Author of "When Mary and I were Wed," "A Woman of Fire," "Round the World on a Wheel," "The Real Siberia," itc.

When the world had been duly apprised that Mary and I were an engaged couple, and after people had finished wondering • what we were to live on, and observations had ceased at after- noon teas that Mary had simply thrown herself away, and that I was ruining all my prosper& at the Bar, Mary got it into her little head so, would be nice to give an "At Home." Her object was to put me on exhibition before all her girl friends and make them jealous. "You- see, dear," she explained, when I raised an objection, "aunt is quite willing, and it needn't cost much. Of course, our flat is a very tiny place, but that won't make any difference. I don't think we need invite more than fifty friends."

"Where on earth are you going to put them? There's hardly 4room for you and your aunt to turn round, and that's why. you and I always occupy the same easy-chair, isn't it—just to save room?" ,

Oh, but you don't understand," she said, in a flutter. "It's wonderful how many people you can cram into a flat." "You cram them flat sometimes, I know. You haven't enough chairs to accommodate a third of them."

" What do you want chairs for, silly, at an 'At Home '? ' Aren't there the walls in the hall where the men can lean against, and can't the women sort of camp out on the—on the floor? It's often done."

"Oh, yes, I know it's done," I acknowledged, with painful recollections. "But can you afford it? You know it'll be awfully expensive!"

"Now, you know nothing whatever about it," she said, slowly, beating time to her words with a finger within an inch of my face. "You see, all we need get is some sponge-cake--" "Oh, sponge-cake!" I sighed in dismay. "You .needn't say 'Oh, sponge-cake!' for ye.. always eat a horrid lot of it wsenever you have the chance. Why, at Mrs. Merryweather's last Thursday, the way you ate sponge-cake was per- fectly-" "And what else?"

"We'll have to get a few bottles of lemonade. Then we can buy some cheap claret—l don't think any of the girls would like strong claret, do you?—and we can mix it up, and then there will be claret cup. And then we can get a pound or so of little cakes—nice little—little—" 'lndigestible things, made of butter and covered with sugar stuff, and with a greengage stuck in .the middle. I know them."

"I wish you wouldn't interrupt. The way you constantly interrupt me is most rude. You used to sit and never say a word. If you're so shock- ingly impolite while we're engaged, I wonder what you'll be like hen-" "But, my dear-" "Well, nuisance, what is it?" And she stuck her little hands against her little waist, and looked up at me with an air of indignant resignation:

"I was just going to say, dear, that you can't expect my men friends to put up with—with sponge-cake and diluted claret." "Oh, indeed! I suppose you would like me to get in brandy and soda, a few dozen bottles, eh, so that the place will be like a—a• taproom? Of course, auntie would allow that, wouldn't she? Perhaps you would like me to get in a lot of pipes and cigars, so that they might fill the house with horrid—"

"I do think they might like a—a cigarette the course of the evening, for, you know, a man can hardly be expected to—" "Well, Tom Franklin, Esquire, you needn't think you're going to bring a lot of your nasty, guzzling, smoky, smelling men here. If they want to smoke they can go out on the staircase. Yes, they'd better go out on the staircase. It'll be such fun hearing the two old maids over us coughing." I knew all the fellows would cut me dead if I invited them to an "At Home" where there was nothing decent to drink. And I knew they, would cut me if I didn't invite them. So what was I to do?

It's a terribly dangerous thing for it. man to have anything to do with an "At Home." He's sure to give mortal offence to a dozen folks. I know Sadler always gets out of the difficulty at his wife's affairs by surreptitiously sidling up to a guest, and while looking at an ornament, whis- pering. "Take anything?" "Rather, but just a drop ! " "Then come with me." and the pair glide through the throng, pretending to look in- nocent. Sadler takes them into a cupboard, about three feet square, called a larder, and they always come out wiping their lips. I felt, therefore, I would have to do some- thing of the kind, by bribing the servant, and thus save the reputation of my future wife and her aunt.

So I said, "Oh. yes, very well; it's your 'At Home,' not mine."

"Oh. I'm quite willing to take all responsi- bility for any catastrophes that may occur!" And Mary glared at me and played a tattoo on the piano-top, and muttered something about somebody always taking a delight in throwing cold water on any little scheme she had taken the trouble to think out. I apologised, and offered to help her to make out the invitations.

"I suppose we don't want any old fogeys?" she inquired. "No, I think not." I replied; "except, per- haps, my aunt. She'll be able to talk with your aunt, and they can get in a corner and tell each other about our naughty little ways when we were juct so high." "Oh. yes; that will be delightful." • "Well, now, who else?" "Of course, I'll have to invite the From/Iton girls?" "What! those frights? I exclaimed. _ _ _ _ _

"Well, they are - frights I admit, dear." said Mary. apologetically; "but their father's awfully rich, and they have just asked in a mild sort of way What I should like them to give me when I—that is, when we are--"

"Oh, well. then, -you'd better," I said, dejec- tedly; "but I do hope that Ethel girl will have the mumps or a prior engagement, so that she will have to be—what is it?—exceedingly sorry that she cannot avail herself of the pleasure of— and so on. Did you ever see such a waist?" "It isn't a waster," and Mary giggled. I frowned. "My dear girt" I said, "I wish you wouldn't try to be funny. I suppose you intended that for a pun. Well, I can only tell you it was a most atrocious—" "All tight, all right, old disagreeable; anu who next?" _

"Well, there's Bert Wallis." d Oh, you mean the Mr. Wallis I met at the Gilmours, with beautiful hair and soft,' dreamy eyes. Oh, yes, do let us ask him. He is simply lovely." _ _

" Oh--er, by the way, I think—er—that Wallis has an engagement for that evening." "How sad, and I did so want to—but are you sure he has an engagement?" _

--.l`lle- didn't exactly tell me he had an engage- ment, but er—l gathered—that is, I had the um- pre e "Well, invite him any vvay I " said Mary, Isvenll—, w decisively. "No. I don't think so." "But why?" "Well, I don't think he's exactly the sort of fellow you would/care to—"

my dear boy. I know what_ it is. You're jealous!"

"No I'm not!" - "Yes you are!" "Jealous, indeed, of a curly-haired, effetnin. ate, wax—er--" "Well, he's courteous to ladies, and that's more than can be said of somebody I know," and Mary sat bolt upright in her chair. _ . . .

"f tell you w.hai. it is, if you invite Wallis, Polly Blythe will also be invited." "Oh, the wind blows that way, does it. I always thought you liked Polly Blythe, the pale-faced, fluffy-headed thing!" • "No I don't!" I declared.

"Oh. you—you fibber! If you're sp fond oi her, why don't you go to her now? I'm sure she'd be very glad to have you, and I know I'd be—very glad to—to—" "No, it's no good crying, Mary," I said, de- termined, to be firm.

"I'm not—l'm not crying," she sobbed, wiping her eyes with a four-inch square piece of thin muslin she called a handkerchief._

"Then why don't you put that thing away ! " "Because I've got a speck or something in my eye." "1 daresay. Well, look here, we'll not invite either Wallis or Polly Blythe, and then we're quits." " Right! " shouted Mary, like a woman who has scalped someone, and then she dashed her pen into the ink-pot. The ink flew up and her fingers were all dirty. She was about to wipe them on her handkerchief, but I gave a gasp, and she desisted. Then she turned up the bottom of her frock and wiped them cu the lin- ing. That was so like a woman. "It won't shew there, dear," . she said, with. a smile, just as though I appreciated her. device. She put her elbows on the table, stuck the pen in her mouth, looked across at me, and tried to appear businesslike. Next? "

"Well," I said, "you'd better rush in all vour girl friends. It will be your only onnor- tunny, tor in the Suture rau needn't expect our house is going to be a place of resort for the ;onsumption of chocolate."

WALKERS' V. WRITECROSS.—WaIker's Brewery billiard team played their first match (since the club's inauguration) on Thursday with the Whitecross Social Club team as their opponents. The teams consisted of 10 players aside, and resulted ima very easy win for the Whitecross team who in a great many games showed marked superiority over Walkers' players. For visitors J. Davies, T. Corless, and T. Gleave plaied exceedingly well and gave their respective opponents good beatings. For the home team W. Allen, T. Heacock and F. Rogers played well, and in two cases just managed to win by narrow margins. The games terminated about 10.30, and everybody expressed the opinion that they had spent a very enjoyable evening. WAYSIDE NOTES. A Gilbertian Sub-Committee.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.] The Sites and Buildings Sub-Committee of the Education Committee has succeeded in carrying its resolution to build the largest school in Warrington on the site of Graces pottery. This we are told is "the finest site in Warrington for a school." Ye gods! to what extraordinary hands are the health_ and education of our children committed. Already this Comic Opera Committee has decided to build a large school on the verge of Sankey brook. It is said that political prisoners in Spain are sometimes, with refined cruelty, placed in dungeons through which there is an open sewer. The Govern- ment of course is soon relieved of its prisoner by typhoid. But in Warrington more than 1,000 innocent children are to spend their school lives on the very verge of a sluggish stream loaded with filth. These children at any rate cannot be guilty of politics, unless it be—terrible thought—that warned by the folly, the self-seeking, the blindness of the city fathers the young rascals will grow up to be Socialists. If so, well, open sewers are useful;

* * * But this latest scheme out-Herod's-Heroa.. The site itself is one of the many bad bargains • the Council-has made. It is said that any- one might have had the land as a gift if he would pay the ground rent upon it. Whether this is so or no, the Education Committee will be saddled with a heavy ground rent for all time. The advocates of the site lay stress upon the fact that the. _ land already belongs to the town. As a matter of fact it would pay the town to give the land away. At any rate the Council will be glad enough to push its exceedingly bad bargain upon the Education Committee, adding of course to the Education rate, and giving the Councillors the opportunity of lamenting with copious tears the growing cost of Education. * * *

How many of our readers have been in this neighbourhood . recently we wonder? It would be exceedingly educative to take a tram ride to the terminus at Longford, get out, and wait for the next tram back. This would be quite long enough, and about as much as the ordinary citizen could stand without restoratives. The district is a dreary swamp, in many cases a considerable depth below the road- way. Black, filthy mud, semi-solid water, render building impossible. These Solons of the Sites and Buildings Committee see in imagination a rapid development- of building taking place around the new school similar to the growth around the Beamont School. " Their's not to reason why,:' etc.

But the atmosphere! Rich is not the word. Why not build a School of Science in this neighbourhood? The students would have unparalleled opportunities for analysis of vapours; mal-odorous gases and floating organic matter. The Longford Depot, the Gas Works, other similar and no doubt necessary processes tend to give the -whole atmosphere of this district a strength, a • pungency, a power of penetration, which once inhaled becomes a life-long memory. We believe the inhabitants of this district have again and again petitioned the Health Committee for an abate- ment of the various nuisances which render life almost unbearable. They state that the atmosphere is so ba,d, that they can neither "eat, work, nor sleep. It is on this site then, and in this atmosphere that the largest school is to be built. To it are to be brought the children in the new district of the , Whitecross Ward,—the Lovely-lane district. How long will White- cross Ward maintain its enviable prominence of having the lowest death rate if 600 of its children are to spend six hours each day this asphyxiating atmosphere? It is also to relieve the new district around the Beamont School. It cannot supply the needs of any but a comparatively small number of childrep in its immediate neighbourhood, for there is only a small number of children there !

* * We have already one school named after the Chairman of the Sites and Buildings Committee and cannot very well have another. We venture however, to make. a suggestion. This School should be named "Bolton's Folly."

_ _ A large number of people do one of these three foolish things. That's \-hy they never regain their former health. starvation is a common practice among pcople who have weak stomachs, but does it sound reasonable to you to deprive the body of its nourishment every time you wish to give the stomach a rest? The Examiner, Saturday, October 23, 1909.

D

I

MR. SPENCER LEIGH HUGHES (Liberal).

DR. SALTER (Labour).

PICTURES OF THE WEEK.

MR. J. DUMPHREYS (Unionist).

The Budget Battle in Bermondsey i 3 in full swing. The Budget League, the Budget Protest League, the Tariff Reform League, the Labour Party, Socialists, and two sections of Suffragettes are all on the ground, and the fight is accordingly being carried on with great vigour by all parties Mr. Spencer Leigh Hughes, who is a well-known London journal- ist-, s the Liberal Candidate. He is a clever platform speaker, and has been aptly termed the licensed jester of the Liberal Party. The Unionist and Tariff Reform cause is in the hands of Mr. John Dumphreys, who is a Bermondsey man. As a candidate for Parlia- ment, the Borough Council, 'lie London County Council, and the old London School Board he has fought twenty-three contests and won sevenl.cen. He eppcsed Mr. Joseph Chamberlain in Birmingham twenty-four years ago. Dr. Salter, the Socialist Candidate, is known in Bermondsey as "the poor man's friend." He is a staunch temperance advocate and a Quaker.

LONDON SOCIALISTS AND FERRER'S EXECUTION.

SCOTLAND YARD MEN TAKING NOTES.

Ten thousand people gathered round the Lions in Trafalgar square on Sunday to protest against the execution of Senor Ferrer. The speakers indulged in language of the most incendiary kind, and one banner which was unfurled during Mr. Cunninghame Graham's speech was seized I y the police. At the close of the speeches the demonstrators marched on the Spanish Embassy, where they came into contact with the police. Fortunaiely no serious damage was done, and no arrests were made in connection whit the disturbances. Our photograph shows shorthand writers from Scotland Yard taking notes. The speaker is Mr. J. O'Grady, M.P. _ _ .

===== 0002601_19091023_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEW WESLEY DISCOVERIES.

John Wesley's journal is an English classic. Said Mr. Augustine•Birrell : "If you want to get into the eighteenth century, to feel its pulse throb beneath your finger,be content to leave the letters of Horace Walpole unturned, nay, even deny yourself your annual reading of Boswell and ride up and down the 'country with the greatest force of the 18th century in England. No man lived nearer the centre than John Wesley. You cannot cut him out of your national life. No single figure influenced so many minds, no single voice touched so many hearts. No other man did such a life's work for England. Happily for us, his journals remain,and from them we can learn better than from any- where else what manner of man he was, and the character of the times during which he lived and moved and had his being,"

• And again: "Read the journal, which is a book full of plots and plays and novels, which quivers with life, and is crammed full of character."

Well known to men of letters and diligently read by Methodists, it is only in recent years that it has become a popular book. In an abridged form it has been sold in hundreds of

"Then in regard to the love affair," said Mr. Curnock, with a smile, "the theory has always been that the girl was a flightly, superficial creature. Now, on Wesley's own showing in an exquisitely beautiful account of it which he wrote a year afterwards, we find that it was precisely the reverse. She was very young, a pupil of his,highly intelli- gent, in good circumstances, and having property. If he had married her she would have given him -an established colonial position of great influence apart from his own personal power. But she broke off everything suddenly by. marrying another man. Wesley himself was, as he frankly expl4ined to her, under a vow not to marry so long as he had any chance of carrying the Gospel to the Indians. He was clearly very much in love with her." "Don't you feel," Mr. Curnock was asked, "that this new matter gives us a more human Wesley and less of 'the tireless rider ' ? "

"Oh, very much more human," he replied, "and, at the same time, more remarkable. The story of the love affair gives us an inti- mate view of his emotional nature, and the new matter in relation to Oxford shows the extraordinary charm and winsomeness of his character." The first volume of the new Journals as edited by Mr. Curnock will ar

JOHN WESLEY'S CYPHER.

A page from John Wesley's private journal written in the cypher which was so difficult of decipherment. The figures on the left mark the hours of the day, beginning with 12 midnight and showing how Wesley spent his time from 5 a.m., the hour of rising. The figures on the right record how many minutes in each hour were given to private devotion.

===== 0002601_19091023_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
thousands and several firms of publishers are giving it to the world in varied and dainty forms. Though a masterpiece of prose the journal seems to have been written without a thought for literary style. The passages are daily entries jotted down each evening with more or less care and intended at first as a private record. There came a day, however, when Wesley under provocation from a detractor decided to print the chief passages from his journal and began to issue it in shilling parts. These passages form the "Journal" as we have had it until now. Now comes the stimulating news that we are to have a transcription of the unpublished parts, together with many private and care- fully-made notes dating from Wesleys younger days. It comes as a shock and a reproof to us to learn that Wesley could find time not only for a daily entry in his journal, but for notes made every hour and contain- ing the results of his self-examinations. His days begin at 4 am. and end at 9 or 10 p.m. and every movement is accounted for. Frequently a few moments in each hour are devoted to prayer, and two or three times a day he looks into his heart and writes down his findings thereupon.

In addition to all this he wrote for his mother an account of his daily doings not omitting the most trivial of follies into which he was sometimes beguiled. We have had the privilege of inspecting some of these records, too sacred to be given to the world, and inspiring one with strange wonder. The thought of John Wesley, Oxford Under- graduate, thus laying bare his heart to his , mother is impressive indeed. We doubt if literature could show a parallel. The most trifling flirtations, the saying of words that might flavour of indiscretion, are recorded with terrible fidelity and it is a very human John Wesley that stands revealed. These things will not be given to the public, but we are to have very soon a transcription of some much more interesting matter. The private journals of Wesley's early manhood are written in Byrom's shorthand and cypher, and to the task of deciphering these Mr. Nehemiah Curnock has given several years of painstaking effort. The shorthand notes easily yielded, but the cypher, which was of Wesley's own making, has presented serious difficulty. Poring over the difficulties and almost ready to give them up, Mr. Curnock at last found the solution as Howe found the secret of making the sewing machine. By the unconscious action of the brain the clue was discovered and the result revealed in a dream.

We enjoyed the other day a chat with the veteran antiquarian to whose patience we owe these most interesting discoveries. Wesley is a man who, as Mr. Birrell says, cannot be left out of our history. He changed his century and has changed every genera- tion since. It is of the deepest interest therefore that we should see the man in the making, and these hourly records, these absolutely honest, penetrating, comments upon his own heart and motives show us the man as nothing else could do. The Wesley who stirred England " was a man already made. Hard trials and disappointments, varied experiences across the seas had quickened his senses and tempered his heart, and it is the record of these told in his own words that are to be read in the private journals. His early love story will now be known for the first time, and Mr. Curnock gives the emphatic opinion that the biography of his early life will have to be re-written. "As regards Georgia" said Mr. Curnock "on the most sober estimate, the new matter gives us a new and absolutely authentic view, not simply of John Wesley himself, but of Oglethorpe, the founder of the 'colony, and of the development of the colony itself.' _ _ _

" We discover -now that John Wesley was not only intimate with, but was confidential adviser and secretary of Oglethorpe, thus eaving his mark on Colonial government.

month and then Wesley's numberless admirers will burn midnight oil. A.M.

===== 0002601_19091023_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. JOHN AMBULANCE BRIGADE.

Crosfield Shield Won by Northwich

The "Crosfield Shield" presented by Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd. 11 years ago to the North- Western District of the St. John Ambulance Brigade was competed for on Saturday afternoon in Croslield's Dining Hall.—The objects of the Brigade is certainly a worthy one, rand the services it has rendered are very much appreciated. Eight teams entered, and their performances reflected great credit npon them. The teams were as follows :—Bolton, Northwich, Stock- port, Whitetleld, Barrow-in-Furness, Oldham and Ratcliffe. The judges were : Dr. Pita (viva voce) and District Chief Superintendent Garnett (practical test.) The result was declared as follows (viva voce and practical test respectively) : Northwich, 81, 85 ; Bolton, 76, 82 : Barrow-in-Furness, 75, 78 : Oldham. 77, 68 ; Whitetield, 62, 65 ; Ratcliffe, 62, 68 ; and Stockport, 53, 77. Mr. J. J. CRO SFIELD presented the shield, and amongst those preseht were Colonel Trimble, C.M.G., V.D., L.R.C.P., Chief Snperintendent Waring (Warrington Corp), Superintendent Rose and first officer Cawley (Crosfield's). Mr. J. J. CnosriFam, after apologising for the absence of Captain Crosfield, who was addressing a N S.L. meeting at Sheffield, congratulated Colonel Tritubler and the Brigade on the efficiency they bad shown. Indeed, he should imagine they were not only proud of the Brigade, but proud to belong to it. The object of the Brigade was a noble and worthy one—to render first aid to the suffering. It was a training that all should go through for they would benefit by going through it. If any testimony were wanted as to the results attained they had not far to look for where there was a large concourse of people there were ambulance men present. Application had been made for their presence at Blackpool. If any further testi- many were wanted surely it was to be found in the fact. that the Government were prepared to call in their services for most responsible duties, for he understood they took up special training for service as reserves in the Army and Navy hospital work. Thus they had a new opportunity of serving their country and that WOS a fine duty to undertake. Dr. Bean was very pleased with the inspection he made. They required 1,400 men for that service and they had 700 and he hoped that division would supply a fair number of men. Con- tinuing he said his company had always taken a deep interest in that Brigade—(hear, hear)—and he thought it was only right as a large employer of labour that it should be so. St. John Ambulance Brigade was an organization which must have the sympathy of all and be felt also the support of all. But especially was that the case with manufac- turers, because as they knew, unfortunately accidents would and did occur in works and therefore there must always be a call on their services. For that reason his Company gate that shield years ago and if it had pro- moted competition amongst and benefitted the Brigade, it had well served its purpose. (Applause) He then presented the shield to the Northwich Brigade and also presented a clock to each of the members. He also presented field glasses to the Bolton team for being 2nd and carvers to Barrow-in-Furness for being 3rd. Colonel TRIMBLER proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Crosfield. Referring to Captain Crosfield he said that they had never had a very much better friend than Captain Crosfield. (Hear, hear.) He had displayed for a number of years a very keen interest in that Brigade. To show his keenness in the work he undertook the duty of occupying a position on his (the speaker's) staff and during that time he gave great assistance for which he was extremely grateful. The shield had been an incentive to keen competition in ambulance work and the firm might be well assured of the fact that it had rendered valuable services in the interests of first aid to the public in general. Continuing he said that the War Office had promulgated a scheme to assist the Territorial Royal Army Medical Corps in case of invasion as it was impossible with its present staff to cope with the work devolving upon them incase of war. The Brigade up to now had discharged a very patriotic duty in giving men to the sick berth home hospitals reserve and bearer companies.lle was thoroughly convinced that it would further demonstrate its patriotism in filling up the voluntary aid sections to be established immedi- ately throughout the country. There would then ha an ample opportunity again to the Sisters of the Brigade to join in that movement and he was convinced that the nursing units of the St. John Ambulance Brigade would enter heartily into the work. Conclud- ing he thanked the judges. Deputy Commissioner GARNETT seconded and the resolution was carried, the men giving three cheers. On the proposition of Captain WARING a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Colonel Trimbler and his officers.

The members and friend of the Brigade then partook of a splendid tea in the girls Dining Hall.

THE DEVELOPMENT BILL.

In submitting the Devdlopment Bill to the House of Lords, Lord Carrington said it was not a Right to Work Bill nor was it a Bill to establish relief works. I quite agree it is neither of these things. We are not very particular about the Right to Work provided we get the opportunity to work. I have never favoured the carrying out of great schemes of national develoOlent on the lines of relief works. The State should do these things on business lines. It should give good wages and good conditions, and by that it will attract the best workmen, and their places in private employment would be filled by the unemployed. It is most earnestly to be hoped that the Lords will spare the Development Bill. I can see no reason why they should seek to destroy it. It can do them no harm ; but on the contrary may be of great benefit to them, for anything which develops the coun- try adds to the value of land. Moreover, as the Chancellor of the Exchequer pointed out. it is proposed to do by public money under the powers of this Bill, things which good" landlords have been doing at their own expense, It is calculated that one half of the £500,000 a year,which is to be spent on public improvements will be a clear gift or saving to the landowners by doing for them what they had been doing for themselves.

Public -interest is so -absorbed in the Budget just now that little attention is given to the cry of the unemployed, But should severe weather come the state of things will become intolerable, and it is to be hoped that local authorities and the several Government departments will look round at once and try to see if they have any work they could put in hand to relieve the depression of the Labour Market.

UNITED IRISH LEAGUE.

Address on Home Rule at Warrington.

The weeitly meeting of the Warrington branch of the United Irish League was held in the League rooms, Winwick-street, on Sunday evening last, when Dr. J. D. Liston presided over a large attendance. Mr. J. C. Dalton ('ate of Warrington and now president of the Chester U.1.L.) delivered an address on " Why does Ireland want Home Rule ?" He dealt with his subject in a lengthy and exhaustive manner and based his argu- ments chiefly on the failure of British rule in Ireland during the last 100 years. He maintained that Ireland should have the control of its domestic affairs, as it had been proved on every occasion that when legislation was introducedinto the House of Commons affecting I ribh questions the ignorance on those matters displayed by other than Irish members was very palpable. During the last 50 or 60 years ths population of Ireland had decreased by half, its population now being only 4,000,000. There had been a emit famine which bad carried away thousands of his country men and in addition to its baneful results they had had the sad spectacle of three separate insurrections. Those disaffections were clearly traceable to misgovernment and the refusal to administrate the law in accordance with Irish ideas and lush sympathies. A royal Com- mission bad shown that Ireland had for tits last 50 years been overtaxed to the extent of £2,750,000 per annum, but now the revenue from that country was less by £1,P00,000 than the cost to govern. That fact in itself, the speaker was sure, would make a serious Impression on the minds of the people of England. He believed that if the English workingmen were more thoroughly conversant with the question of Irish Government they would readily concede the Irish demand to-morrow. Certainly something was required to lift Ireland industrially and agriculturally from the state it was in at the present, and to the minds of the overwhelming majority of the Itish race throughout the world, there was no other remedy but Home Rule. (Applause). Councillor TOOLE proposed a vote of confidence in the Irish party in the House of Commons, so ably led by Mr. John Redmond. He said it was to the Irish party alone that they could look for any sensible settlement of the issue,vihich was very vital to the life of the Irish nation. They were all th4nkful for the splendid services which the I ribh party had rendered in the past and they would stand by them until the end was attained for which they had fought so long. No other question would be allowed by them to occupy a more prominent position than the question of Home Rule. (Loud applause.) Mr. P. CONWAY secondeci and the vote was carried heartily.

On the proposition of Mr. J. FAY a vote of thanks was passed to Mr. DALTON.

Mr, DALTON paid a similar compliment to the chairman which was heartily accorded. Tbe proceedings terminated with Mr. Dalton singing "God sate Ireland.''

She suffered mentally and physically through St. Vitus' Dance.

.1 .1 FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Mr. Philip Snowden, M.P. THE NEW BUDGET CRY.

The opposition to the Budget has taken on a new form. The word has gone forth that the dukes must be dropped, and the cry of unemployment must be taken up. The Budget is to be attacked not because it taxes millionaires and landlords, but because it does nothing to find work for the unemployed. This is certainly calculated to be a more popular appeal. In this new crusade the solution of the Work-for-all problem is to be found by . taxing the foreigner. This is the alternative policy on which the opponents of the Budget are to fight the coming General Election.

===== 0002601_19091023_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
A GREAT GAIN.

' It is a great gain to have the unemployed question taken up by a great political party as the leading issue of a .General Election. Though we may differ altogether from the Tariff Reformers as. to the efficacy of their proposals we are glad that they are going to put them forward as a panacea for unemploy- ment. This will lead to a thorough examina- tion of their claim, and it will also compel other parties to submit alternative sugges- tions. Out of this conflict and controversy something may come which will benefit the unemployed.

===== 0002601_19091023_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
A PEER ON UNEMPLOYMENT

The Tariff Reformers have a job I do not envy in trying to prove that taxing imports will gain the home market for the British workman. It is positively astounding that a man like Lord Curzon should have com- mitted himself, as he did at Leeds a few days ago, to the statement that the importation of £150,000,000 worth of manufactured articles into this country every year,untaxed, is the cause of unemployment here. The statement involves a mass of incorrect and fallacieus assumptions. In the first place. there are not .£150,000,000 worth of manu- factured goods annually imported into this country. Included in that total is a very considerable volume of partly Manufactured articles which are worked up by British labour and re-exported. A tax on these things would destroy our trade in these lines.

===== 0002601_19091023_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPORTS AND LABOUR.

Sight is lost, also, of the fact that the importation of £150,000,000 worth of goods must employ a vast amount of labour in transport and handling. The goods are brought in British ships, and they require dockers, carriers, railwaymen, and,gthers to transport them. But the great llacy in Lord Curzon's statement—a fallacy which underlies all Tariff Reform statements and arguments—is that it ignores the fact that the imported goods are paid for by exported British manufactures. There is, of course, a certain proportion of our imports which represent the interest on foreign investments, and this interest would have to be paid in goods under Tariff Reform. Apart from the imports which represent interest on foreign investments, all other goods which come into the country are in payment of British manufactured goods sent abroad.. We have nothing to export but coal (which represents a large amount of employment) and manufactured goods, and the total.of our exports is about three times the figure given by Lord Curzon as the value of imported manufactured goods. The British workmen who are employed in making goods for, export might keep in Mind the fact that stopping imports means stopping exports, and thereby reducing the amount of employment at home.

===== 0002601_19091023_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
TAXING THE FOREIGNER

It is really too absurd of Lord Curzon to talk about "taxing the foreigner." This sort of stuff should be left to the street orators of the Tariff Reform League. The financial position of Germany, at her wits end to raise national revenue, and having to do so by internal taxation should have laid that fallacy forever. The Antißudget League is covering our hoardings just now with posters which represent the British workman staggering under a load of Customs duties. He evidently has to pay those now imposed, and in the same way he would pay all others which might be levied on imported goods. THE BUDGET AND UNEMPLOYMENT.

The problem of unemployment is serious, and the Tariff Reformers are at least doing good service in talking about it. Their propaganda, as I said, will compel other parties to submit alternative proposals. It is not true to say the Budget ,does nothing for-unemployment. It touches the unem- ployed question at several points. It is a very light touch I admit;' but it is a little, and on the right lines. The Chancellor of the Exchequer admits that he expects his new taxes to bring in a very much greater yield next year. He has promised to use part of that 'surplus for unemployment insurance, part for removing the poor law disqualification from the Old Age Pensions Act, and a good part of it for Dreadnoughts. The unemployed insurance scheme will do more than anything else. which is immediately practicable, for the unemployed. But in another way the Budget deals with employment. The Development Bill sets aside £2,500,000 for works of public utility, and also not less than £600,000 a year for making new roads. It is true that ten, twenty times these sums could be usefully devoted to these purposes, but these are beginnings on right lines.

THE " DISTRESSED " COMMITTEES. The Local Authorities are much concerned about the coming winter. Although the Board of Trade returns shows a percentage of unemployed which is lower than at this time last year, the demands which are being made upon the Distress Committees are much greater. The unemployed are register- ing in much larger numbers. The reason for this may be that some impression has got abroad that the Distress Committees are now better able to find work. Unfortunately that is not so.

A Conference representing some fifty Dis- tress Committees was held last week in Manchester. Every delegate had the same story to tell of utter inability to find work for their genuine unemployed. In their despair, the Distress Committees appeal to the Government. The Government have nothing to offer but the promises of legisla- tion to which I have referred. Meanwhile, until the promised legislation matures, there is apparently nothing for the unemployed to do but to wait and starve. This cannot and ought not to be allowed. If work cannot be found or made, maintenance should be pro- vided. This winter promises to be little or no better than last. We all remember the excuses for unpreparedness which were made a year ago. These excuses cannot avail now. I readily admit the difficulty of organising large schemes of work in a hurry; but, I repeat, that failing work the unemployed are entitled to maintenance.

OUR SERIAL STORY.

wno wOuta expect to ne greeted as a friend. In :point of fact, that was exactly what hap- pened; for when, in the course of the even- ing, Rufus Cridge visited a music-hall, a hand was suddenly clapped upon his shoulder, and a man who was an utter stranger to him ;meted him laughinalv. ••111.3r clear .13;tael.u.iie, w:io in the, world would have thought of il•xling you in such a ala oe -as this?" said the man, who warn In 3vening dress, and who was evidently one of a small and rather festive party. „. "Thanks—but I can't do that,” said Cridge, backing away nervously. "The truth of the matter is I'm in London solely on business. I've only looked in here for half-an-hour. So you'll have to excuse me, please." "Why—what's come over you?" exclaimed Lie other -man. "Prosperity doesn't seem to agree with you. You used to be veiy dif- fere tit- " _ _

Rufus Cridge got away from him, with ,muttered excuses, and left the building. Outside in the street he thrust John Baxen- dale's hat from off his forehead and breath. heavily, and looked with a startled face into this new question as he had not looked be- fore. The game interested him—the very danger of it appealed to him,- but it waa such a matter of fighting in the dark. Now I come to think of it, I wish I'd taken what he had to offer and cleared out," said Cridge to himself. "Too late now. He's not likely to forgive me for his recep- tion at our hands. Why the deuce did I listen to the old woman? Why did I plunge into this busines.s without counting the chances more carefully? Besidcs, when he Comes to himself—" _

He went striding away through the streets, Li sing to gain control over himself— striving to formulate some plan for his future con- The Rufus Cridge that had been so miexpectedly cast aside kept doing things that John Baxendale would not have done— obtruded himself in the walk of John Baxen- iale and in John Baxendale's manners. Thus, when Rufus Cridge wanted to think about some matter he leant against a wall, ind thrust his hat on the back of his head in the old Rufus Cridge fashion, forgetful that he was an exceedingly well-dressed young man, and not the loafer he had been. It was after recoverino., himself from one of those deep thinking fits that he suddenly smote one fist into the palm of the othe- hand, and laughed aloud as a new thought occurred to him. By Jove! I'd forgotten that that which changes me changes him," he ex- Aaimed aloud. "There is a John Baxendale in the world—and a Rufus Cridge; and I'm the John Baxendale! When he comes to himself he is. in Rufus Cridge's home, and in Rufus Cridge's clothes, if he puts his shabby %gad outside Middleton's Rents every cons able will know him for Rufus Cridge. What the deuce have I. got to be afraid of ? "

His difficulties were magnified on the fol- lowing morning, when, as John Baxendale, he had to make his way to the little town of Deepington, and to Baxendale Hall. Here he knew he was coming to close quarters with the enemy; for this would be no place for chance encounters, but a place where every- one knew him, and would know all about him. He thought about that in the train on the journey down, for he knew that he was entering a place where John Baxendale had been known as a child and a boy and a man for a quarter of a century, and where he was, in a sense, the chief personage. And in that time John Baxendale had made friends and doubtless enemies; and this .new John Bax- endale must take his place, and know all about the game from the beginning. - Much must be left to chance; perhaps the extri, ordinary luck that had attended him in his first venture would stick to him throughout. At Deepintston Station an obsequious porter opened the door of the carriage, and touched his cap to the apparent John Baxendale. Rufus Cridge, looking quickly round, won- dered if by any chance there was anyone to be met here or to be greeted; saw with re- lief that the station was comparatively clear. He was quick enough to understand, how- ever, that as the house was some consider- able distance from the railway some vehicle should have been in attendance to meet him; that was a point he had omitted to take into consideration. Properly, of course, he should have telegraphed; now there was nothing for it but to make the best of the business, and to walk. The stationmaster had a pleasant word to say to the young man, and asked if he was expecting anything to be sent from the Hall. Rufus shook his head, and said that he intended to walk. He had an unpleasant feeling that as he went out of the station yard the stationmaster and the porter were looking after him curiously. In that he was deceived. Their sole interest in him lay in a feeling of surprise •at the extra- ordinary gruffness of manner assumed by the usually pleasant and genial John Baxendale. He came at last to the lodge gates, and turned into the long drive leading to tilt" house. And then for the first time the craven fear of the man whct on that other night had crept through the shadows and had broken into the house swept over him, and almost made him turn back. For he heard in the distance again that baying of the hounds that he had heard before; shivered when he re- membered how he had trembled at the thought that they might be loose and might Hunt him down and tear at him. He was glad to think they were chained; he went on again with renewed confidence. But the dogs were loose after all. He did not fully realise that until he had come within a short distance of the house, and met a groom riding, with three or four great clo'gs leaping and playing about, him and the horse. They saw this John Baxendale walk- ing. With joyous barks they made straight for him. And then in a moment thti first of them stopped, sniffed suspiciously, and drew back; bared his teeth in a snarl, and started barking fiercely, circling with the others round the man, who kept his crafty eyes upon them. As the groom rode up, one of them, bolder than the others, made a savage leap sideways, and almost caught Cridge by the arm. . .

"Here—beat thew) brutes off!" exclaimed Cridge, backing away from the dogs, and making a cut at one of them with a light cane be carried. "Beat them off, I say!" The groom, in utter bewilderment, rode straight in among the dogs, beating them off with his whip, and crying to them loudly. He kept his eyes on his master, looking at him in a strange, hesitating manner, as though he did not understand this extra- ordinary turn of events. " W'y!—vvot's come over 'em, sir?" he ex- claimed. "It .looks almost as if they didn't know you, sir. I never saw anything like it." "Send them back to the house and fasten them up. See they're not loosed again," said Rufus Cridge, shaking from head to foot. "Understand that I hold you responsible for them. If any of them are loosed again they shall be shot. Now, take them back to the house."

The man, a little hurt on his own account, touched his hat sulkily, and plied his whip savagely among the dogs, and sent them be- fore him up towards the house. Rufus Criqe leant against a tree, breathing heavily and wiping the sweat from his face. He had evidently been badly frightened. "I don't like this," lie muttered to him- self. "It's a bad beginning! It's a mighty bad beginning!"

Coming to -the house itself, he found him- self in no better plight. As we already know, his former entrance and , exit had been by means of windows, secretly. He knew no- thing of the house in any way, and yet was. supposed to be absolutely familiar with it. With what confidence he could command he went up to the great doors, and was in dills course admitted by an obsequious servant, who took from him John Baxendale's hat and waling-stick. Rufus Cridge saw before him round the great hall half-a-dozen doors, and he decided that he must make a plunge for one of them.

tile cnoice was a' aisastrous one. lie opened the door of a room, hich happened to be the smoking-rooth, ancl came 'face to face with a young man, stout of body and round of face, who was at that very moment lighting a cigar. It was, of course, Dick Munby, who Clad played his part of the dor- mouse to perfection, and had, in a sense, been utterly forgotten • by John Baxendale. For Baxend4e, as we know, had gone out to lunch on that day when Munby should have gone to town; and, as luck would have it, Dick Munby had slept through the whole morning, snd had only woke un after his Pena (tact no out to se 6 Evelyn and her aunt. Then tt had occurred to him that after a solitary lulch he would go for a walk, and would come back in time to explain to his friend. But he returned to discover that John Baxendale had gone to London. Dick having no concerns of his Own anywhere, de- cided and his explanation until the following day. So that here he Was in the smoking-room, ready to meet this new John Baxendale. Rufus Cridge stopped, and stared at the man with natural astonishment; and, strangely enough, that was just the astonish- ment that Dick Munby expected. He laughed a little foolishly, and held out his hand. • -

. "I'm awfully sorry, Jack," he said, a little "I overslept myself yesterday—and then you bolted off before L had a chance to explain. So X thought I'd wait and tell you how sorry I was that I had outstayed my wel- come."

Rufus Cridge was quick to grasp the situa- tion. This was a friend of John Baxendale, and must be treated with cordiality. He grasped Dick's hand, and laughed, and spoke with frankness.

"My dear boy, stay as long as you like," he said. "I should have been quite sorry to find you gone." "But you expected that I should have gone yesterday morning," said Dick, in some sur- prise. What took you off in such a hurry yesterday?" "I had to go to London—on business," said Cridge. "I suppose nothing's happened in my absenoe? " "Miss Kaylor cane? this morning—quite early, I %believe," said Dick, puffing at his cigar. "What a lucky fellow you are, Jack!" he added.

"I suppose I am," retorted Rufus Cridge, putting together the two points that a Miss Kaylor had called, and that he sins a lucky fellow on that account. He found himself wishing that he knew Miss Kaylbr's Christian name. He had, too, an uneasy sense that this man., whose name hottlid not know, was watching him curiously. "How glum you are, Jack," said Dick Munby; not like yourself in the least. What's wrong with you?" , "Nothing that I'm aware of," said Cridge, impatiently. "What should be wrong with me? Why do you stare at a man bike that?" "I beg your pardon. I'm sure," retorted the other, a little ruffled. "By the way, there are some letters for you there, and a note left by Miss Kaylor." He pointed as ho spoke to some letters on the table. It was strange work opening another man's letters in the prennee of a witness. Rufus Cridge hardly liked it—not from conscien- tious scruples, but because he had not yet persuaded himself fully that lie mils safe in this new character. However, he contrived to open the letters, finding nothing very in- teresting in them, until he came to the note this mysterious "Miss Kaylor" had written. He could scarcely control the laughter that rose up in him as he opened the note and read it. It was wonderful to think how he held this girl as well as John Baxendale in his power. " DEAREST,—I am a little worried and anxious about you; you seemed out of sorts yesterday, and I cannot understand this sudden and mysterious going away. You should have no secrets from me, Jack dear. If anything troubles you I want you to let me help you. Please come and see me directly you return. "Always your own

" EVELYN."

He was to go and see her—and he had not the remotest notion where she lived. So much was gained, because he knew her name now; but he dreaded this encounter more than he had dreaded anything yet. His knowledge of the world taught him that the eyes of a woman were keener than those of a man—especially the eyes of a woman in love; she might penetrate to the heart of the busi- ness at once. Yet that was unlikely, because it was obvious from the lettet that John Bamendale, although troubled about this strange discovery of his brother, had told the girl nothing concerning it. Therefore she would be unsuspicious—and in that lay Rufus Cridge's safety. • "Nothing the matter, I hope, Jack?" said Munby, watching the other's serious face. "No—nothing the matter," answered Criclge. "Only she wants me to go and see her."

"Well, that's easy enough, I shoul think," said Dick, with a laugh. "I sup- pose, by the way, you won't mind if I stay till the evening?—there's nothing to take mg., to London in a hurry," he added. "My dear boy, I'll be delighted," said the other; "and if you've nothing better to do, you might walk down with me. I'll go and. see—see Evelyn at once." That artful thought had occurred to him as an easy Way out of the difficulty. But he ' was to be checkmated in an unexpected' fashion; for Die& Munby laughed, and his reply meant a dashing to the ground of Rufus Cridge's hopes. "With pleasure, my dear fellow," said Dick; "but you'll have to shew me the way. I've never had the grivilege of visiting Miss Dorcas Stubbs or her niece, and I know no- thing about this part of the country. Come along, I'm your man!" Here was a pretty situation! Rufus Cridge would have to walk out of the house and start off in some direction or other; and he would have this man, whose eyes already seemed to be watching him with suspicion, as a witness of the extraordinary fact that he did not know his way to the house of the lady he was to marry. However, to do him justice, Rufus Cridge was no coward—he faced the situation at once. In some,,fashien things would play into his hands. If would trust to luck to pull him •through. Fortune certainly fought for him on this occasion. As by a species of instinct he sta'rted to walk' through the grounds accom- panied by this man whose name he did not know, he wished he had looked more care- fully at the photograph he had seen on the night of his first coming to Baxendale Hall; but even in that matter he was to be helped marvellously, for suddenly the man beside him exclaimed, "How impatient these lovers are! She's coming to meet you!"—and he saw- coming towards them through the trees a young girl. He began to wonder desperately then how he should greet her, and what she would say to him._

"I think I'll leave you together," said Dick; but Rufus Cridge took him by the arm, and drew him on. And in that way they. met the girl who was coming straight to- wards them, and looking only, as it seemed, at the supposed John Baxendale. ' "Dear Jack!" she exclaimed, in a low tone, as she took his hands. And then in a moment she had dropped her eyes from his face, and was looking curiously at the hands she held. "Why, what have you been doing with your fingers?" she asked in amazement. He understood at once that this delicate woman had discovered what a man might not have noticed—that in the place of the smooth, well-kept hands of John Baxendale were the hands of Rufus Cridge, with hard, rough skin, and with broken, stumpy nails. He almost snatched the hands away from her, with a laugh that had in it something of. defiance. "What should I have been doing mith them? " he demanded, roughly. "A little secret business, if you must know; a little rough work of an amateur sort. How are you, my dear?" He made an awkward movement, and en- circled her with his arm;_ she drew away from him, hurt and offended and pained, and glanced with a burning face at Munby. Mun- by was looking, for his part, in undisguised astonishment at this extraordinary John Bax- endale, whose manners seemed to have left him with the smoothness of his hands. "Oh, I'm sorry," said Cridge, with 'an awkward laugh; but we don't count him, you know. I had your note; I was coming tr vou."

"if you'll excuse me, Miss Kaylor, I'll ,walk back to the house," said Dick Munby, glancing for a moment at Rufus Cridge. I shall see you presently, Baxendale," he added, a little stiffly: As he went away Dick Munby muttered to himself: "If I didn't know him, I should swear the man had been drinking. I wonder if he's worrying over anything? I wonder if anything's gone wrong with him? He seems very strange—quite unlike himself." Meanwhile. Rufus Cridge walked on beside the girl, casting furtive glances at wit-, wondering What he should say. She was very beautiful, with a beauty of which her por- traits had given no real hint; a fierce feeling grew up in the man's mind that here was linnething else worth possessing, and ready to his hand in that new character of John Baxendale. This was the Woman John Bax- endale was to marry; and here was the new John Baxendale, cheated of his birthright for so many years, and coming fully into it now in good earnest. "I didn't mean to upset you, Evelyn," he said. presently, schooling his voice to a new softness. "Only you know that I love you, you know what my feeling for you is—" "Oh, it's all right, Jack,' she replied hastily, with a smile and blush. "Only you were a little demonstrative, you know. We won't say any more about it. I want you to tel me what it was that took you off in such a hurry, and why you seemed so worried yester- day. Come, tell me everything." . . . .

She slipped her hand -coaxingly into his- arm; he kept those tell-tale hands of his in his pockets. "Oh, it was only a matter of business," he said—" a sudden call to Lon- don. I want you to promise me something, Evelyn—something that means more to me than life or death—or anything." He stopped, and he now stood facing her; his hands were gripping hers. •• , Yes Jack—what is it?" she asked. _

" I want you to marry me as soon as r-s- -sil,le; I want there to be no delay;" he said: I've got an idea that all sorts of , things might happen—all sorts of things do happcn to a man who has money. . I want you to marry me—well, as soon ars it can be arranged." "But it is to be in a month, dear,' she pleaded. "I couldn't be ready. before."

"You must be," he answerea. "I'll make arrangements somehow; you must be ready." And then suddenly he put his arms about her and held her close, so that she struggled a little, protestingly, in his grasp. "Jack! Jack! You hurt me ! "

He kissed her roughly, and let her go. She looked at him for a moment, and then turned, muttering something about going back, and ran away from him through the woods. He stood looking after her, laughing. "I'll (1-.) it," he said. If I marry her I cement my pesition; I'm John Baxendale to the end of time. Let the other man have a turn at being Rufus Cridge—loafer and thief, cud what not. PH defy him to come back. gatne's mine; no one'll find out the truth until it's too late. It's a better game than I thought." His exultation was bound to find a vent; and the dull, quiet house was scarcely the place in which to find it. Dick Munby went off early in the evening—dismissed a little curtly, if the truth be told, by John Baxen- dale; and that John Bamendale, as he was sitppesed to be, looked about in. search for amusement. Nothing presented itself, and be determined that he would go fuither afield. Before doing so, however, he nerved himself for the coming ordeal with the aid of a con- siderable quantity of champagne at dinner; and then sallied out, and made his way into the quiet and unsuspecting little town of Ikepington. There is a quiet little inn at Deepingten, known as the Baxendale Head—the very model of what an inn should be; but en that particular night it was disturbed and shaken to its depths. Mr. Felix Goode, bay- ing other business to transact with Mr. Jilin Baxendale, after his return +0 London, had suddenly made up his mind to journey down to Baxendale ; and, as it happened, on that night he walked through the little town of Deeptngton on his way to the Hall; for it was Er fine night, and the old man felt he would enjoy the walk. Coming in time to the Baxendale Head, he heard a great disturb- ance—a sound of rioting and shouting and singing; and stopped for a moment in some curiosity. The highly respectable landlord, who had known Mr. Goode for years, and who happened to be at the doer, ran out, and caught him by the arm, and implored his help. It seemed that young Mr. Baxendale had, in the most unaccountable fashion, entered the inn, and had "made free," as the land- lord expressed it, with everybody; had with difficulty been coaxed into a private room, and locked in. The landlord could not under- stand it; would Mr. Goode go up and talk to the young gentleman? Very much perturbed, Mr. Goode went up and opened the door, and shut himself in with Rufus Cridge. Rufus Cridge, recognising him even in his drunken condition as "the lawyer, man," - begged him to sit down and make a night of it; "John Baxendale, what is this?"' ex- claimed the old man, in deep distress. "What have you been doing?" "To the deuce with John Baxendalc ! " exclaimed the other. " Call me Rufus, old friend, and we'll make a night of it ! " Felix Goode stared at him, going close to him for the purpose; clapped a hand upon his awn lips to restrain a pry. "The other man ! " he whispered, falling wick from the reeling creature before him. "But where's John Baxendale?"

HIS FATHER'S SON.

BY TOM GALLON

'Author of " Tatterley," "Picky Monteith," "Kiddy," "A Rogue in Love,' "Fate's Beg- gar 'Maid," The Charity Ghost," &c.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: The guests have separated after the dinner which celebrates John Baxendale's inheritance of his fortune, and he has told Evelyn Kaylor, the beautiful girl ho is to marry, that he has not a care in the world. Yet there is a strange foreboding of coming ill. He feels practically certain that someone is prowling about the grounds. When the last guest has retired, Felix Goode, the family lawyer, begins the unfolding of the sinister secret which is to alter the whole tenour of John 13axendale's life. In revenge for a wrong which his father did to a woman named Johanna Cridge she had stolen away one of twin sons, leaving the awful message that, just as her life had passed into darkness and evil, so should his. He should be educated, and yet trained to a life of infamy. That brother of John Baxen- dale may now at any time confront him. This story is told him at his father's request, under his dying injunction that it shall be preserved a secret. Baxendale, when on the way to his room, hears a sound in-the drawing-room. Entering, and go- ing to one of the windows, he confronts and seizes a burglar, to find himself face to face with a man in his own likeness._ _ .

Then John Baxendale does a dramatic thing. Inquiry convincing him that this man is his own brother, he astonishes him with the assurance that he purposes to divide equally the inherit- ance between them on the sole condition that the new-comer leaves the country and keeps the secret. He is taken with Rufus's suggestion that he should go and see the life he has lived, and so one evening makes his way to Middle- ton's Rents, on the south side of the river. On entering the house he is. felled from behind. With mischief in his eye, Rufus Cridge puts on the clothes of his wealthy brother.
