CHAPTER 111.

The woman seated by the fire had not moved, save to glance round once, when John Baxen- dale was struck down. She turned now, and re- garded this transformed Rufus Cridr and the prostrate man on the floor. Raising her head to look at him, she displayed a face old and wrinkled, but which must at one time have been one of striking beauty; the eyes still held some of their old fire and lustre. She called out sharply, to Cridge. "Don't stand there. Get away to your work; you know what you have to do," sitti exclaimed. "This is the chance of your life; go and seize it." "Don't you worry about me, mother," said Rufus, adjusting John Baxendale's hat care- fully on his own head. "It was a wonderful bit of luck that I should start off—all on my own account—to crack my own crib, as it were. I've told you what happened. This wonderful brother of mine "—he spurned the prostrate figure with his foot—" actually of- fered to share everything with me—on con- dition that I went abroad! I think I see myself going abroad—after having been cheated of my birthright for 'a quarter of a century. I've been the sport of others so far; now the game is in my hands, and I can do what I like."

"And we shall all live 'appy ever after- wards ! " exclaimed one of the men who had been lounging at the window when John Baxendale first came in—an old man, with ragged grey hair and . a venerable grey beard. "Because we're all in it, Rufus, you know."

---"Trust Amos Voles to make the most of the business and to look after his share of the plunder," said Cridge, with a laugh. "Well, perhaps you're right • we've had lean times long enough, and the fat ones are com- ing. And we'll all'share; we'll all have the fat times—in a certain proportion." The old woman had risen from her chait by the fire. She walked across the room to where Rufus Crjdge stood. With no tender- ness about her, she took him by the elbows and turned him round, and then back again, examining him closely. The two men who had assisted in the business drew back, half in fear and half in respect for her. "It's a great game," said the woman at last; "and you've been well trained for it, Rufus. Don't forget your lesson. The house of Baxendale begins from to-day fo go down- hill; the new John Baxendale begins to go the pace, and starts that great game of drag- ging the name of Baxendale in the dust. I spit upon Baxendale! I leave you to the work you have to do. By the memory of the dead child—poor thing of shame !—I held in my arms, and whose place you took, I send you out to do your work—and to do your worst. Go!"

She touched him lightly on the breast, m if in a sort of uncanny benediction; then turned quickly to the other men. "Put the old clothes on him," she said, indicating the unconscious Bamendale. "We must have a Rufus Cridge in the house, to ehew if necessary." • "Good-bye, mother," said Cridge, putting an arm for a moment carelessly round the old woman's shoulders, and kissing her. "You shall hear from me in a day or two: I won't forget. The first person I have to pass is the constable at the end of the court. H's a little afraid that something may have happened to John Baxendale. I'll convince him that hie fears were groundless. Do I look all right? Am I the man that came into this place a quarter of an hour ago? Look me_ over _carefully."

"Go!" exclaimed -the old woman again. And Rufus Cridge, subduing himself to the new character he had to play, went out of the room, and down the stairs into the court.

At the end of the court he was careful to nod to the constable (who had hitherto re- garded him, in the character of Rufus Cridge, as a man to be watched) and to as- sure that astute officer that all was well with him. "You weret afraid I shouldn't come back, eh?" he said, slipping a coin into the man's hand. "They're a rough lot—but I wasn't to be frightened by them. Good-night to you "And good-night to you, sir," said the conetable, touchicg his helmet. "There's one of 'em that's about as tough a lot as you could come across—been in our 'ands good- ness knows 'ow many times. Rufus Cridge is the man I mean, sir." "I know the chap you mean. You keep an eye on him," said Mr. Cridge; and so, with a nod to the constable, departed. In a sense, Rufus Cridge may be said to have shared those feelings, in a larger way, that had belonged to John Baxendale at an earlier period of the day; only that in this case the man had stepped out of an old husk, as it were—a butterfly from Out a grub. He thrust his hands into his pockets and fin- gered the money there, and a bunch of keys that belonged properly to Baxendale; re- membered, with a feeling of relief, how easily he had been mistaken for Baxendale on the previous night, and began to under- stand that there was an easy prospect before him, and that life generally had taken a newer and a better shape. It was for him to make the beet of it.

Night had fallen, and all London was be- fore him as it had never been before; he had money to spend, and he had unlimited credit. The -thoughts of the man turned to certain low haunts where, as Rufus Cridge, he was well &flown. He pictured himself flinging money about among old shady cronies, and cutting a dash generally. But that would • not do. Rufus Cridge was left behind in Middleton's Rents, and was to be accounted for there; this new John Baxen- dale had to, be careful of what he did. He determined to go to a first-class hotel for the night, and to-morrow to take up his new position ad John Bamendalo of Baxenclale Hall.

HOCKEY.

Latchford defeated Leigh last week 7-0 despite a fine exhibition of goal-keeping by the visiting custodian. The Leigh forwards failed to make use of several opportunities of scoring or the win or Latch- ford would not have been so pronounced. The game was fast aud open throughout and the home team were only a goal in front at half time. Banks having cleverly hooked the ball in the net. Immediately after the interval, however, following a run down by the left wing, Pearson further increased the lead with a smart shot, and this caused an epidemic of scoring for before the conclusion of the game Travis scored twice. Hackney once, and Pearson and Banks also made their contribution with two each. The Lanchford backs played well aid Travis nod his wings had a lively game. To-day the First Eleven at Winnington Park is as follows t—J. Appleton, goal l J. Wilkinson, W. H, Pickles. backs; F. Tole. A. Knight, J. Albinson, halves ; H. Booth, H. Parker, J. B. Wilkinson, E. Order of Druids.

The annual meeting of the Royal Arch Chapters of the Order of Druids was held on Saturday at the White Horse Hotel, Worsley, when the whole of the Chapters were represented, with the exception of Manchester No. 1 District, and Stockton-on-Tees, Bros. Robert Perdue and Edwin Allen representing the Warrington District. The meeting reported a net decrease in membership. of seven during the past year, but a slight increase m the funds. A proposi- tion to amend rule enacting that it shall be obligatory for a member to continue his membership in the primitive order to entitle him to full benefits in the chapter was ultimately withdrawn on the understand- ing that it be sent to the various lodges for their con- sideration and be placed upon the agenda for further discussion and settlement next year. Nominations were sent in from Salford, Wigan and Warrington Districts for the Office of President and ultimately Bro. Edwin Allen (Warrington District) was elected President for the next twelve months. Bro. J. T. Shepherd (Salford District) was re-elected Secretary and Bro. W. R. Proctor (Salford District) was re-elected Treasurer. It was decided to hold the next annual meeting in Warrington.

THE "SPRING CHICKEN "AT WIDNES. At Widnes on Monday, P. Leech, of City•gardens, St. Helens, wits fined £1 for stealing a chicken the property of Fred Wilson, greengrocer, Waterloo-road. He called at the shop and priced the bird, but when the assistant turned her back he ran away with the chicken. Police-constable Quinn arrested him later with the fowl in his possession. He•pleaded guilty and said he we sorry.

WOMEN LIBERALS.

ANNUAL TEA MEETING AND CONCERT

The Benefits of the Budget.

One of the most successful gatherings ever bold in connection with the Warrington Women's Libeial Association was the annual tea and concert which took place in the Bold-street Wesleyan schoolroom on Monday evening. The Association, which has already done great service in the progressive cause, continues to make encouraging heads ay and no doubt it is destined to play an equally useful part in furthering the advance of Liberalism whenever an appeal to the electorate is made. The arrangements for Monday's social meeting were all that could be desired and the members and friends spent a very pleasant evening. About 250 eat down to an excellent tea, the catering being in the bands of Atkinson's Cafe, Limited. The following ladies assisted at the tables :---Mesdames ,Lindley, Pemberton, Locker, Osborne, Clegg, Farness, Simpson, OR en, Walker, Dutton,Maginn and O'Brien and Miss Short. The concert was presided over by Mrs. C. E. Palker (president of the association), who was accompanied on the platform by Mrs. Armitage, Mks Crosfleld, Mrs. Walker (bon. treasurer)) and Mrs. 04orne (hon. secretary). Songs were given in delight- ful style by Miss Cox, Miss Jones and Miss Plinston. Mrs. PARKER, in the course of a short address; said they were there primarily as a Women's Liberal Associa- tion and as a rule they had a lecture on some political or social subject to teach them how to be good Liberals. That night, however,they were to have a change as Miss Croslield had kindly promised to give them an account of her recent trip to India. Continuing Mrs. Parker extended a hearty welcome, on behalf of the association' to all present. They were delighted to see such afine gathering—it was quite the best she had been to in connection with the associa- tion—and to be assembled under such happy circum- stances. They might congratulate themselves on having secured such a splendid room, which was so comfortable and convenient. She did not know whether all present were staunch Liberals. She hoped they were, and if they were not she hoped they soon would be. These ,were days of great activity, when they needed to stir up all the loyalty they could possibly command. Unfortunately a great many of them did not exactly know why they were Liberals and when they beard the other side of the question they were apt to think that side was quite as good, whereas if they knew the facts it was not half as good. (Applause.) It was always a great puzzle to her why workingpeople should be Conservatives, but. she could understand rich people supporting that party. Why the workers of the country should do so she did not know, because there was everything in the Liberal programme and principles that was in favour of the workingpeople, and it was the effort of the Liberal party to safeguard their rights. They had only to look at the Liberal record for the past three years, and they would feel great satisfaction in having rendered a Liberal member. (Applause.) If the Liberal Govern- ment bad done nothing but give old age pensions— (applause)—it would be a memorable Government for all time. She had no doubt everybody in that room knew of some heart which had been lightened and some home relieved from want by the certainty of receiving ss. a week. The Budget asked all to contribute a little towards the pensions, and what made the Budget such a memorable and wonderful thing was its great fairnesss in only asking those who could not pay much to pay a little, and in asking those who were rolling in wealth and splendour, and who would not feel it however much they paid, to pay in proportion to their incomes. That was what all the furore was about. Those who heard Mr. Ure's address at Warringkon would remember that he told them a significant fact—that although proportionately the poor were taxed as much as the wealthy, there had been no grumbling, murmur- ing, or unwillingness on their part to pay their share. All the talk about poverty and having to. give up different things had come from those who could well afford to pay the taxation that had been put upon them. She was not going to launch into a speech on the Budget but she wished them to understand that it was primarily intended to safeguard the people of England, their homes and their labour, and to make the conditions happier and better for everyone, and chiefly for those who had not too much happiness in their lives. Mr. Winston-Churchill—(applause)--summed up the Budget in his magniflcient speech on Saturday and she would quote them a short extract : .

Upon what objects and policies do we propose to spend the extra revenue which this Budget will unquestionably yield in future years? People talk vaguely of the stability of society, of the strength of the Empire, of the permanence of a Christian civilisation. On what foundation do they seek to build? There is only one foundation—a healthy family life for all.—(Hear, hear). If large classes of the population live under conditions which make it difficult, if not impossible, for them to keep a home together in decent comfort., if the children are' habitually underfed, if the house- wife ' is habitually overstrained, if the bread- winner is under employed or underpaid, if all are unprotected and uninsured against the common hazards of modern industrial life, if sickness, if accident, if infirmity, if old age, if unchecked intemperance or any other curse or affliction break up the home, as they break up thousands of homes, and scatter the family, as they scatter thousands of families in our laud, it is not merely a waste of earning power or the dispersal of a few poor sticks of furniture ; it is the stamina, the virtue, the safety, and the honour of the British race that is being squandered. (Cheers). No, the object of every single constructive proposal to which the revenues raised by this Budget will be devoted, not less than the object of the distribution of the taxes which make up the Budget, is to buttress and fortify the homes of thepeople. Look at them for yourselves—the old age pensions which have been given already on a scale unexampled in any other country, the legislation which has practically passed through Parliament to deal with the evils of sweating where they are found to exist, the attempt to multiply small hold- ings, which we have not done with yet, the improved methods of finding work through a system of labour exchanges, the system soon to be Intro- duced of insurance against unemployment, the State-aided insurance against invalidity, against sickness, and for a provision for widows and orphans. The main object and result of each and all of these is to give a greater measure of security to the family and tho home of the sober and industrious worker. (Cheers.) That is our aim, to that task we have bent our backs. In that labour we shall not be daunted by the machine-made abuse of partisans or by the nervous clamour of selfish riches. What- ever power may be given us shall be used for this object. Continuing, Mrs. Parker said they were wives and mothers to whom home was far and away the most important thing in life and the safeguarding of that and the giving to their childten the best possible conditions must always be their first aim and object. That was what the Budget meant to the women of England and of Warrington. At the last election there was glorious enthusiasm and the women all rallied round and did their utmost in support of Mr. Arthur Crosfleld—(applause)—who was the champion of the untaxed cupboard and the cheap loaf. Free Trade was the great issue at the last election and now there was talk of a fight on the Budget. When the election did come she was certain the women of Warrington

would rally in the progressive ranks and help to return Mr. Ciostleld triumphantly at the head of the poll. (Applause). Alluding to the question of the franchise Mrs. Parker said she was strongly in favour of women having the vote but she was dead against alt law breaking and a great deal of the notorious conduct of some of the advocates of the movement. As to the right of women to the vote, her feeling was just the same as it had always been. Until they got the vote they could influence those with whom they lived to support the right man and do something to spread the knowledge of Liberal principles. (Applause). In con- clusion Mrs. Parker announced that she had received letters regretting inability to attend from Miss Bessie Mitten and Mrs. Garnett,and a telegram and donation from Mrs. A. H. Crosfield.

Miss TRUDA CROSFIELD then gave a most interest- ing account of her trip to India, which has already been described in the "Examiner." She referred to the history and customs of that wonderful land and her itinerary included places like Cawnpore fraught with sad recollections to Englishmen by reason of their associations with the horrors of the mutiny. Miss Crosfield spoke in praise of the English rule in India and expressed the opinion that the great majority of the inhabitants looked upon the English with grati- tude and respect because of the relief works and the help to agriculture which they had established. It was only a few of the educated people who were trying to breed unrest. She was struck with the fact that there seemed to be so little real poverty in India. The famines were terrible when they came, but the lot of the poor was much easier than it was in these cold northern countries. They were able to exist on very little, but on the whole they looked contented and free from care. She was told on good authority that the land question in India was as much a difficulty as it was in Entihind.—The lecture was illustrated with some beautiful slides, tlie lantern being manipulated by Mr. Parkinson. At the close some amusing biosoopic views were shown. A vote of thanks was passed to Mrs. Osborne and the committee on the proposition of Mrs. PARKER and the meeting closed with the National Anthem.

THE EARTH IS THE LORDS'. ---- An Interesting Illustration of

Increment Value.

The correspondence between Sir Richard Stepley and the agent of the Duke of Bedford which Is published in the Chronicle will be, we have no doubt, of great help in the Budget controversy. From what- ever point of view it is regarded, it destroys the sophistries with which the real position of the land- lord is concealed in this controversy. We take only one aspect of the case, (says the "Westminster Gazette." What becomes of the argument that land .is like any other commodity? The Duke requires £6O a year for the land if a building is to be used as a residence, and £lOO if it is occupied as offices. Imagine a man buying a carpet, selecting his pattern, and asking the price, to be met with the reply, For what purpose are 7ou going to use the carpet? If it's for your drawing-room it's £6O, but if for the board-room of your office it will be £100." probably the would-be • purchaser'would hasten to the nearest policeman and ask that the state of mind of the seller should be examined. Yet an attitude which suggests mental derangement when applied to other commodities is a commonplace in the sale of land. precisely because land differs from every other commodity. The facts of Sir Richard Stapley's case require only to be stated. The Duke of Bedford grants a lease of land in a residental square with the stipulation that the building on the land shall only be used as a residence. Believing himself secure in the fact that the other leases contain a similar clause, the tenant spends some thousands of pounds on improving the residence. Then the Duke, for higher rents, allows the surrounding property to be converted into offices and thereby destroys the residential value of the houses that remain, When, however, the tenant, finding that his house no longer has value as a resi- dence, wishes in his turn to sell his lease for business purposes, the landlord demands an increase of £4O a year in rental. That extra ren.t means that the tenant receives £l,OOO less for his house and suffers a heavy loss. The Duke, mean- while, if he secures his higher rent, is in a position to claim enhanced compensation from the public for a necessary widening of the thoroughfare. Of course the Duke claims no more than be is legally entitled to do. Sir Richard Stapley is simply the victim of system that declares that the earth is the ,landlords. So without building, without improving, without spending a penny on his property, the Duke is enabled merely by changes in the needs of the community, to first destroy a square as a residental area, and then to pocket the whole increment which arises from the demand for offices. Could anything be more fair than that the community which has added the value should itself reap a portion of the increment?

A SUCCESSFUL EXPERIMENT.

Advantages of seed, soil and climate have always enabled Scotland to grow the finest oats in the world. This year, exceptional " growing " weather has produced oats so rich, so bold and so well flavoured that all past records are left behind. From this grand new crop we arc the best.

The first, consignment of varioni varieties of peaches from Ontario has recently arrived in London. The fruit is large, exquisite in flavour and of a rich color and bloom. The shipment is in the nature of an expert- merit, being the first supply that has ever been sent from Ontario to this country. It had not previously been thought possible to transport so delicate a fruit to England. Although ordinary care was taken, the fruit was noi specially packed, but, however, arrived in splendid condition. A fine display of the fruit may be seen at the Ontario Government Offices, 163, Strand, W.C., at Covent Garden, and will also be exhibited hl various parts of London, and should find a ready sale. Doubtless this first shipment will be the beginning of.e regular supply of delicious fruit from Ontario to this country.

Cheers were then given for the troupe and the concert which was a huge success concluded with the singing of the National Anthem.

Moore. FATAL CYCLING ACCIDENT.

Mr. T. Ridgway, coroner, held an inquiry at the Holly Hedge Farm on Saturday on the body or Robert Postlethwaite (48), pedlar, Ship-yard, Warrington, who sustaked fatal injuries by being knocked down by a cYclist,Mr. J.L. Fryer,of Daisy Bank Farm, Padgate,the Previoos Thursday night. Before opening the inquest the jury inspected the scene of the accident, which is about 200 yards higher up the road. There is a Lend in the roa-1, which has a steep gradient at this point. The Cororer informed the jury that the cyclist was Etill in bed and if they thought that death was not absolutely accident-4 it would be his (the coroner's) duty to adjourn the inqoiry in order to bring the cyclist's evidence before them. Owen Monks, a collier, of Queen-street, Bolton, said that on Thursday night he was with a youth named Proctor. on the highway to tnester from Warringtoo. They were resting on the side of the road about two hundred yards past Holly Hedge Farm. While there, the deceased,who wos carrying three props, came up to them and asked which was the nearest place to stoy the night. Witness told him Warrington would be the nearest. In ens% er to the Coroner, witness said deceased smelled slightly of drink. Wit- ness had just risen to his feet and deceased was stand- ing close to the middle of the road. The night was wary dark and windy. The first intimation witness bad Of the approach of the cyclist was the sound of his free • 'wheel going, and on looking round he saw the bicycle lamp. He was then about eight or nine yards away, and Witness shouted to him to" hold up." He was too near however to do that and the machine caught deceased right • in the middle of the back. Witness had only time to pull Proctor out of the way. On the collision taking place the cyclist called out " Ob," and nothing else was said. Deceased tried to get up two or three times and witness went to get hold of him so that he would not fall down. Deceased did not say anything. Another cyclist came down the hill about three seconds after- wards and witness shouted to him to stop. The first Cyclist would be going at the rate of about eight miles an hour. On the second cyclist coming on the scene witnese went for help to Mr. Ball's farm. When he returned the second cyclist was bathing the other one's bead in a small stream close by. William Proctor, the last witness's companion corroborated his statements. Vii lam Fryer, living at Daisy Farm Padgate, said he was brother to the injured cyclist. On the night in question witness kept about HO yards nehind his brother, as his brakes were not so sure as his brother's. He bad an acetelyne lamp, and his brother tad an oil one. It was a very dark and windy On hearing Monks shout he pulled up. Dr. Binne, of Stockton Heath, said that on examining ileceased he found a cut about two inchss long over the ,right eye. There was also a bruise on the right cheek. xle had made a post-mortem examination and found two fractures at the bi,se of the skull which were notacient to cause det.th. A letter was read Ly the Coroner from Mrs.J.L. Fryer, the wife of the injored cyclist, sympathizing with the deceased's widow in her terrible loss , The -Coroner sad that if the jury were satisfied !hat Fryer took proper precautions and was not to :lame, it was for them to return a verdict of Accident death" After a short consultation the jury agteed that death was accidental end that Fryer was not to blame. no coroner said they were all extremely sorry for the dow that her husband should have met his death in that manner.

OPEN AFTER Ho LOCAL TRADESMAN Fib:ED.—Charles Nixon, a chip potato salesman, of Heath-road, was summoned for keeping his premises open after the specified time 11 30 o'clook.—Actigg Sergeant Knott,said that on the 9th of the present month he in company with Sergeant Washington visited the defendant's chip potato shop in Heath-road, at 11.25 p.m. and they heard voices within and on entering saw eight men, four standing up and four sitting down, eating "chips." The door was closed,and the shutters were up to the windows. In answer to a query by Sergeant Washington defendant said there was no specified time for closing on his licence. The serjeant then said "Well, I have warned you before," and- the defendant admitted that be had. At 11.30 o'clock four of the men went out and at 25 minutes to 12 the remaining four also departed. The shop was supposed to be closed at the same time as licensed .beer houses.— Defendant said he was totally ignorant as regarded the time for closing his premises.—ln answer to this Sergeant Washington spoke to warning defendant about keeping his shop open after hours and he had occasionally kept open since. Defendant said be asked the sergeant what was the time specified for closing and ho said "I don't know exactly and added that he would look the Act up and let him know.—Sergeants Washington said that was not true. Defendant's father had kept the shop before him and defendant should know the right time for closing.—Defendant said his goods were perishable and he had to sell them or they would be no use to him and if anybody ordered them he left the shop open until they called. The Chairman said that defendant had been warned by the sergeant and he had no business to keep the shop open. They had to administer the law And he would be fined 10s. RNMOVED ANIMALS WITHOUT A LICENCE.—Jobn Wylde, a milk dealer, of Lower Walton, was charged with moving four pigs without a licence frofn Poole Cottage, Lower Walton to Oak Free Farm, High Legh. James Burgess was charged with receiving the pigs knowing the same not to be accompanied by a licence. A constable stated that W3lde left a declara- tion at his house, and said he was leaving some pigs at Oak Tree Farm. Wit- ness afterwards ascertained that he had no licence to remove the animals. On questioning him he he said he thought it was all right. Witness then went to Mrs. Burgess and she said that she thought Wilde had a licence. He told them they could be reported.— Mrs. Burgess, who appeared instead of her husband, said that when Wylde went to her house with the pigs, he put a paper down on the table and she thought it was a licence whereas it was only a declaration. Wylde said he had not had to bother with a licence before as the forms had always been filled up for him.—The case against Burgess was dismissed and Wylde was ordered to pay 10a. VISIT OF THE REV. T. A. TURNEY.—In connr- c ion with the Wesley Guile the Rev. T. A. TurLey, of Liver- pool, one of the most successful and piu ular of the •younger ministers in the Wesleyan Churcl,. visited Padgate on l'hurseay. In the afternoon he preached an impressive sermon on "The presence of God." basing his remarks on the v. ores from St. Matthew xxviii. 20, "Lo, I am v.ith you alway." In the evening Mr. T. B. Duckworth presided over a well attended meeting when Mr. Turney delivered a lecture on the subject "How to succeed in lite." He said that at the outset he wished to make it clear that by success he did not mean the amassing of riches or the attaining of a position in the worlds of science, of politics or of thy. church. The lecturer's idea of success was something deeper and higher. To be a success one must have quality as well as quantity, he must realize the divine purpose of his creition. The first step towards success must he decision for God. kart from this all else spells failure. A man to be truly success- ful must live close to God, must be God's companion. God must he at the heart of all his life. Success as the world counts it may be written down "folly" in the Master's ledger. The second step, the most essential, is definiteness of aim. The lecturer believes that the great fault of the present day is a lack of serious- ness. The life of the vast majority seems to consist of eating, drinking, slaeping, working, pleasuring, nothing more, no ideal to be attained; simply setting out on the sea of life without any definite aim, and having no true conception of what God requires of them. Every human life ought to be the expression of the thought in the heart orour Creator. If it is not vre.fail. Along .with a definite aim must be persistency of effort. It is one thing to have an ideal, it is another to persist in the attainment of it. Mr. Turner went on to warn his hearers against the peril of superficiality. In conclusion he gave as the essentials for a successful life. "Decision for God, definiteness of aim, persist- ency of purpose, a strong will, a well instructed mind, deep seated convictions, a sensitive conscience, and a sanctified heart." At the close the lecturer and the chairman were accorded a hearty vote of thanl,s on the motion of Mr. Plinston seconded by Mr. Roscollar.

St. Paul's Church. The harvest thanksgiving services were continued on Sunday. In the morning the service was attended by the members of the Warrington Battalion of the Church Lads' Brigade and the Boy Scent; who were heartily welcomed by the Rev. W. Bracec,amp, vicar, in the name of the congregation. The preacher was the Rev. J. R. Jones, vicar of Sankey. The church was crowded at the evening service when the Vicar preached from the text. "What mean ye by this service ?" He said that some people, who wanted to be notorious by their fanaticism, also objected strongly to harvest festivals and to churches, as they said, being turned into "greengrocers' shops." With such people they had nothing whatever to do that night. No Christian ought to want to advertise himself. Still if there were some who wished to do so the best way was by hard, consistent work, by doing their level best to bring men out of darkness into light, and not to discourage any effort to bring together such a large congregation as that which was then assembled in that church, where they had met with one object in view— and that was to thank God for Ilia harvest mercies.

Wycliffe Congregational Church.

Services of thanksgiving for the ingathering of the harvest were held at this church on Sunday, when large congregations assembled. The church bad been very tastefully decorated with choice flowers by lady mem- bers of the congregation, under the direction of the Misses Porritt. At the morning's-service the choir ren- dered the anthem" Thou visitest the earth" (Barnby). The Rev. John Yonge took for his text the words "The Sacrifice of Praise." He said he would like to feel that the large increase of recent years in the popularity of harvest festivals was a real evidence of the growth of human gratitude, or, should he say, of Chri,tian gratitude ? Surely such services must at least help to foster it. He thought they might he so devised and solemnized as to do so, even more perhaps, than was yet the case. It would considerably make for the best result if, at least in a town or district, the festivals could be held on the same day. There would be an impressive- ness about such a unanimity of celebration which would be very beautiful and helpful. As it was, the occurrence of the festival in different churches upon different days tended rather to divide the interest, to create a rivalry in the decoration and in musical service, and so spread more of dissipation of thought than a devout concentratien of worship. The Christian harvest festival should be animated with the spirit of a trye reverence and a deep sincerity if it was to he acceptable to Him for Whose glory it was intended. In the evening Mr. Young based his discourse on the text "The goodness of God leadeth thee to repentance." The choir gave hiehly crediable interpretation of the anthems "0 Gladsome Light" (Sullivany and "Let the people praise Thee" (W. H. Payton). The latter is the composition of the church organist and it is bright yet devotional. The tenor solo was sung by Mr. W. Booth.

Bewsey-road Wesley Guild and Bible Class League.

The Bewsey-road Wesley Guild and the Warrington Wesleyan Bible Class League held a joint meeting in Bewsey-road Lecture Hall on Monday evening. Mr. Charles E. Parker presided over a good attendance and he was supported by the Rev. E. B. Reeling, Messrs John Upson, J.P., J. C. Holland, R. Corbett and J Maile.

The CHAIRMAN referred to the tenderness of Jesus Christ and said we were rather apt to look upon Him as meek and mild whereas strength predominated. The Rev. E. B. KEELING dealt upcn the importance of consecration and of carrying out the precepts of the Bible in everyday life. Mr. JOHN UPSON spoke of the disappointment which came from unrewarded effort. He said that times of barrenness were experienced in Social and Christian Service, and in these times when the Heavens seemed of brass unless the motive of the service were of the highest inevita ly we gave up in despair. That motive —the love of Christ—was the driving power which would carry them successfully through all discourage- ments. No service was unfruitful. Often its most valuable function was a reflex one. It strengthenei our purpose, purified our lives, and protected us often from temptation. Results, too were not in cur hands. Our duty was to labour, to use opportunity, to sow the seed and leave the result with God. Sooner or later the seed would fructify turd the harvest was sure. Mr. HOLLAND said that great happiness accrued from being engaged in the service of the Lord Jesus Christ. The experience of those who were followers of Christ even the memory of the House of God was an incentive to them to be loyal to their profession. Mr. Connwrr explained the need for doing their own part in the Master's service. If everybody would do their own part instead of looking upon others the Church would prosper in a different way to what it had done in the past. During the evening Miss Maud Hipkiss gave splendid renderings of " The Loreley " (Lizst) and " Bid me discourse" (Wshop). Mr, T. %V. Pearce was the accompanist.

TRAFALGAR DAY.

PrEsmtation of Nelsen Shield. and

Nizes.

Thursday being Trafalgar Day, Alderman Bennett vi sited Holy Trinity school to present the Nelion Shield, which contains copper from the ship "'Victory," aid .which he offers, for competition annually for the best essay on a selected patriotic subject. Councillor FULTON-SIIITH presided and the Rev, E. C. E. Carleton, the Rev. H. R. Wilde, the school managers, and a number of parents of the children were also present. The CHAIRMAN delivered an interesting address to the children on the lessons of Trafalgar Day and called upon Alderman Bennett to present the -shield and distribate the prize which had been won by Marjory A. Gleave. Alderman BENNEW said lie would like specially to congratulate Mica Davies, her assistants .and the children upon haxing for the -third time succeeded in winning the shield. Addressing the children on the subject of the essay" My duty to England," he said that in order to do their duty to their country they must first of all do their duty to themselves and therefore they must endeavour to be strong in body, clever in brain and good in charaCter. The greatness of a nat;on consisted in its individual citizens and if every child in tngland grew up to be healthy, wise and good, it followed as a matter of course that England would be a great nation: Some of the scholars m;ght have the ability to promote the fame and prosperity of their country by 'achieving dis- tinction in science or in literature and he hoped that many of them as they grew to marihood and woman- hood, would try to serve their native town on the Education Committee, in the Council Chamber, or in some of the numerous other ways which were Open to all of them. It was even possible that some of them might eventually serve their country in Parliament itself, and he hoped many of the scholars would climb the educational ladder which might eventually lead to the University. If in the future any of them were called upon to fight for their country, he hoped they would do so with the valour and unselfishness of Nelson, and be willing like jiim to lay down their lives for England. Alderman BENNETT handed the following books to Marjory A. Gleave :—" Empire Annual for Girls," (Buckland), " Puck of Pooks Hill" (Kipling), "A Child's History of England" (Dickens), and "The Dream of an Englishman" (Arthur Bennett). Mr. W. BIBBY, Councillor BROADHURST and others also spoke. ' The party afterwards went to St. James' school where a similar ceremony took place, Alderman Bennett presenting the second prize to Fred Slater. The books awarded were :—" Empire Annual for bbys " (Buchland), " Puck of Pooks Hills" (Kipling), "-A Child's History of England" (Dickens), and "The Dr6m of an Englishman (Bennett). Allusion was made to the fact that St. James' school had won the shield 'on one occasion, tied with Arpley-- street school on another, and had twice succeeded in obtaining the second prize. The Rev. H. B-. Firth, the Rev. H. H. dorbsie, Alderman Bennett and Mr. W. H, Stansfiela (head- master) delivered addresses. .

At both schoowls an interesting musical programme was rendered, "The Death of Nelson" and Kipling's 'Recessional" being sung at Holy Trinity school with excellent effect.

The presentation of essay prizes at the Secondary School took place at the annual prize distribution on Wednesday and is reported in another column. The books given by Alderman Bennett were :—To Ruby Witts, " The Poetical Works of Tennyson," " Life of Alin Howard" (Gibson)," Golden Treasury" (Pulgtave) " ETereward the Wake " (Kingsley) and " The dream of an Englishman" (Bennett) ; to John Sephton " The Poetical Works of Tennyson.'° "Harold "(Lytton) "Story of King Arthur and his Knights (Cutler), Crown of Wild Olive " (Ruskin) and "The Dream of an Englishman" (Bennett). TEMPERANCE LECTURE.

Warrington Board of Guardians

Criticised.

A practical lecture 'on the value of temperance was given by Dr. Morris P.C.S. (Day School Lecturer, L. i and C. Band of Hope Union) n the Parr Hall on Thursday evening when he took for his subject"' The nutritive value of intoxicating drinks." Mr. Thomas Dale presided over a large attendance and was supported on the platform by Rev. J. S. Hughes and Mr. Gandy. The CHAIRMAN said that they had a Board of Gusrdians in Warrington and they held a meeting last Wednesday. There was a letter sent to them in the shape of a request from Mr. Batley asking permission to give a lecture on "Alcohol and the human body" to the children that were in the Padzate Schools under the care of the Warrington Board of Guardians. Those ladies and gentlemen deliberated with regard to the request and it was stated definitely so as not. to harm the tender feelings of those sensitive gentlemen that such words as "drunkenness," "public-house,' or "pledge" should not be named. It was moved and seconded and carried that that request should he laid on the table. (Shame.) They knew what that meant. It meant that it was not to be considered for the time being. There was another gentleman present who made even another sugges- tion that it be laid underneath the table. hey believed that such statements in connection with so important a question as that were out of harmony with the views of the vast majority of the voters of Warrington. (Applause). The gentleman who made that very discreditable remark was a publican. (Shame). They realised the importance of training children, and especially the young children who were under the rare of the Guardians. He thanked the ladies who stood up for their cause. (Applause.) Many of the children who were in the homes were there through intemperance or intoxicating drink. Dr. MORRIS gave a very interesting and instructive lecture and illustrated his points as temperance lecturers do, by testing the various wines and proving that there was alcohol in them. At the outset he referred to the action of the Board of Guardians and said that t' e Chairman of the Guardians had said that the lectures were not given in the villages except in very rare CASES. Be (the speaker) said that 'was a lie. (hear, hear.) He was one of the lecturers who took lib a great part of Lancashire, and the Lauca,hire County Education Authority had granted permission for temperance lectures all the schools in the area—which was a village district —provided the managers gave their consent. In no case had he found one of the authorities in his district which refused, which showed that the statement was wrong. There was issued by the Board of Education in London in June last a syllabus which said that lessons in temperance should be given at least three times a year, and yet the Board of Guardians said that they were not neceisary. He was only sorry that such state- ments had been made. Proceeding with the lecture, Dr. Morris criticised a number ot medicated e ines and nutiitive medicines because of the alcohol they contained—as muelt as 18 and 20 per cent, in sotue cases. Could alcohol drinks build up their bodies? To become strong they must take into their bodies go. d food, such as milk, bread, meat, fish and oatmeal. Milk was a drink as well as a food. That proved at once that they could have drinks which were foods. Some people were advised to get some nourishing stout, it was not !nourishing. There was contained in milk— water, sugar, lime and curd. The curd was the best part of the milk. It was the only part of milk which built up the body. There was only a twentieth part of mud in the best beer obtainable, as compared to milk, so that if they bought ss. Od. worth of beer and 3d. worth of milk, the curd would be equal. Coh.nel Hall Walker said in the House of Commons in March that beer was liquid bread. The curd in hoer was incomparable with the curd in -bread, so that beer was not liquid bread. He supposed Colonel Walker wanted the people to think that because his firm made beer from a very good barley it must be good for people. Barley was a good food, but when it had been made into malt it got less so, and when n.ade into beer the good food Nanished. Even beer had got more curd in it than port wine. Concluding, he said that alcoholic drinks did not contain the curd which was necessary to build up the body, and money spent upon such things was money wasted. (Applause). The lecture was followed by a cinematograph exhibi- tion, which portrayed .the life and misery entailed

through dr7nk, and ho v a happy re-union of the family was brought about by giving it up. Mr. GANDY, on behalf of Mr. F. H. Smith, of Lymm Hull, presented two gold brooches to Miss May Isher- wood, retiring Band of Hole Queen, aud Miss Elsie Hewitt, Queen Elect. During the evening the Select Choir (Band of Hope Union) sweetly rendered "Stars that en your wondrous way," "The delight of Spring," and "The gipsies laughing trio," the solo in the latter being taken by Master A. Hamblett. Mr. A. Spruce nal the conductor, and Miss Elsie Spruce the accompanist.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RUNCORN VIC TOIZIA IE.IIORIAL HOSPITAL.

On Saturday afternoon the "Samuel Taylor" Ward of Runcorn Victoria Memorial Hospital was opened by Mr. A. .T. Smith, managing director of Castner Kellner Electro-Alkali Company. The ward is the generous gift of Mr. Samuel Taylor, 3.P., of Runcorn and Dove- holes, Buxton, Iv ho has been six times chief magistrate of Runcorn. The h spital, twit seven ytara ago by voluntary effort, had I:euorne inadequate to the needs of the district, and Mr. Taylor spontaueously made On offer of .V. 03 for a new wing. The contract was £5lO, and Mr. Tay lt.t enlarged his offer to that extent. The total cost of the extension, including the furnishing, is £1,050, and towards this sum the United Alkali Com- pany contributed £lOO, Castner Kellner £25, Vighfield Tanning Company £25, and Salt Union £25. There were many other smaller subscribe: a. There was a large company present at the opening ceremony, which was conducted by Mr. F. Wiswall, J.P., chairman of the Urban District Council. Mr. Taylor presented Mr. Smith with a beautiful silver key to open the door. He referred in terms of eulogy to Mr. Taylor's timely and generous gift, and mentioned that the hospital required £6OO to .£7OO a year for maintenance. One of the pleasantest and most healthy features of the institution was that one-half of that sum was provided by the voluntary subscriptions of working men. (Applause). This was an example of true Socialism. Mr. Taylor announced amid applause that Mr. Smith had given twenty guineas towards tbe furnishing.

===== 0002601_19091023_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. HELENS.

SUDDEN DEATH IN THE MINE.

Mr. Brighouse held an inquest at Haydock, on Mon- day, on Robert Jones, aged forty-seven years, of Toll- bar Cottage, Haydock, who dial suddenly while at, work in the Old Boston Colliery, on Friday. Evi- dence was given that the man soddenly collapsed while working, and was dead before be could be got out of the pit. Three doctors bad conducted a post- mortem, and they stated that death was due to rupture of the aorta, which had been affected by aneurism. The jury returned a verdict of "Death from natural causes."

EXTENSION OF A ST. HELENS HOSPITAL. As part of a scheme for extending the Providence Free Hospital, St. Helens, with a view to doubling the accommodation at a cost of £lO,OOO, the operating theatre in one portion of the extension which has been completed was opened on Tuesday. and the foundation- stone was !aid of the Magdal*-n Taylor Wards, in memory of Mother Magdalen Taylor, who founded the institution 25 yea, s ago. Lard Derby was to have laid the stone, but he was unable to be present, and his brother, Mr. George Stanley, took his place. Lord Gerard presided, and said the extension would increase the accommodation from 50 to 100 beds. The most pressing need was the children's hospital, which would be quite separate from the other buildings. Inside the hospital the Duchess of Norfolk unveiled' a memorlal-stone to the memory of Mother Magdalen Taylor, and Lady Gerard opened the new operating theatre, which (she said) Sir Frederick Troves said filled every modern requirement. After luaoheon a bazaar in the Town Hall, which is to occupy five days was opened by the Duchess of Norfolk. She said that she had been through the hospital, and that it was one of the best hospitals she had seen so far as modern re4uirements went.

LABOUR M.P.'S AT ST. HELENS. SOLUTIONS FOR UNEMPLOYMENT.

A big Labour' demonstration in support of the Budget was held at St. Helens on Tuesday night, and was addressed by sevei al Labour members of Parliament. About 2,000 people were present. Mr. T. Glover, M.P. for St. Helens, said that mining royalties should be the property of the State, and that miners should receive a minimum wage of Bs. a day. Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P. for Newton, said that neither Free Trade nor Tariff " Reform" would solyo the pro- blem of unemployment. There was only one solution, and that was reduction of hours of labour and more equitable distribution of the proceeds of wealth created by the workers. (Cheers). The chief cause of unem- ployment was the introduction of machinery, which ought to have been a blessing to mankind. but bad proved a positive curse. This particularly applied to the glass trade. He contended that with six working hours a day under modern conditions they could live the existence intended by the Creator. (Cheers.) Mr. C. Duncan, M,P. for Barrow-in-Furness, said that the only solution of the present difficulty was to end the House of Lords. Noble lords saw there was trouble ahead, but they did not know what to do with the Budget. A resolution in favour.. of the Budget was carried with enthusiasm.

EARLESTOWN AND. NEWTON.

MR. J. A. SEDDON, M.P., AT WAVERTREE, INTERESTING ADDRESS.

There was a large gathering of members and friends at Dovedale-road Baptist Church Brotherhood, Wavertree, on Sunday afternocn, presided over by the Rev. J: Fleming Shearer, w Lc) said they welcomed Mr. Seddon, not only as a member of Parliament, but as a great leader of the people, and a whole-hearted tei lever in Evangelical Chi ist iani (Applause.) Mr. Seddon, who was very cordially received, referred to the Spanish horror which had stirred the hearts of every man and woman in that great gathering, and he scanted to say in deprecating any attempt either in that ,thurch or elsewhe.e at making any political or sectarian capital out of the crime, how horrified he was, and to express his abhorrence at the inhuman crime which had been committed in a civilised country. If it WdS true that the blood of the martyrs was tl.e seed of the Church it was equally true in that particular instance that the blood of the martyr was the liberty of the people. (Loud applause). Mr. Seddon then went on to state that fifteen years ago he became what he had expressed elsewhere, a Radical in religion and a Nonconformist in politics—(laughter)— ; but since then he had travelled many weary miles of duty, and he wanted to say that if there was to be any inspiration in their lives, any continuity of effort, it could only be by living associa- tion and union with Jesus Christ, the Founder of their religion, who would guide and direct them into the right path. (Hear, hear.) It was no reflection upon Christianity to make the statement that the Church had been remiss in some of her ctuti,s, and that the social conditions around them were, therefore, indepen- dent of the teaching of Christ. (Hear, hear.) But he would like to say if the Church wished to retain her own and those in honest doubt—and this was a very pitiable condition in which t 3 he found—it would not fe brought about by excommunicating them, but by a display of pity and sympathy. (Hear. hear,) The first lesson from nature study was that of meditation, which to-day was a Icst art lhey ran after DPW sensations ; had no time to think. Ile rush of their modern con- ditions left the majority of men wholly outside the sphere of meditation upon nature and the works of the Creator ; as manifested everywhere, they bad allowed themselves to he creatures of circumstances. He rejoiced to see them bonded together in that huge gathering ; but it would not sound so exclusive if they admitted into their ranks the female members of the community, because, to use an Irishism," Woman is th=t best man." (Laughter.) Be, however, welcomed the awakening of social responsibility on the part of the Church, because he believed, with the statement of the Rev. Silvester Horne, that" as a nation we were bleed- ing to death." That was literally true, and if that hemorrhage was to he stopped it would only by the Church recognising its responsibility. (Applause).

Parr Hall Organ.

Sir,—l was requested to play the above organ on Monday and Tuesday last on the occasion of the Unionist Meeting and the S.P.G. Meeting, but lam sorry to say that I found it impossible to play at all. in consequence of the dilapidated and unsatisfactory state of the instrument. The organ is old and past efficient repair ; and in my opinion even the tuning of same is waste of money, if it is ever tuned at all. Cannot something be done to remedy this state of affairs, and an organ tit for such a splendid hall be purchased by the Corporation.—Yours &c.,

_ A. B. RANDALL. Organist of Holy Tx inity Church

A Growl from Gorsey-lane.

Sir,—l used to be a nice country lane not long ago, lilt now, I air neither one thing nor another. I have been blamed and strong words have been uttered, owing to my miserable condition. lam full of holes, with plenty of pools of water, in fact people have said I resemble the lake distrigts and I den't like it. You should see the horses struggling bravely with their heavy loads, and the drivers c--Oh, it makes me shiver when they are passing over me! • I long to be a well behaved lane and would be, it I were properly attended to. Winter will soon be here, and if lam not put in order before, I shall be sorry for the dwellers on my aides, and those who frequently use me, and rhope these good people will not say strong -words about me, as I have suffered quite enough.—l remain, dear sir yours dissappointedly,

GORSEY LANE

Warrington, October 21st, 1909

===== 0002601_19091023_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
Fairfield Girls' Schools Amalgamation.

Sir,--I hasten to acknowledge my responsibility for the letter written to the members of the Education Committee and, for the figures therein stated of £200,000. As to half of it I am quite clear. I have carefully calculated that the cost of replacement of the Church of England schools within the Borough of Warrlngton with all up-to-date requirements would be on ordinary terms of sale ar.d purchase at least £lOO,OOO. Competent authorities support me in this. But this calculation takes no account of the cost of the compulsory purchase of suitable sites already built on, which might be anything. We have thought possibly anothee Now as to the main point. The managers of the Fairfield Schools, after long deliberation and at great cost to their own feelings, have made a fair and reasonable offer, so fair and reasonable that, with the exception of the concluding words which were doubt- less not understood at the conference and require further consideration, it received the unanimous consent of the School Management Committee. This offer has now been refused.' It remains for the Education Committee to make their offer. The managers know their own minds and they know also their own Trust Deeds, hnd if the Education Com- mittee refuse fair terms and determine to exercise powers which doubtless they have, the managers will undoubtedly in the conscientious fulfilment of their duty exercise the powers which they too possess I am, Sir, yours truly

FREDERIC W. 'WILLIS The Rectory, Warrington, October .?2nd, 1909.

===== 0002601_19091023_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
The Guardians and Temperance.

Sir,—Adverting to your issue of Saturday last con- Inining a report of a meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians I notice that the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union (a society that has spent large sums in sending certificated teachers to give lectures in Day Schools on the nature of Alcohol' had applied for permission to give a lecture in the Padgate Industrial School. Although the application plainly stated that every precaution would be taken to avoid offending anyone who habitually made use of intoxicating liquors—such words as "Pledge," "Drunkenness," " Publichouse," never being used— the majority of the Guardians persisted in misundm: standing the application, whilst one gentleman—wff, from the nature of his trade would have shewn better taste in taking no part in the discussion or voting— made remarks which to say the least were very dis- courteous. Notwithstanding the earnest efforts of the minority the application was refused—five voting for it and nine against it. I should like to ask, how it comes about that the majority of the Guardians present at the last meeting could afford to reject this gratuitous offer, whilst the nation is spending large sums annually in training young teachers in our Training Colleges, in order that this subject of Temperance may shortly find a place in the curriculum of all our Elementary Schools. I notice that one of the Guardians who voted with the majority, int:mated that the morals of the children were well looked after. Whether this is so, I have no means of judging, but if the said children are to learn their manners from the majority of our Board of Guardians, who, instead of courteously declining an offer they could not see their way to accept, ordered the letter to lie on the table —it would perhaps be well for steps to be taken to see that no copy of your issue of Saturday last, finds its way into the hands of any of the inmates of Padgate Industrial school.— Yours truly) THOMAS DALE, EL P (Chairman of Warrington Band of Hope Union.) 76, Priestley-street.

Sir,—Some members of our Board of Guardians seem to me to be very intolerant and don't seem to possess much of that truly English quality of "Fair Play." When the letter from the Band of Hope and Temperance Union asking permission to give a lecture to the children came up for discussion it might have been treated with a moderate amount of courteousy, and Messrs. Jolley and Bent might have shown before the lady Guardians a little of that good breeding English gentlemen are noted for. If after the society had given its scientific lecture Messrs. Bent and Jolley and the Chairman had seen fit to give a reply lecture showing the good effects of beer and spirits on the body, their -brother and sister Guardians would have granted them an opportunity. I should like, myself, to see these three gentlemen supported by Mr. Forshaw holding forth in public meeting assembled as to the good effect and stimulating powers of fourpenny beer and pointing out how much stronger a man will be on it in preference to good solid food. -I am, &c., PHIL •SQUOD.

Mr • H. Wardman, of 10, Wilkinson-street, also writes to protest against the action of the Guardians, and points out that" what the Board of Education thinks is needful for the children of our elementary schools, our Guardians deny to the children who are not so fortunately situated." He invites Mr. Jolley as an educationist to tell us what the advantages of alcohol are and Mr. Forshaw. to prove that the suggested lectures are" A travesty of Science."

Wycliffe Band of Hope

The Band of Hope in connection with Wycliffe Sunday School was awakened on Wednesday evening from its somnolence of the last 18 months when a meeting was held in the Memorial Hall. A good audience was presided over by the Rev. John Yonge who hoped they would go on with the Band of Hope work as it was very essential to that Church as to all others.—Mrs. Simpson was the speaker and she trusted that the training the members would receive would bring out good results and that they would grow up to be temperance men and women. LIBERAL VAN CAMPAIGN.

Successful Meetings.

The van caropaTgn promoted by the Warrington Liberal Association has proved a great success, several largely attended meetings having been held in various wards. On Monday evening a meeting took place on the waste ground off Bewsey-road, when Mr. R. Lewis, and Mr. Sydney Arnold were the speakers. Mr. Joseph Owen-presided. The CHAIRMAN, in his opening address, said that that meeting was held to supyort the great Budget, which was the greatest financial measure of modemn times. (Applause). It was possible to get money badly and to spend it in worse fashion, but there was nothing in that Budget which would injure commerce or trade. It would not impose undue burdens cn the working classes and one good feature was that the yield of the land taxes would increase as years went on. By diverting to the State a portion of the unearned increment it would, instead of going into the pocket of the landlord,help to fill the nation's coffers and so help the nation's progress. All these things were sufficient to justify him in saying that it was the most important Budget they had had for at least fifty years. (Applause). Mr. ARNOLD said the Budget, was at present in the report stage, and it was going to pass through the House of Lords too—(hear, hear)—or the Peers would hear about it. (Applause). Referring to the taxes imposed by the Budget the speaker said 3i millions of the 14 millions had to be raised by the working classes by an increase of tie tobacco and spirit duties and the remaining 10A millions would be obtained from the rich, the liquor trade, and the land. The Tory party's method of raising the money would be to tax food—bread and all the necessaries of life. They said they would put a tax on foreign corn, but it would only raise the price of the same article that was grown at home, and in the end instead of the 14 millions which the present Government needed tariff reform would cost the people 50 millions. Germany had tariff reform but wheat in this country cost 325. a quarter while the price in Germany was 435. 11d, or eleven shillings more. The speaker pointed out that if food stuffs were taxed the foreigner would not suffer and it would be the consumer who would have to bear the burden. The Budget was a great monu- ment to the resources of Free Trade. It had nearly passed through the House of Commons and they wanted it to pass through the House of Lords. (Applause.) 'they did not know what was going on there, nobody knew. They did queer things in the House of Lords, Lord Rosebery and Mr. Balfour had mid so. (Laughter.) They had killed many measures and they might try to do the same with the Budget. If they did there would be a fight to the finish, such as they had never seen before in this country, and he said frankly he should not be sorry. (Applause.) The position of the Lords had become well-nigh intolerable. In conclusion the speaker asked his hearers to examine the Budget for themselves and they would be con- vinced that it was a fair and equitable Bnciget. (Applause). Mr. -Lewis, in alluding to the land clauses of the Budget, said that a chairman of a certain Corporation llonsing Committee was looking out for some suitable land in order to build some respectable houses. One day be went to a sale where SA acres were offered. The auctioneer said be wanted -£4,500 for it—no sale. (Laughter.) The chairman saw the auctioneer and asked him if he could have the land as he was the chairman of the Housing Committee. As soon as the auctioneer heard this be said "We want £6,C00 for it." It was necessary that the Corporation should have the land, and so they had to ply £6,000. (" Shame." Was that fair? (Voices: "No.") Continuing, he said the Cle.neellor of the Exchequer had been called a thief, a robber, and lastly a Welshman—(laughter)--but he had never been called a "Welsher." (Laughter.) The dukes had said that if the Budget were passed they would be six weeks from the Workhouse. Where would the millionaires be? Why, three days from the Workhouse. (Laughter.) With regard to unemploy- ment he said they wanted to dispense with the casual labourer. He went down to Wales the other week and saw fields and fields of corn yet standing. He asked the farmer where all his men were. He said they had gone to town where there was better work and better wages. They thought that where there were more people there would be more employment and higher pay. After trying to gain employment they were unsuccessful and sank lower and lower and gradually lost all self-respect. To alleviate this the Budget proposed to place labour exchanges all over the country in order that men seek- ing work might be brought into touch with it as speedily as possible. Concluding the speaker alluded

The Examiner, Saturday, October 23, 1909.

BLACKPOOL CROWDS INSPECTING THE FLYING MACHINES.

seconded by Ccurcillor CUNNINGTON, a vote of thanks was accorded to the Chairman ard the ipeal4er. CONCLUDING MEETING ON WEDNESDAY.

The concluding meeting in connection with the cam- paign, which was held on Wednesday evening near ' Fairfield schools,was a very large and enthusiastic one. ' Mr. George Walker presided and was supported by I Councillor Kendal, Mr. Robert Lewis (Manchester) and Mr. J. Howes, jun. The CHAIRMAN said they were met there that night as a kind of educational meeting to discuss the Budget— cue of the most momentous Budgets and cne of the . most far reaching in its consequences of any be thought that had been previously brought before the House of . Commons. It was a•Budget they knew was popular— popular at least s% ith the masses. They could not afford to have set on one siee the social regeneration of the pecple by a party to very strcugly interested. as landlords and peers. (Applause). Councillor KENDAL saki that the Budget was popular because it revealed to them the fact that certain sections of the community had keen paying too much and certain sections too little—(bear, hear)—and because it was a fair and equitable attempt not to deal harshly with anybody, but to spread the taxes over the people, leaving no class exempt end none with too heavy a burden. He had read in the papers that day that the Budget Protest League had decided to put up the shutters. Their opponents bad keen trying to excite the country into huge protest about the Budget and they were met with the solemn fact that the game was up and the cat would not jump. (Laughter.) The reason the Budget Protest League was going to give up was that there was nothing to protest against the Budget. The masses of the people recognised some- thing in the Budget which ought to have been there years ago. The most unfair thing of the whole of the agitation was the misreprepresentation that the 'Work- ingman was clamouring against the. Budget proposals. The opposition to the Budget was very small when they came to think of it. All the landowners were not against it. The British Workingmen were facing the question like men. If they were to have "Dread- noughts "and battleships and old age pensions they must stand their corner and they were standing it. (Hear, bear). Whether the Budget would pass the House of Lords was a.question which was taxing their minds. Personally, be hoped they would not pass it, but he could not imagine that the House of Lords would be such "blithering fatheads "—he could not find any better phrase—as to throw it out. (Laughter). To putl a finger into the firegrate was a far more sensible thing than for the Lords to throw it out. If it were thrown out it would Le settled whether the people were to rule or 600 non-elected men of the country. (Applause). Mr. LEWIS said he had been talking on behalf of the Budget for about three months and they bad been about the happiest three month's of his life ; 'because it had been an interesting subject, and an interesting and enthusiastic audience. There was enthusiasm not because the Budget proposed robbery of enthusiasm, not because it took from and gave to; of enthusiasm, because the fount of life was not again to be poisoned and that industry was not to be hampered by the ground landlord. (Applause). In the future industry would be less hampered and the workers would have a larger opportunity. The vista that the Budget opened before the country had been a dream of some of them for many years and yet to-day it was to come about. (Applause.) He heard that a man in the Parr Hall on Monday night— (laughter)—who was a member of the gentlemanly party, say that Mr. Lloyd-George was in receipt of a salary of £5,000 a year, he was worth £5OO of it and that he was being overpaid to the extent of £4,500 a year. The country had called for Mr. Lloyd-George because he had the character and the ability for tie position. (Hear, hear.) If they had a simple clear test of the value of both men, they would gladly p,y the one £5,000 and the other one sd. If the British House of Commons con- sidered that Mr. Lloyd-George was unfit for his posi- tion he would not be in it. (Applause.) That sort of criticism was twopenny-halfpenny, clap-trap by min who bad been fortunate not to earn a living, but to acquire one. (Applause) Those were the kind of objec- tions that were being urged against the Budget. They would not meet them in solid argument, and they were not the people-whom they would pay heed to. (Hear, bear). _ . .

The CHAIRMAN proposed a vote of thtaks to the speakers, which was heartily accorded. A vote was then taken as to the opinion of the meet- ing with regard to the Budget. There was only one dissentient. Mr. HOWES announced that there would be a meet- ing at the Parr Ball 3n November 18, when Colonel Seeley would speak. (Hear, hear.) It would not be like the Tory meeting on Monday—a ticket one—where when they got in there was no resolution against the Budget. (Laughter and applause).

to the benefits of Free Trade and made special refer- ence to the Lancashire cotton industry. 97 per cent of the cotton goods exported from this country went from Lancashire, land the reason was that we could produce it so cheaply. That was because there was no burden upon raw material. Lancashire had the biggest market for cotton goods in the world and that was some- thing to any. (Applause.) The opposite party said that there was more unemployment in this country than there was in Germany, but that u as untrue. TUESDAY'S M BETIN G.

Tho Liberal Campaign was ccntinued on Tuesday evening at the corner of Longford-street and Orford lane. There was a large attendance. Councillor .John Wilkinson. J.P. presided and was supported by Coun- cillor T. Grindle J.P., (Eccles), and Councillor Cunningten. The CHAIRMAN said it was certainly gratifying to hint to see SO many of them rally round. It told him that a great wave of Liberal enthusiasm was finding its way over to that end of the town. The Budget had cemented and strengthened the Liberal and Labour forces into cne compact whole, so much so that to-day they were ready for an election whenever the time came. In fact they would welcome one if it came to-morrow. (Hear, hear.) Why was it that there was so much opposition to the Budget? One great opposi- tion came from the lands d preprtetors of the country. Our Chancellor of the Exchequer, Mr. Lloyd George —(applause)—had been so good as to include in the Budget a tax on what was called the unearned !cerement. He (the speaker) could assure them that if it had happened 30 years ago our present town would not have been carrying half the rates as it was to-day. They had read in the papers and no doubt heard many speeches on the other side of the great hardships that would be laid on the landowners. (Voice: 'We do not believe it.") For years the Warrington Corporation had been afraid to join with other municipalities to , send to Parliament a memorial that they should get some Bill passed so that land, which was let for build- ing purposes, should pay its share of tbe taxes. That was brought before the Council three times and eitch time it was commanded to lie on the table, although it was for the good of the municipality. One night in the Council he rose up apd proposed that the matter should be left open for discussion and they cried him down saying it was a Political question. (Laughter.) He answered that the day would come when they would want to discuss the question. (Applause.) The object of the Budget was to raise money to pay for Dreadnoughts that they had beard so much talk about—(Voice: "Who is crying for them ?")—and for old-age pensions. They said, ."Go to where the money was to raise it." They were not going to tax the food of the country. They were going to tax the land of the country. (Applause). Mr. Lloyd-George had tapped one of the fountain springs of revenue in the taxation of land values--a spring from which revenue would flow into the Exchequer, and which would find the ways and means of educating the com- munity, of lifting the community up, of giving better dwelling-houses for the artisans, and at the same time pay for old age pensions, thus bringing into the homes of many thousands of the hard-working aged poor, a little sunshine and comfort which they well deserved. (Loud applause.) Councillor GRINDLE at the outset of his speech replied to several attacks .made upon the Budget and Mr. Lloyd-George. The Chancellor of the Exchequer had been called a "Dick Turpin," who simply robbed the rich and gave to the poor. Well,. he (the speaker) would rather be a" Dick Turpin "who robbed the rich and gave to the poor, than one who robbed the poor and gave to the rich. There was a placard out representing a big. stalwart workingman and in the corner it said" Less beer," as though beer were the ' thing that was uppermost in the minds of the British workingman. It was not uppermost. (Hear, hear.) The British workingman thcught more of his family than his beer and his tobacco. It was a libel on the workingman and the Trade Unions of our country ought to protest against it. He (the speaker) went down to London as a representa- tive of Eccles Town Council about unearned increment and the other representatives were all in favour of its taxation. They were not composed mainly of Liberals, but of Conservatives.. Mr. Harmood Banner, Conserva- tive member for Liverpool was one of the foremost men in the agitation under the late Conservative Government for the taxation of land values, but when the present Government proposed it and -be saw some chance of it being done, the boot was on the other leg. The Lords had too low,' made the laws of the land. The speaker explained the various clauses of the Budget and his speech was punctuated with signs of assent.

The audience were asked if they were in favour of the Budget and there was a unanimous show of hands in the affirmative.

POOR LAW REFORM.

Lecture at the Adult School.

"Some account of the Poor Law Commission reports, 1909," was the subject of a lecture delivered at the Warrington Adult School on Thursday evening by Mr. J. C. Radley, of Manchester. There was a fair attendance, Mr. A. J. Jolley presiding. The CHAIRMAN said he was afraid there had not been as deep interest shown in the reports of the Commis- sion as might have been desired and it was as yet very uncertain whether we were likely to have legislation dealing with the problems that were partially dealt with in the reports, at an early date. The reports really were the outcome of a somewhat vague feeling of dissatisfaction with the existing administration and condition of the Poor Law which had been growing in recent years. Having traced the history of the Poor Law system Mr. Jolley. said that the evils that were generated partly by lax administration and partly. by. legislative defects were so serious at the beginning of the nineteenth century that they absolutely threatened the vitality of the nation. The 1834 Act, whatever might be its drawbacks, was a bold attempt to deal on sound scientific lines with the tremendous problem of poverty. It would be quite easy to offer a very strong and satisfactory .defence of the Poor Law and of its administration, so far as the more progressive parts of the country were concerned, but there had been a great lack of uniformity. Many unions had been sadly behind the times and the defects of are adminis- tration in those areas had more or less cast a slur on the administration of tho Poor Law all over the Country. There were, however, many unions in which the administration had been thoroughly good and sound and where honest attempts had been made to combine efficiency with economy, securing the kindest' possible treatment of the deserving aged poor, and the best treatment for the young. Mr. Radley said that.as the result of the Commis- sion we had at last got a text book to the social question. At the present time there were more paupers than there were twenty or thirty years ago by about half a million—a surprising and disappointing result in view of the fact that we spent £14,000,000 a year on the Poor Lair, and £20,000,000 on education, that wages seemed definitely proved to have risen in actual money value and still more in purchasing power, and that thrift amon* the people seemed to be on the increase. It might roughly be said that up to now the problem of preventing pauperism had hardly begun to be seriously dealt with. In the majority and minority reports of the Commission the average methods of the average Board of Guardians had come in for very drastic censure and, in the words of the minority report, the Poor Law as at pre- sent administered was as a system " intellectually bankrupt." In the opinion of both reports the Poor Law was at the end of its tether. The speaker then dealt exhaustively with the recommendation of both reports. Both suggested that Boards of Guardians should be superseded, but while the majority report suggested the formation of new authorities for t Poor Law work the minosity report recommended the utilization of existing County. Councils and public authorities, the various classes of poor to be dealt with by different committees. The scheme outlined in the minority report was a national one, and the appointment of a Minister for Labour was recommended The Chairman contended that a 16t of the evils were not remediable by legisldtion. The case was one against human nature quite as much as against the Poor Law.

The discussion was taken part in by Messrs. W. Nightingale, J. Morton, G. Walker and W. U. Sherburn, and a • vote of thanks was passed to the lecturer and the chairman.

SHEERNESS SAVED!

The attempts by torpedo boats to enter Sheerness Harbour unperceived during the night failed, the vessels being discovered by the troops mobilised on the land defences. The Territorials assisted the Regulars.
