"TENNYSON : HIS ART AND PERSONALITY.'7

The Rev. W.L. Schroeder, M.A., delivered the first' of a course of six University Extension lectures en " Tennyson : His art and personality" in the Assembly' Room, Conservative Club,, on Wednesday evening' ' Those who heard Mr. Schroeder's delightful lecture on Charles Lamb several years ago anticipate a treat in the present course and, if the first lecture mai be taken as a criterion, they will not be disappointen It was gratifying to see such a large attendance' Canon Stevenson, who presided, briefly introduced the lecturer. The Rev. W. L. SCIIROEDER said the supreme function of the poet was in the imaginative interpreta- tion of experience. It was his glorious office t° present in artistic guise the deeper meanings of eslffsf" ence. The real and the ideal with him were one. J'ae saw about the things of life the. glory of a higilet; world. Tennyson was indeed one of the masters his craft. The excellence of his metrical skill, tne unquestioned music of his verse yielded a haunting sweetness that entranced the ear and subdued the questioning mind. Tracing Tennyson's career, t,ne lecturer pointed out that from boyhood he had Oh! sense that he was a poet, and the feeling that he tans' cultivate his gift and se it worthily was an ever" abiding feature of his thought. Although some of 111 earliest attempts at verse contained crudities cn' thought and expression, they showed unmistakable genius for rhythm and verbal melody. When he 109 about seventeen years of age he and his .brothet,r Charles issued the "poems by two brothers" Tennyson was wont to refer to afterwards as" rot." Dealing with Tennyson's career at Cambridgef the lecturer said he achieved little in the Avg ° academic honours. The studies of the Universityr were to him uninteresting and so much matter of fact' The outstanding feature of this period of his life Noe his beautiful friendship with Arthur Henry Hallnra,i which was characterised by depth and tenderness an't spiritual strength. The death of Hallam was a gro blow to Tennyson aud he had enshrined his cora- panion's memory in the imperishable verse of 111 Memoriam." After a period of despondency an,,' grief he gave himself to hard and strenuous war' Perpetual idleness, he used to say, must be one of the' punishments of hell. Political and social probleras interested Tennyson and drew from him a pceti°f response. Physically as well as mentally he was ° commanding nature. He looked a poet and his looks did not belie him. From his whole being- there streamed a subtle fragrance rerninis- cent of high thought, poetic musing, strenuous aspiration and spiritual coihmunion. Other features of Tennyspn's life which the lecture' dwelt upon were his remarkable tran9e4ile seizures, his abiding friendship with the Brow-nit and Edward Fitzgerald, and his ideal home life. e acted up to his own favourite counsel " make the liwe of children as beautiful and as happy as possible., The lecturer referred to the late Queen Victoria:. admiration for Tennyson and mentioned that she to',, the poet that next to the Bible "In Memoriam was her comfort in her bereavement. Tenili- , son's intercourse with Gladstone was of the pleasantest kind, despite differences of politics - The lecturer referred to Tennyson's elevation to the peerage, to Carlyle's deep regard for him, to his love of outdoor life and sympathy with the lower creatioll, and to his closing days and death in 1899. In On' elusion he said it would be difficult to over-estimate the charm of the personality of such a man as Teanc son. Loveable, sensitive to' kindness,appreciative all his friends were ever ready to do, this great soul lived in and by love. It was our great thought that the work which made the closest appeal to 013-1. affections and sympathies' was just that which care out of the passionate devotion of his love to the memory of Arthur Henry Hallam. The other five lectures of the course will be delivered on the following dates : —October 20th and 27t4 November 3rd, 10th, 17th and 24th. _

MUNICIPAL ELECTION, NOVEMBER IsT, 1909 TO THE ELECTORS OF WHITECR OS WARD. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN, After seven and a half years' service as your repre- sensative on • the Town Council, my father has reluctantly decided, through ill-health, not ,to seek re-election. I have been asked by the Liberal Ward Committee and numerous ratepayers to of!er myself as a candidate in his stead at the forthcoming election, and 1 have acceded to the request. In asking for your support I can assure you of my desire to SERVE you to the BEST of MY ABILITY. For 22 yeard I have been a resident in the WARD, and claim to know its requirements. Progress has been made in the making of streets and back passages, but much remains to be done, and, if elected, I shall press forward this work. lam in favour of the ACQUISITION of the SITE in LOVELY LANE for the provision of an OPEN SPACE for RECREATION PURPOSES and SCHOOL. Trusting to receive your support on November Ist next, I remain, Yours faithfully,AMES T. BARTON. Sunnymead, Lovely Lane.

CODY'S AEROPLANE COMES TO GRIEF. "This has been an epoch-making week in the history of aviation. For the first time we have had flights on a big scale, and great things have been done both at Blackpool and Doncaster. There were over a hundred thousand spectators at Doncaster on Saturday, while it is reckoned that fully fifty thousand more were at Blackpool on the opening day, In addition to the brilliant flights the spectators had one or two thrills occasioned by the vagaries of some of the flying machines. Mr. S. H. Cody, for instance, had a miraculous escape at Doncaster. The front wheel of his aeroplane ploughed into the earth, and the huge structure swung over with the aviator underneath. Beyoni aefew scratches, however, Mr. Cody was unhurt. our illustration shows the wheel caught in the earth. • BUDGET BATTLE IN BERMONDSEY.
